The Left Adjoint of Derived Parabolic Induction
Claudius Heyer

Abstract
. We prove that the derived parabolic induction functor, defined on the unbounded derived category of smooth mod p p representations of a p p -adic reductive group, admits a left adjoint L ⁡ ( U , − ) \mathrm{L}(U,-) . We study the cohomology functors H i ∘ L ⁡ ( U , − ) \mathrm{H}^{i}\circ\mathrm{L}(U,-) in some detail and deduce that L ⁡ ( U , − ) \mathrm{L}(U,-) preserves bounded complexes and global admissibility in the sense of Schneider–Sorensen. Using L ⁡ ( U , − ) \mathrm{L}(U,-) we define a derived Satake homomorphism und prove that it encodes the mod p p Satake homomorphisms defined explicitly by Herzig.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
11F85, 18G80, 20G25

1.
Introduction

1.1.
The setting

This article is devoted to the investigation of the left adjoint of the derived
parabolic induction functor in natural characteristic. Let
G
be a
p
-adic
reductive group, that is, the group of
\mathfrak{F}
-points of a connected reductive
group defined over a finite field extension
\mathfrak{F}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.
Let
k
be a coefficient field of characteristic
p
and denote
\Rep_{k}(G)
the
category of smooth
G
-representations on
k
-vector spaces. Since several
important functors fail to be exact, like the functor of
K
-invariants for
compact open subgroups
K\subseteq G
, Schneider
[
Sch15
]

introduced the derived category

\mathrm{D}(G)\coloneqq\mathrm{D}(\Rep_{k}(G))

of unbounded cochain complexes to remedy this problem. The main result of
op. cit.
proves that, given a torsion-free open pro-
p
subgroup
K\subseteq G
, taking
derived
K
-invariants induces a derived
equivalence of
\mathrm{D}(G)
with the derived category
\mathrm{D}(\mathcal{H}_{K}^{\bullet})
of
dg-modules over a certain differential graded algebra
\mathcal{H}_{K}^{\bullet}
. This
strongly suggests that the study of smooth mod
p
representations is
best done on the derived level.

Let
P=UM
be a parabolic subgroup of
G
with Levi subgroup
M
and unipotent
radical
U
. The parabolic induction functor
i_{M}^{G}\colon\Rep_{k}(M)\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(G)
is defined as the composition

\Rep_{k}(M)\xrightarrow{\Inf^{M}_{P}}\Rep_{k}(P)\xrightarrow{\Ind_{P}^{G}}\Rep_{k}(G),

where
\Inf^{M}_{P}
is the inflation along the canonical projection

P\longrightarrow\hskip-11.99998pt\rightarrow\hskip 1.99997ptM
and
\Ind_{P}^{G}
is the smooth induction functor. The
functors
\Inf^{M}_{P}
and
\Ind_{P}^{G}
are exact. Hence also
i_{M}^{G}
is exact and
extends to a functor

\mathrm{R}i_{M}^{G}\colon\mathrm{D}(M)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(G)

on the derived categories: Here,
\mathrm{R}i_{M}^{G}
is given by applying
i_{M}^{G}

termwise to a complex. (The different notation serves the only purpose of being
able to distinguish between underived and derived parabolic induction.) The
compatibility of
\mathrm{R}i_{M}^{G}
with
an analogous functor on
\mathrm{D}(\mathcal{H}_{K}^{\bullet})
is explored in

[
SS22
]
, which also discusses the existence of left and
right adjoints of
\mathrm{R}i_{M}^{G}
, referring for the former to my unpublished notes.

At this point one might ask why one should care about a left adjoint. I propose
two answers: for one, in the classical theory of smooth representations, the
left adjoint is exact and plays an essential role in the classification of
irreducible smooth representations in terms of parabolic induction, namely, the
irreducible representations which do not appear as a subrepresentation of a
parabolically induced representation are precisely those in the kernel of the
corresponding left adjoint. This strongly indicates that the left adjoint for
derived parabolic induction will take a fundamental position in understanding
the category
\mathrm{D}(G)
as a whole. The second answer
is that the derived left adjoint will be an important tool to compute higher

\Ext
-groups between parabolically induced representations. There are already
some computations which use Emerton’s higher ordinary parts functor,

[
Eme10b
,
Hau16
,
Hau18
]
. But the strongest of these results
are conditional to an open conjecture of Emerton, which states that the higher
ordinary parts functor is a right derived functor. In the follow-up paper

[
Hey23
]
I use the derived left adjoint to unconditionally compute these

\Ext
-groups.

Let me briefly discuss why it is not immediate that
\mathrm{R}i_{M}^{G}
admits a left
adjoint. It is well-known that the Jacquet-functor
\Rep_{k}(G)\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(M)
,

V\longmapsto V_{U}
, is left adjoint to
i_{M}^{G}
. The left
derived functor of
({\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})_{U}
would be a promising candidate for the left
adjoint of derived parabolic induction. Unfortunately, the category
\Rep_{k}(G)

does not have enough projectives, since
\Char(k)=p
. (It is a folklore
conjecture that there exist no non-zero projectives at all; first evidence in
this direction is contained in
[
DK23
, Rmk. 2.20]
, which proves the statement for compact
G
; see also
[
CK23
, Thm. 3.1]
for the more general statement about profinite groups.)
Therefore, one cannot use projective resolutions to prove that the left derived
functor exists.
A second approach is to show that
\mathrm{R}i_{M}^{G}
commutes with arbitrary small
products and to then use a variant of Brown representability to deduce the
existence of a left adjoint. It is easy to show that
i_{M}^{G}
preserves
arbitrary products, but since products in
\mathrm{D}(M)
and
\mathrm{D}(G)
are not computed
by taking products of representing complexes, it is not obvious that
\mathrm{R}i_{M}^{G}

should have the same property.

In view of these concerns it was surprising that
\mathrm{R}i_{M}^{G}
does in fact
commute with small products. The initial proof was rather technical
and relied on an analysis of Hochschild–Serre spectral sequences associated
with
K_{P}\cap U\trianglelefteq K_{P}
, where
K_{P}
is a compact open subgroup of

P
, and analyzing how these behave with respect to restriction to smaller
subgroups. The more conceptual proof presented here is by appealing
to one of the main results of
[
BDS16
]
. The key ingredient is the
observation that the derived functor
\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(U\cap K_{P},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\colon\mathrm{D}(K_{P})\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(K_{P}/U\cap K_{P})
preserves compact objects provided
K_{P}
and
K_{P}/U\cap K_{P}

are torsion-free.

1.2.
Main results

Note that
\mathrm{R}i_{M}^{G}=\mathrm{R}\Ind_{P}^{G}\circ\mathrm{R}\Inf^{M}_{P}
so that it suffices to determine the left adjoints of

\mathrm{R}\Ind_{P}^{G}
and
\mathrm{R}\Inf^{M}_{P}
separately. The left adjoint of
\mathrm{R}\Ind_{P}^{G}
is
easily seen to be the restriction functor
\mathrm{R}\Res^{G}_{P}\colon\mathrm{D}(G)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(P)
,
and hence it remains to show that
\mathrm{R}\Inf^{M}_{P}
admits a left adjoint.

Granted the existence, the next task would then be to find an explicit
description of said left adjoint. In view of this it is convenient to study the
derived inflation
functor
\mathrm{R}\Inf^{T}_{S}\colon\mathrm{D}(T)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(S)
along a surjective open monoid
homomorphism
f\colon S\longrightarrow\hskip-11.99998pt\rightarrow\hskip 1.99997ptT
with kernel
U
, where
S
and

T
are open submonoids of some
p
-adic Lie groups.
The most relevant example is the following: Fix a compact
open subgroup
K_{U}
of
U
and consider the positive monoid
T=M^{+}

consisting of those elements
m\in M
with
mK_{U}m^{-1}\subseteq K_{U}
as well as
the open submonoid
S=K_{U}M^{+}
of
P
(in which case the kernel of
S\longrightarrow T
is

K_{U}
). This added flexibility is useful to
deduce precise statements about the left adjoint of
\mathrm{R}\Inf^{M}_{P}
.

In this general setting, the first main result is:

Theorem A

(Theorem
3.2.3
)
.

The functor
\mathrm{R}\Inf^{T}_{S}\colon\mathrm{D}(T)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(S)
admits a left adjoint
\mathrm{L}_{U}
. In
particular,
\mathrm{R}i_{M}^{G}
admits a left adjoint
\mathrm{L}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
.

We deduce the theorem from a variant of Brown representability by proving that

\mathrm{R}\Inf^{T}_{S}
commutes with small products. Observing that restriction to
compact open subgroups is conservative and product preserving, we reduce to the
case where
S
and
T
are torsion-free compact
p
-adic Lie groups. We then
verify that the categories
\mathrm{D}(T)
and
\mathrm{D}(S)
are rigidly-compactly generated
(in the sense of
[
BDS16
, 1.2. Hyp.]
) and that the right adjoint

\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
of
\mathrm{R}\Inf^{T}_{S}
preserves compact objects. With these
hypotheses in place, the theorem follows from
[
BDS16
, 3.3. Thm.]
. In
fact, under these restrictions on
S
and
T
, the shifted invariants functor

\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})[\dim U]
is a left adjoint of
\mathrm{R}\Inf^{T}_{S}
.

As a special case of Theorem
A
we obtain that
\mathrm{R}\Inf^{M}_{P}
admits a
left adjoint, which we denote by
\mathrm{L}_{U}
.
The next task then is to try to explicitly compute
\mathrm{L}_{U}
, or at least its
cohomology functors
\mathrm{L}^{i}_{U}
. A formal argument shows
\mathrm{L}_{U}^{i}\cong 0
, for

i>0
,
and that
\mathrm{L}_{U}^{0}
is the usual functor of
U
-coinvariants (
i.e.
, the left
adjoint of
\Inf^{M}_{P}
). In order to analyze
\mathrm{L}_{U}^{i}
for
i<0
, we employ

p
-adic monoids. To motivate the idea, we recall a different description of the
(underived) coinvariants functor
\mathrm{L}_{U}^{0}
. The unipotent radical
U
is the
union of compact open subgroups, say
U=\bigcup_{r\in\Lambda}H_{r}
. It is
a well-known and easy fact that
\mathrm{L}_{U}^{0}\cong\colim_{r\in\Lambda}\mathrm{L}_{H_{r}}^{0}
. Although this isomorphism describes
\mathrm{L}_{U}^{0}
in terms of
functors of coinvariants with respect to compact open subgroups (which should be
easier to understand), it is in this formulation difficult to extend to the
derived setting. This is where the positive monoid enters the stage: Fixing a
torsion-free compact open subgroup
K_{U}
of
U
, the positive monoid
M^{+}
of
elements
m\in M
with
mK_{U}m^{-1}\subseteq K_{U}
acts naturally on

\mathrm{L}_{K_{U}}^{0}(V)
, for
V\in\Rep_{k}(P)
. It is well-known that there exists a
central positive element
z\in M
such that
U=\bigcup_{i\geq 0}z^{-i}K_{U}z^{i}
.
There is a natural isomorphism
\colim_{i}\mathrm{L}_{z^{-i}K_{U}z^{i}}^{0}(V)\cong\colim_{z}\mathrm{L}_{K_{U}}^{0}(V)
, where the transition maps in the latter colimit are
given by multiplication with
z
.
In fact,
\colim_{z}
is the (exact) functor
\Rep_{k}(M^{+})\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(M)
given by
inverting the action of
z
.
It is left adjoint to the restriction functor
\Res^{M}_{M^{+}}
and also denoted

\ind_{M^{+}}^{M}
. Thus, there is a natural isomorphism

\mathrm{L}_{U}^{0}(V)\cong\ind_{M^{+}}^{M}\mathrm{L}_{K_{U}}^{0}(V)
, which can easily be extended to
the derived categories. Concretely, writing
P^{+}\coloneqq K_{U}M^{+}
(it is an open
submonoid in
P
), the derived inflation
\mathrm{R}\Inf^{M^{+}}_{P^{+}}
admits a left
adjoint
\mathrm{L}_{K_{U}}\colon\mathrm{D}(P^{+})\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(M^{+})
, and we find a natural isomorphism

\mathrm{R}\ind_{M^{+}}^{M}\mathrm{L}_{K_{U}}\mathrm{R}\Res^{P}_{P^{+}}\xRightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{L}_{U}

of functors
\mathrm{D}(P)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(M)
(Proposition
3.4.6
). The group

K_{U}
is a compact, torsion-free
p
-adic Lie group and hence has finite
cohomological dimension by a result of Lazard and Serre
(see
[
Ser65
, Cor. (1)]
).
We deduce from this fact in Corollary
3.4.21
that
\mathrm{L}_{K_{U}}
is
naturally isomorphic to
\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},\omega_{P^{+}}\otimes_{k}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
, where

\omega_{P^{+}}
is a certain smooth character of
P^{+}
sitting in cohomological
degree
-\dim U
, and
\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
is the right derived functor of

\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\colon\Rep_{k}(P^{+})\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(M^{+})
where the
M^{+}
-action on each

\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},V)
is the “Hecke action” from
[
Eme10a
]
.
To summarize:

Theorem B

(Corollary
3.4.23
)
.

One has an isomorphism
\ind_{M^{+}}^{M}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},\omega_{P^{+}}\otimes_{k}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\Res^{P}_{P^{+}}\cong\mathrm{L}_{U}
of functors
\Rep_{k}(P)\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(M)
.

An immediate consequence of Theorem
B
is that
\mathrm{L}_{U}
restricts
to the bounded derived categories.

Similar results trivially also follow for the left adjoint
\mathrm{L}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\coloneqq\mathrm{L}_{U}\circ\mathrm{R}\Res^{G}_{P}
of derived parabolic induction
\mathrm{R}i_{M}^{G}
. It is
well-known that
i_{M}^{G}
and the Jacquet-functor
\mathrm{L}^{0}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})

preserve admissible representations, and it is a natural question whether the
same still holds on the derived level. The correct notion of admissibility for

\mathrm{D}(G)
was introduced by Schneider–Sorensen in
[
SS23
]
:
A complex
X
in
\mathrm{D}(G)
is called
globally admissible
if for some
torsion-free compact open pro-
p
subgroup
K\subseteq G
and all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
the
cohomology groups
\mathrm{H}^{i}(K,X)
are finite-dimensional
k
-vector spaces. They
then proceed to show that globally admissible complexes are precisely the
reflexive objects for a natural duality functor on
\mathrm{D}(G)
. In this direction,
we prove:

Theorem C

(Theorem
4.2.1
)
.

The functors
\mathrm{R}i_{M}^{G}
and
\mathrm{L}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
preserve globally admissible complexes.

We finally compute
\mathrm{L}^{i}(U,V)
for the irreducible smooth

\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}[\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})]
-representations
V
and all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
U
consists
of the upper triangular unipotent matrices. For
i\in\{-1,0\}
the
representations
\mathrm{L}^{i}(U,V)
are listed in Table
1
on
page
1
, while for
i\notin\{-1,0\}
these
representations are zero.

The proof uses a derived variant of the Satake homomorphism, which I will now
describe. Let
K
be a compact open subgroup of
G
satisfying
G=PK
. Given

V\in\mathrm{D}(K)
, there is a natural isomorphism

\mathrm{L}(U,\ind_{K}^{G}V)\cong\ind_{K\cap M}^{M}\mathrm{L}(K\cap U,V)
, where we write

\mathrm{L}(K\cap U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\coloneqq\mathrm{L}_{K\cap U}\mathrm{R}\Res^{K}_{K\cap P}
. Hence, the functor

\mathrm{L}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
induces a
k
-algebra homomorphism

\mathcal{S}_{V}\colon\End_{\mathrm{D}(G)}(\ind_{K}^{G}V)\longrightarrow\End_{\mathrm{D}(M)}\bigl(\ind_{K\cap M}^{M}\mathrm{L}(K\cap U,V)\bigr)

which we call the derived Satake homomorphism.
1
1

1

In fact,
\mathcal{S}_{V}
is
the
0
-th cohomology of the morphism induced by
\mathrm{L}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
on

\RHom
-complexes, but we will not make use of this generality.

In a different context, a version
of the derived Satake homomorphism was defined and studied by Ronchetti,

[
Ron19
]
. The relation between these homomorphisms is still
unclear: The fact that
\mathrm{L}(K\cap U,V)
is a proper complex (even when
V=\mathbf{1}
is the trivial representation) makes a comparison difficult. However, it
is possible to relate
\mathcal{S}_{V}
with the (underived) mod
p
Satake
homomorphisms introduced by Herzig,
[
Her11b
, Thm. 2.6 and §2.3]
.
Composing
\mathcal{S}_{V}
with the
(-n)
-th cohomology functor
\mathrm{H}^{-n}
yields a

k
-algebra homomorphism

\mathcal{S}_{V}^{n}\colon\End_{\Rep_{k}(G)}(\ind_{K}^{G}V)\longrightarrow\End_{\Rep_{k}(M)}\bigl(\ind_{K\cap M}^{M}\mathrm{L}^{-n}(K\cap U,V)\bigr),

and we prove:

Theorem D

(Theorem
4.3.2
)
.

The mod
p
Satake homomorphisms constructed in
[
Her11b
]
coincide
with
\mathcal{S}_{V}^{0}
and
\mathcal{S}_{V}^{\dim U}
.

In particular, Theorem
D
shows that the mod
p
Satake homomorphisms
appearing in the literature are induced by explicit functors. For

\mathcal{S}_{V}^{0}
this is
essentially contained in
[
HV12
, Section 2]
, where
Henniart–Vignéras give a functorial description without, however, explicitly
mentioning the functor inducing
\mathcal{S}_{V}^{0}
. It was also known that

\mathcal{S}_{V}^{\dim U}
and
\mathcal{S}_{V}^{0}
are related by taking duals, see

[
Her11b
, Lem. 2.11]
or
[
HV12
, Prop. 2.4]
.

In the forthcoming work
[
Hey23
]
I use
\mathrm{L}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
to prove a geometrical lemma
à la
Bernstein–Zelevinsky and apply it to the
computation of some
\Ext
-groups of parabolically induced representations.

1.3.
Structure of the paper

In §
2
we recall several fundamental concepts starting with a
recap of adjoint functors and the calculus of mates in §
2.1
.
In §
2.2
we introduce
p
-adic monoids and give a quick overview
of their smooth representation theory. The subject of §
2.3
is
the derived category
\mathrm{D}(S)
of smooth representations of a
p
-adic monoid
S
.
We recall Brown representability, the tensor triangulated structure on

\mathrm{D}(S)
, and how to obtain spectral sequences using truncation
functors.

In §
3
we prove that the derived inflation functor

\mathrm{R}\Inf^{T}_{S}
admits a left adjoint. We first prove this in the case where
T

and
S
are torsion-free compact
p
-adic Lie groups, see
§
3.1
. The general case is then deduced from the compact case
in §
3.2
. In §
3.3
and §
3.4

we specialize to
p
-adic Lie groups and show that
\mathrm{L}_{U}
satisfies a
projection formula and compute more explicit descriptions of the

\mathrm{L}_{U}^{i}
.

In §
4
we investigate the left adjoint of derived
parabolic induction. In §
4.2
we show that
\mathrm{R}i_{M}^{G}
and

\mathrm{L}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
preserve global admissibility, and §
4.3
is concerned
with the derived Satake homomorphism.

The explicit computations of
\mathrm{L}^{i}(U,V)
, for irreducible smooth

\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}[\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})]
-representations
V
, are the subject
of §
5
.

1.5.
Notation and conventions

Throughout this article,
k
will denote a coefficient field, which will be
assumed to be of characteristic
p>0
from §
3
onwards. The
category of
k
-vector spaces is denoted
\mathrm{Vect}_{k}
, and we write
\mathrm{D}(k)
for the
unbounded derived category of
\mathrm{Vect}_{k}
.

If
S
is a locally profinite monoid, we denote
\Rep_{k}(S)
the abelian category
of smooth
S
-representations on
k
-vector spaces and by
\mathrm{D}(S)
the
unbounded
derived category of
\Rep_{k}(S)
. Given
V\in\Rep_{k}(S)
, we denote by
V[0]
(or
just
V
if no confusion is likely) the representation
V
viewed as a complex
concentrated in degree
0
.

We make the convention that a diagram of functors, say,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{B}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

commutes if there exists a natural isomorphism
b\circ f\xRightarrow{\cong}f^{\prime}\circ a
, which will usually be specified in the proof if it
is unclear which natural isomorphism is meant.

If
G
is a group,
K\subseteq G
is a subgroup and
g\in G
, we write
{}^{g}K\coloneqq gKg^{-1}
and
K^{g}\coloneqq g^{-1}Kg
.

2.
Foundations

This section is devoted to collecting facts about
adjoint functors (§
2.1
), smooth representations of locally
profinite monoids (§
2.2
), and the derived category of smooth
representations (§
2.3
).

2.1.
About adjoint functors

In this section we recall well-known facts about adjoint functors. For
references and more details we refer to
[
KS06
]
and
[
ML13
]
.

Let
\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D}
be categories. We fix two functors
L\colon\mathscr{C}\longrightarrow\mathscr{D}
and
R\colon\mathscr{D}\longrightarrow\mathscr{C}
.

Definition
.

We say
L
is
left adjoint
to
R
(or
R
is
right adjoint
to
L
),
written
L\dashv R
, if there is a bijection

(2.1.1)

\Hom_{\mathscr{D}}\bigl(L(C),D\bigr)\xrightarrow{\cong}\Hom_{\mathscr{C}}\bigl(C,R(D)\bigr)

which is natural in both
C\in\mathscr{C}
and
D\in\mathscr{D}
.

Remark 2.1.2
.

If
L\colon\mathscr{C}\longrightarrow\mathscr{D}
is an additive functor between additive
categories, then a right adjoint
R
(if it exists) is additive as well and the
map (
2.1.1
) is an isomorphism of abelian groups, see
[
ML13
, IV.1,
Thm. 3]
.

It follows immediately from the definition that one can compose adjunctions:

Lemma 2.1.3

(
[
KS06
, Prop. 1.5.5]
)
.

Consider the functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{E}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{\prime}}

Assume that
L
and
L^{\prime}
are left adjoint to
R
and
R^{\prime}
, respectively. Then

L^{\prime}\circ L
is left adjoint to
R\circ R^{\prime}
.

Lemma 2.1.4
.

The following assertions are equivalent:

(a)

L
is left adjoint to
R
.

(b)

There exist natural transformations
\eta\colon\id_{\mathscr{C}}\Longrightarrow RL
and
\varepsilon\colon LR\Longrightarrow\id_{\mathscr{D}}
, called the

unit
and the
counit
of the adjunction, respectively, satisfying
the “triangle identities”
R\varepsilon\circ\eta R=\id_{R}
and
\varepsilon L\circ L\eta=\id_{L}
.

Proof.

For the details we refer to
[
ML13
, IV.1]
.
If
L
is left adjoint to
R
, the map
\eta_{C}
is given by the image
of
\id_{L(C)}
under (
2.1.1
) and
\varepsilon_{D}
is given by the
preimage of
\id_{R(D)}
under (
2.1.1
). It is then easily checked
that
\eta
and
\varepsilon
are natural transformations and satisfy the
triangle identities.

Conversely, if
\eta
and
\varepsilon
are as in
(b)
one
checks that the compositions

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathscr{D}}\bigl(L(C),D\bigr)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{R}\Hom_{\mathscr{C}}\bigl(RL(C),R(D)\bigr)\xrightarrow{(\eta_{C})^{*}}\Hom_{\mathscr{C}}\bigl(C,R(D)\bigr)

and

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathscr{C}}\bigl(C,R(D)\bigr)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{L}\Hom_{\mathscr{D}}\bigl(L(C),LR(D)\bigr)\xrightarrow{(\varepsilon_{D})_{*}}\Hom_{\mathscr{D}}\bigl(L(C),D\bigr)

are inverse to each other.
∎

Lemma 2.1.5

(
[
KS06
, Prop. 1.5.6]
)
.

Assume that
L
is left adjoint to
R
.

(a)

The functor
R
is fully faithful if and only if the counit
\varepsilon\colon LR\Longrightarrow\id_{\mathscr{D}}
is an isomorphism.

(b)

The functor
L
is fully faithful if and only if the unit
\eta\colon\id_{\mathscr{C}}\Longrightarrow RL
is an isomorphism.

The following result shows that adjoints are uniquely determined.

Theorem 2.1.6

(
[
KS06
, Thm. 1.5.3]
)
.

The right adjoint of
L
(if it exists) is unique up to unique isomorphism.
Moreover,
L
admits a right adjoint if and only if the functor
\Hom_{\mathscr{D}}\bigl(L({\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}),D\bigr)\colon\mathscr{D}\longrightarrow\mathrm{(Sets)}
is representable for every
D\in\mathscr{D}
.

Dually, the left adjoint of
R
(if it exists) is unique up to unique
isomorphism. Moreover,
R
admits a left adjoint if and only if the functor

\Hom_{\mathscr{C}}\bigl(C,R({\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\bigr)\colon\mathscr{C}\longrightarrow\mathrm{(Sets)}
is representable for
every
C\in\mathscr{C}
.

Proposition 2.1.7

(
[
KS06
, Prop. 2.1.10]
)
.

If
L\colon\mathscr{C}\longrightarrow\mathscr{D}
admits a right adjoint, then
L
commutes with all
colimits that exist in
\mathscr{C}
.

Dually, if
R\colon\mathscr{D}\longrightarrow\mathscr{C}
admits a left adjoint, then
R
commutes
with all limits that exist in
\mathscr{D}
.

For a stronger (and dual) version of the next extremely useful result, see

[
HS71
, II, Prop. 10.2]
.

Lemma 2.1.8
.

Assume that
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
are abelian categories and
R\colon\mathscr{D}\longrightarrow\mathscr{C}
is an additive functor admitting an exact left adjoint. Then
R

preserves injective objects.

Proof.

Let
I\in\mathscr{D}
be an injective object. The functor
\Hom_{\mathscr{C}}\bigl({\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}},R(I)\bigr)\cong\Hom_{\mathscr{D}}\bigl(L({\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}),I\bigr)
is exact,
which means that
R(I)\in\mathscr{C}
is injective.
∎

Finally, we discuss the formalism of “mates” of natural transformations
involving adjoint functors. As usual, given two functors
F,G\colon\mathscr{C}\longrightarrow\mathscr{D}
, we denote by
\Nat(F,G)
the class of natural transformations from
F
to

G
.

Proposition 2.1.9

(
[
KS74
, Prop. 2.1]
)
.

Consider a diagram of functors

(2.1.10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\dashv
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{\prime}}
\dashv

and denote
\eta\colon\id_{\mathscr{C}}\Longrightarrow RL
,
\varepsilon\colon LR\Longrightarrow\id_{\mathscr{D}}

and
\eta^{\prime}\colon\id_{\mathscr{C}^{\prime}}\Longrightarrow R^{\prime}L^{\prime}
,
\varepsilon^{\prime}\colon L^{\prime}R^{\prime}\Longrightarrow\id_{\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}

the units and counits. There is a natural bijection

(2.1.11)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nat(L^{\prime}F,GL)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nat(FR,R^{\prime}G),}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(R^{\prime}G\varepsilon)\circ(R^{\prime}\alpha_{R})\circ(\eta^{\prime}_{FR}),}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varepsilon^{\prime}_{GL})\circ(L^{\prime}\beta_{L})\circ(L^{\prime}F\eta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

If
\alpha\colon L^{\prime}F\Longrightarrow GL
corresponds to
\beta\colon FR\Longrightarrow R^{\prime}G
under
(
2.1.11
), we say that
\alpha
is the left mate of
\beta
and

\beta
is the right mate of
\alpha
.

The “naturality” above means that the bijection (
2.1.11
) is
compatible with horizontal and vertical composition of squares of the
form (
2.1.10
), see
[
KS74
, Prop. 2.2]
for a precise
statement.

Example 2.1.12
.

Let us make explicit two special cases of vertical composition. Suppose we are
in the setting of Proposition
2.1.9
.

(i)

Let
L_{1}\colon\mathscr{C}\longrightarrow\mathscr{D}
be a functor with right adjoint
R_{1}
, and
let
\alpha\colon L\Longrightarrow L_{1}
be a natural transformation with right mate

\beta\colon R_{1}\Longrightarrow R
. Then the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nat(L^{\prime}F,GL)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nat(FR,R^{\prime}G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nat(L^{\prime}F,GL_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nat(FR_{1},R^{\prime}G)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G\alpha)_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F\beta)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

commutes.

(ii)

Let
L^{\prime}_{1}\colon\mathscr{C}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathscr{D}^{\prime}
be a functor with right adjoint

R^{\prime}_{1}
, and let
\alpha^{\prime}\colon L^{\prime}_{1}\Longrightarrow L^{\prime}
be a natural transformation with
right mate
\beta^{\prime}\colon R^{\prime}\Longrightarrow R^{\prime}_{1}
. Then the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nat(L^{\prime}F,GL)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nat(FR,R^{\prime}G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nat(L^{\prime}_{1}F,GL)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nat(FR,R^{\prime}_{1}G)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha^{\prime}_{F})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\beta^{\prime}_{G})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

commutes.

It is not true in general that (
2.1.11
) preserves
isomorphisms. However, in very special cases this does hold.

Example 2.1.13
.

Let
L_{i}\colon\mathscr{C}\longrightarrow\mathscr{D}
be a functor admitting a right adjoint
R_{i}
,
for
i=1,2,3
. Let
\alpha\colon L_{3}\Longrightarrow L_{2}
be a natural transformation with
mate
\beta\colon R_{2}\Longrightarrow R_{3}
. It follows from Example
2.1.12

that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nat(L_{2},L_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nat(R_{1},R_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nat(L_{3},L_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nat(R_{1},R_{3})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

commutes.
Hence, if
\alpha^{\prime}\colon L_{2}\Longrightarrow L_{1}
has mate
\beta^{\prime}\colon R_{1}\Longrightarrow R_{2}
, then
the mate of
\alpha^{\prime}\circ\alpha
is
\beta\circ\beta^{\prime}
. Note also that under

\Nat(L_{3},L_{3})\xleftrightarrow{\hphantom{i}\cong\hphantom{i}}\Nat(R_{3},R_{3})

the mate of
\id_{L_{3}}
is
\id_{R_{3}}
; this is a reformulation of the fact
that the unit and counit of the adjunction
L_{3}\dashv R_{3}
satisfy the triangle
identities.

It follows from this discussion that
\alpha\colon L_{3}\Longrightarrow L_{2}
is an isomorphism
if and only if its mate
\beta\colon R_{2}\Longrightarrow R_{3}
is an isomorphism. This is a
strengthening of the uniqueness statement in Theorem
2.1.6
.

2.2.
Smooth representations

For our purposes it is useful to consider smooth representations over locally
profinite monoids. Everything in this section is well-known for locally
profinite groups, see,
e.g.
,
[
BH06
]
or

[
Vig96
]
, and so we will restrain ourselves to a very brief
treatment.
We fix a coefficient field
k
.

2.2.1.
General properties of smooth representations

Definition
.

A
locally profinite monoid
is an open submonoid of a locally profinite group.
A
p
-adic monoid
is an open submonoid of a
p
-adic Lie group.
A morphism between locally profinite monoids (resp.
p
-adic monoids) is a continuous
monoid homomorphism.

Example 2.2.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{F}
be a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and consider a connected
reductive group
\mathbf{G}
defined over
\mathfrak{F}
. Then every closed subgroup of

\mathbf{G}(\mathfrak{F})
is a
p
-adic Lie group
[
DDMS99
, 9.6 Thm.]
. Let
\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{M}\mathbf{U}
be a parabolic subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
. Fix a compact open subgroup

K_{P}\subseteq\mathbf{P}(\mathfrak{F})
such that
K_{P}=K_{M}K_{U}
, where
K_{M}\coloneqq K_{P}\cap\mathbf{M}(\mathfrak{F})
and
K_{U}\coloneqq K_{P}\cap\mathbf{U}(\mathfrak{F})
. Then

M^{+}\coloneqq\left\{m\in\mathbf{M}(\mathfrak{F})\,\middle|\,mK_{U}m^{-1}\subseteq K_{U}\right\}

contains
K_{M}
and hence is a
p
-adic monoid.

The topological monoid
P^{+}\coloneqq K_{U}M^{+}
contains
K
and is therefore a

p
-adic monoid.
2
2

2

Observe, however, that
M^{+}K_{U}
is
not
a
monoid.
Note that there is a surjective monoid homomorphism

P^{+}\longrightarrow\hskip-11.99998pt\rightarrow\hskip 1.99997ptM^{+}
with kernel
K_{U}
.

Lemma 2.2.2
.

Let
S
be a locally profinite monoid and
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
a submonoid. Then

S^{\prime}
is open if and only if
S^{\prime}
contains an open subgroup of
S
.

Proof.

One direction follows from the fact that the neutral element in a
p
-adic Lie group admits a fundamental system of open neighborhoods which are profinite subgroups. The other direction is clear.
∎

Definition
.

Let
S
be a locally profinite monoid. Let
V
be a representation of
S
on a

k
-vector space,
i.e.
, a (left) module over the monoid algebra
k[S]
.

(i)

A vector
v\in V
is called
smooth
if the stabilizer
\left\{s\in S\,\middle|\,sv=v\right\}
of
v
is open in
S
. We denote by
V^{\mathrm{sm}}\subseteq V
the subset of
smooth vectors in
V
.

(ii)

V
is called
smooth
if
V=V^{\mathrm{sm}}
.

We denote by
\Rep_{k}(S)
the full subcategory of the category
\Mod(k[S])
of

k[S]
-modules consisting of smooth representations of
S
.

Lemma 2.2.3
.

Let
S
be a locally profinite monoid and let
V
be a
k[S]
-module.

(a)

Let
v\in V
. The following assertions are
equivalent:

(i)

The vector
v\in V
is smooth.

(ii)

There exists an open subgroup
S_{0}\subseteq S

fixing
v
.

(b)

V^{\mathrm{sm}}
is a smooth
S
-subrepresentation of
V
.

Proof.

The equivalence in
(a)
follows immediately from
Lemma
2.2.2
. We now prove
(b)
. Let
v,w\in V^{\mathrm{sm}}

and
s\in S
. It suffices to show
v+w\in V^{\mathrm{sm}}
and
sv\in V^{\mathrm{sm}}
.
By
(a)
there exist open subgroups
S_{v}
and
S_{w}
fixing
v
and

w
, respectively. Then
S_{v}\cap S_{w}
fixes
v+w
, and then
(a)

implies
v+w\in V^{\mathrm{sm}}
. Since multiplication in
S
is continuous and
sS_{v}
is
open in
S
, there exists an open subgroup
S^{\prime}_{v}\subseteq S
such that
S^{\prime}_{v}s\subseteq sS_{v}
. But then
S^{\prime}_{v}
fixes
sv
which, by
(a)
again,
shows
sv\in V^{\mathrm{sm}}
.
∎

We list some immediate consequences of Lemma
2.2.3
.

Corollary 2.2.4
.

Let
S
be a locally profinite monoid.

(a)

A
k[S]
-module
V
is smooth if and only if
V
is smooth when
considered as a
k[S_{0}]
-module for some open profinite subgroup
S_{0}\subseteq S
.

(b)

\Rep_{k}(S)
is an abelian subcategory of
\Mod(k[S])
.

Let
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
be a closed submonoid of a locally profinite monoid
S
.
The restriction functor

\Res^{S}_{S^{\prime}}\colon\Rep_{k}(S)\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(S^{\prime})

is exact. It admits a right adjoint which we will now describe. Given a smooth

k[S^{\prime}]
-module
V
, we obtain a smooth
k[S]
-module

\Ind_{S^{\prime}}^{S}V\coloneqq\Hom_{k[S^{\prime}]}\bigl(k[S],V\bigr)^{\mathrm{sm}},

called the
smooth induction
of
V
.
Here,
S
acts on
\Ind_{S^{\prime}}^{S}V
via right multiplication on
k[S]
. One easily
verifies that

\Ind_{S^{\prime}}^{S}V\cong\left\{f\colon S\longrightarrow V\,\middle|\,\begin{array}[]{l}\text{$f(s^{\prime}s)=s^{\prime}f(s)$
for all $s\in S$, $s^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}$,}\\
\text{$\exists K\subseteq S$ open with $f(sx)=f(s)$ for all $s\in S$, $x\in K$}\end{array}\right\},

where
S
acts on
f\colon S\longrightarrow V
via
(sf)(t)=f(ts)
, for all
s,t\in S
. We
will often implicitly make this identification.

Lemma 2.2.5

(Frobenius reciprocity I)
.

The smooth induction
\Ind_{S^{\prime}}^{S}\colon\Rep_{k}(S^{\prime})\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(S)
is a right
adjoint of
\Res^{S}_{S^{\prime}}
, that is, there is a natural bijection

\Hom_{\Rep_{k}(S^{\prime})}\bigl(\Res^{S}_{S^{\prime}}V,W\bigr)\cong\Hom_{\Rep_{k}(S)}\bigl(V,\Ind_{S^{\prime}}^{S}W\bigr)

for all
V\in\Rep_{k}(S)
,
W\in\Rep_{k}(S^{\prime})
. In particular,
\Ind_{S^{\prime}}^{S}
is
left exact and preserves injective objects.

Proof.

The last assertion is a formal consequence of the fact that
\Ind_{S^{\prime}}^{S}
admits
an exact left adjoint, see Lemma
2.1.8
.
∎

Remark 2.2.6
.

If
S
and
S^{\prime}
are profinite groups, the projection
S\longrightarrow S/S^{\prime}
admits a
continuous section by
[
Ser13
, Ch. I, Prop. 1]
. Hence,

\Ind_{S^{\prime}}^{S}\colon\Rep_{k}(S^{\prime})\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(S)
is exact by
[
Her12
, Prop. 5]
.

Corollary 2.2.7
.

The category
\Rep_{k}(S)
has enough injectives.

Proof.

Let
V\in\Rep_{k}(S)
. The map
\varphi\colon V\longrightarrow\Ind_{1}^{S}\Res^{S}_{1}V
,

\varphi(v)(x)\coloneqq xv
, is clearly injective. Since
\Res^{S}_{1}V
is
injective in the category of
k
-vector spaces, it follows from
Lemma
2.2.5
that
\Ind_{1}^{S}\Res^{S}_{1}V
is injective in

\Rep_{k}(S)
.
∎

If
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
is open, then
\Res^{S}_{S^{\prime}}
also admits a left
adjoint. For a smooth
k[S^{\prime}]
-module
V
we define

\ind_{S^{\prime}}^{S}V\coloneqq k[S]\otimes_{k[S^{\prime}]}V,

called the
compact induction
of
V
. The smoothness of
\ind_{S^{\prime}}^{S}V

follows from Lemma
2.2.3
.
(b)
and the fact that

\ind_{S^{\prime}}^{S}V
is generated as a
k[S]
-module by
V
, which consists of smooth
vectors.

Remark 2.2.8
.

Suppose that
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
is an open subgroup. Given
s\in S
and
v\in V
, we
define
[s,v]\in\Ind_{S^{\prime}}^{S}V
by

[s,v](t)\coloneqq\begin{cases}ts\cdot v,&\text{if $ts\in S^{\prime}$,}\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

In other words,
[s,v]
is the
S^{\prime}
-equivariant function
S\longrightarrow V
supported on

S^{\prime}s^{-1}
with
value
v
at
s^{-1}
.
In this case we have a canonical injective
S
-equivariant map

\displaystyle\ind_{S^{\prime}}^{S}V

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\Ind_{S^{\prime}}^{S}V,

\displaystyle s\otimes v

\displaystyle\longmapsto[s,v].

It is customary to identify
\ind_{S^{\prime}}^{S}V
with its image in

\Ind_{S^{\prime}}^{S}V
.

Lemma 2.2.9

(Frobenius reciprocity II)
.

Assume that
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
is an open submonoid. Then compact induction

\ind_{S^{\prime}}^{S}\colon\Rep_{k}(S^{\prime})\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(S)
is left adjoint to

\Res^{S}_{S^{\prime}}
, that is, there is a natural bijection

\Hom_{\Rep_{k}(S)}\bigl(\ind_{S^{\prime}}^{S}V,W\bigr)\cong\Hom_{\Rep_{k}(S^{\prime})}\bigl(V,\Res^{S}_{S^{\prime}}W\bigr),

for all
V\in\Rep_{k}(S^{\prime})
,
W\in\Rep_{k}(S)
. In particular,
\ind_{S^{\prime}}^{S}

is right exact.

Proof.

This follows from standard properties of the tensor product.
∎

Remark
.

I do not know whether the extension
k[S^{\prime}]\subseteq k[S]
is flat in general. But it is flat (and hence
\ind_{S^{\prime}}^{S}
is exact) in the following situations:

(i)

S^{\prime}
is a group; in this case,
k[S]
is even free over
k[S^{\prime}]
since, by definition,
S
is contained in a group.

(ii)

In the context of Example
2.2.1
, the extensions
k[P^{+}]\subseteq k[P]
and
k[M^{+}]\subseteq k[M]
are flat (Lemma
3.4.4
).

Corollary 2.2.10
.

Let
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
be an open submonoid. For all
V\in\Rep_{k}(S)
the map

\displaystyle\Hom_{\Rep_{k}(S)}\bigl(\ind_{S^{\prime}}^{S}\mathbf{1},V\bigr)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{H}^{0}(S^{\prime},V)\coloneqq\left\{v\in V\,\middle|\,\text{$sv=v$ for all $s\in S^{\prime}$}\right\},

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle\longmapsto\varphi(1\otimes 1)

is a natural bijection. In particular,
\left\{\ind_{S^{\prime}}^{S}\mathbf{1}\,\middle|\,\text{$S^{\prime}\subseteq S$ open}\right\}
is a set of generators for
\Rep_{k}(S)
.

Proof.

For the definition of a set of generators we refer to

[
KS06
, Def. 5.2.1]
.
The first assertion is an easy consequence of Lemma
2.2.9
.
Therefore, in order to prove the last statement, it suffices to prove that a
morphism
V\longrightarrow W
in
\Rep_{k}(S)
is an isomorphism if the induced maps

V^{S^{\prime}}\longrightarrow W^{S^{\prime}}
are bijective, for all open submonoids
S^{\prime}
. But this
follows from the fact that
V
and
W
are smooth.
∎

Proposition 2.2.11
.

The category
\Rep_{k}(S)
is Grothendieck abelian.

Proof.

By Corollaries
2.2.4
and
2.2.10
we know that

\Rep_{k}(S)
is abelian and admits a set of generators. Given a family

\{V_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of smooth representations, the direct sum
\bigoplus_{i\in I}V_{i}
is again smooth. It follows that
\Rep_{k}(S)
has all colimits and that
they can be computed in
\Mod(k[S])
. Therefore, all filtered colimits are
exact,
i.e.
,
\Rep_{k}(S)
satisfies the Grothendieck axiom AB5. Moreover, the
representation
\bigl(\prod_{i\in I}V_{i}\bigr)^{\mathrm{sm}}
is a categorical product in

\Rep_{k}(S)
, so that
\Rep_{k}(S)
satisfies the axiom AB3*.
∎

Remark 2.2.12
.

If
\Char k
divides the pro-order of every open profinite subgroup of
S
, the
formation of infinite direct products is not exact in
\Rep_{k}(S)
. As an
example, consider
S=\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
k=\mathbb{F}_{p}
. For each
n\geq 1
the map

f_{n}\colon k[\mathbb{Z}_{p}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{p}]\longrightarrow k
,
x+(p^{n})\mapsto 1
, is a surjection between
smooth
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-representations, where
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
acts trivially on
k
. Note that,
for
m<n
, the induced map
\mathrm{H}^{0}(p^{m}\mathbb{Z}_{p},k[\mathbb{Z}_{p}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{p}])\longrightarrow k
on

p^{m}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-invariants is zero (this uses
\Char k=p
). Therefore, the map

\Bigl(\prod_{n\geq 1}k[\mathbb{Z}_{p}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{p}]\Bigr)^{\mathrm{sm}}\xrightarrow{(f_{n})_{n}}\Bigl(\prod_{n\geq 1}k\Bigr)^{\mathrm{sm}}=\prod_{n\geq 1}k

takes values in
\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}k
and hence is not surjective.

The failure of having exact direct products means that

\Rep_{k}(S)
does not have enough projective objects, see

[
Roo06
, Thm. 1.3]
. (In fact, it is conjectured that
\Rep_{k}(S)
has no non-zero projectives at all. For profinite groups this is resolved in
[
CK23
, Thm 3.1]
.) This is the source of many difficulties in the
theory of smooth mod
p
representations of
p
-adic Lie groups.

2.2.2.
Inflation

Let
f\colon S\longrightarrow\hskip-11.99998pt\rightarrow\hskip 1.99997ptT
be a surjective open morphism of
locally profinite monoids with kernel
U
. Inflation along
f
defines an
exact functor
\Inf^{T}_{S}\colon\Rep_{k}(T)\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(S)
.

Proposition 2.2.13
.

\Inf^{T}_{S}
admits a left and a right adjoint. More concretely:

(a)

The functor

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}^{0}_{U}\colon\Rep_{k}(S)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(T),

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathrm{L}^{0}_{U}(V)\coloneqq k[T]\otimes_{k[S]}V

is a left adjoint of
\Inf^{T}_{S}
and, in particular, right exact.

(b)

The functor

\displaystyle\Pi_{U}\colon\Rep_{k}(S)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(T),

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle\longmapsto\Pi_{U}(V)\coloneqq\Hom_{k[S]}(k[T],V)^{\mathrm{sm}}

is a right adjoint of
\Inf^{T}_{S}
and, in particular, left exact.

Proof.

(a)

We write
\Inf^{T}_{S}W=\Hom_{k[T]}(k[T],W)
,
where
S
acts on the right on
k[T]
. The assertion follows from the

\otimes
-
\Hom
adjunction,
[
Ben91a
, Lem. 2.8.2]
:

\Hom_{k[S]}\bigl(V,\Hom_{k[T]}(k[T],W)\bigr)\cong\Hom_{k[T]}\bigl(k[T]\otimes_{k[S]}V,W\bigr).

(b)

We write
\Inf^{T}_{S}W=k[T]\otimes_{k[T]}W
,
where
S
acts on the left on
k[T]
. By
loc. cit.
we have

\displaystyle\Hom_{k[S]}\bigl(k[T]\otimes_{k[T]}W,V\bigr)

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{k[T]}\bigl(W,\Hom_{k[S]}(k[T],V)\bigr)

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{k[T]}\bigl(W,\Hom_{k[S]}(k[T],V)^{\mathrm{sm}}\bigr),

where the second bijection comes from the fact that every homomorphic image of a
smooth representation is smooth.

∎

Remark 2.2.14
.

The description of the left and right adjoints of
\Inf^{T}_{S}
is quite different
from the more familiar description in the case where
S
(and hence
T
,
U
) is
a group. It is instructive to make precise the distinction between both
descriptions.

(i)

Given
V\in\Rep_{k}(S)
, put
V(U)\coloneqq\spann_{k}\{uv-v\mid u\in U,v\in V\}
, where
\spann_{k}\{\ldots\}
denotes the
k
-linear span. There
is a canonical
k
-linear surjection

(2.2.15)

V_{U}\coloneqq V/V(U)\longrightarrow\hskip-11.99998pt\rightarrow\hskip 1.99997ptk[T]\otimes_{k[S]}V,

given by mapping the coset of
v
to
1\otimes v
. Note that, if
sU\subseteq Us
for all
s\in S
, then there is a natural
k[S]
-action on
V_{U}
. If
even
f^{-1}(f(s))=Us
for all
s\in S
, then this action induces a

k[T]
-action on
V_{U}
. In this case, the kernel of
k[S]\longrightarrow\hskip-11.99998pt\rightarrow\hskip 1.99997ptk[T]
is generated as a right ideal by
\left\{u-1\,\middle|\,u\in U\right\}
, and
hence (
2.2.15
) is an isomorphism of smooth
T
-representations.

(ii)

Evaluation at
1\in k[T]
induces an injective
k
-linear map

\ev_{1}\colon\Pi_{U}(V)\lhook\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{0}(U,V).

We observe that, if
Us\subseteq sU
for all
s\in S
, then there is a natural

k[S]
-action on
\mathrm{H}^{0}(U,V)
. If even
f^{-1}(f(s))=sU
for all
s\in S
, then
this action induces a
k[T]
-action on
\mathrm{H}^{0}(U,V)
. In this case, the kernel of

k[S]\longrightarrow\hskip-11.99998pt\rightarrow\hskip 1.99997ptk[T]
is generated as a left ideal by

\left\{u-1\,\middle|\,u\in U\right\}
, and hence
\ev_{1}
is an isomorphism of smooth

T
-representations.

Example 2.2.16
.

In order to see where some of the above conditions may fail, let us consider the
positive monoid, cf. Example
2.2.1
.
More concretely,
let
P\subseteq\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
be the subgroup of upper triangular matrices,
M

the subgroup of diagonal matrices, and
U
the subgroup of upper triangular
unipotent matrices. Put
K_{P}=P\cap\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
,
K_{M}=M\cap K_{P}
, and
K_{U}=U\cap K_{P}
. Consider now the positive monoid
M^{+}=\left\{m\in M\,\middle|\,mK_{U}m^{-1}\subseteq K_{U}\right\}
and put
P^{+}=K_{U}M^{+}
. Let
f\colon P^{+}\longrightarrow M^{+}
be the
canonical projection with kernel
K_{U}
.

It is obvious that
f^{-1}(m)=K_{U}m
,
for all
m\in M^{+}
, and hence
V_{K_{U}}\cong\mathrm{L}^{0}_{K_{U}}(V)
as smooth

M^{+}
-representations, for all
V\in\Rep_{k}(P^{+})
.

However, for
m\coloneqq\diag(p,1)\in M^{+}
, we have
K_{U}m\not\subseteq mK_{U}
. Therefore
\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},V)
is not stable under the
M^{+}
-action and

\ev_{1}\colon\Pi_{K_{U}}(V)\lhook\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},V)
is not surjective. We
remark that
\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},V)
can be endowed with a “Hecke action”,
cf.
[
Eme10a
, Lem. 3.1.4]
, making it a smooth
M^{+}
-representation.

2.2.3.
Symmetric monoidal structure

Given
V,W
in
\Rep_{k}(S)
, we let
S
act diagonally on the tensor product

V\otimes_{k}W
; this is again a smooth representation. Therefore,
\Rep_{k}(S)

carries the structure of a symmetric monoidal category, where the
\otimes
-unit
is given by the trivial representation
k
.

Proposition 2.2.17
.

The symmetric monoidal category
\Rep_{k}(S)
is closed, that is, for each
V
in

\Rep_{k}(S)
the functor

{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}\otimes_{k}V\colon\Rep_{k}(S)\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(S)

admits a right adjoint
\hom_{\Rep_{k}(S)}(V,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\colon\Rep_{k}(S)\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(S)
.
Moreover, there is a natural
S
-equivariant isomorphism

\hom_{\Rep_{k}(S)}\bigl(U\otimes_{k}V,W\bigr)\cong\hom_{\Rep_{k}(S)}\bigl(U,\hom_{\Rep_{k}(S)}(V,W)\bigr).

Proof.

Let
V,W\in\Rep_{k}(S)
. We let
S
act diagonally on
k[S]\otimes_{k}V
and then on
the
k
-vector space
\Hom_{k[S]}\bigl(k[S]\otimes_{k}V,W\bigr)
via
(sf)(s^{\prime}\otimes v)\coloneqq f(s^{\prime}s\otimes v)
. One easily verifies
that the maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\Rep_{k}(S)}\bigl(U\otimes_{k}V,W\bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\Rep_{k}(S)}\bigl(U,\Hom_{k[S]}(k[S]\otimes_{k}V,W)^{\mathrm{sm}}\bigr),}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[u\mapsto[s\otimes v\mapsto\varphi(su\otimes v)]]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[u\otimes v\mapsto\psi(u)(1\otimes v)]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

are natural and inverse to each other. Hence,

\hom_{\Rep_{k}(S)}(V,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\coloneqq\Hom_{k[S]}(k[S]\otimes_{k}V,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})^{\mathrm{sm}}
is right
adjoint to
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}\otimes_{k}V
. Let
X,U,V,W\in\Rep_{k}(S)
. Now, there are natural
bijections

\displaystyle\Hom_{\Rep_{k}(S)}\bigl(X,\hom_{\Rep_{k}(S)}(U\otimes_{k}V,W)\bigr)

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\Rep_{k}(S)}\bigl(X\otimes_{k}U\otimes_{k}V,W\bigr)

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\Rep_{k}(S)}\bigl(X\otimes_{k}U,\hom_{\Rep_{k}(S)}(V,W)\bigr)

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\Rep_{k}(S)}\bigl(X,\hom_{\Rep_{k}(S)}(U,\hom_{\Rep_{k}(S)}(V,W))\bigr).

The last claim follows from this together with the Yoneda lemma.
∎

Remark 2.2.18
.

If
S
is a group, the right adjoint of
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}\otimes_{k}V
has a more familiar
description: The map
\sigma\colon k[S]\otimes_{k}V\longrightarrow k[S]\otimes_{k}V
,
s\otimes v\longmapsto s\otimes s^{-1}v
is an isomorphism of
k[S]
-modules if we let
S

act diagonally on the left hand side and on the first factor on the right hand
side.
There is a natural
k[S]
-linear isomorphism

\Hom_{k}(V,W)\cong\Hom_{k[S]}\bigl(k[S]\otimes_{k}V,W\bigr)\xrightarrow[\sigma^{*}]{\cong}\Hom_{k[S]}\bigl(k[S]\otimes_{k}V,W\bigr)

if we let
S
act on the left hand side via
(sf)(v)=s\cdot f(s^{-1}v)
.
Therefore, the right adjoint of
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}\otimes_{k}V
is in this case given by

\Hom_{k}(V,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})^{\mathrm{sm}}
.

2.3.
The derived category

2.3.1.
Derived functors

Let
S
be a locally profinite monoid. We denote by
\mathrm{C}(S)
the category of
unbounded cochain complexes of smooth
S
-representations and by
\mathrm{K}(S)
its
homotopy category. Then
\mathrm{K}(S)
carries the structure of a triangulated
category
[
KS06
, Prop. 11.2.8]
. We denote by

\mathrm{D}(S)\coloneqq\mathrm{D}\bigl(\Rep_{k}(S)\bigr)

the derived category of unbounded complexes in
\Rep_{k}(S)
. It arises from

\mathrm{K}(S)
by localization at the class of quasi-isomorphisms

[
KS06
, §13.1]
. Note that
\mathrm{D}(S)
inherits the structure of a triangulated
category from
\mathrm{K}(S)
such that the localization functor

\q\colon\mathrm{K}(S)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(S)

is triangulated. The shift of a complex
C
(in
\mathrm{K}(S)
or
\mathrm{D}(S)
) will be
denoted
C[1]
, defined by
(C[1])^{n}\coloneqq C^{n+1}
and with differential

d_{C[1]}=-d_{C}
. We recall the most important properties of
\mathrm{D}(S)
, which
are
summarized in
[
KS06
, Thm. 14.3.1]
. The derived category
\mathrm{D}(S)
admits
infinite direct sums, and they can be computed on complexes. By the main result
of
[
Ser03
, Thm. 3.13]
or by
[
KS06
, Cor. 14.1.8]
every
complex
X
in
\mathrm{K}(S)
admits a quasi-isomorphism
X\xrightarrow{\mathrm{qis}}I
into a
K-injective complex
I
. (Recall that a complex
J
in
\mathrm{K}(S)
is called

K-injective
if
\Hom_{\mathrm{K}(S)}(Y,J)=0
for all acyclic complexes
Y
.)
This amounts to saying that
\q
admits a fully faithful triangulated right
adjoint

\ii\colon\mathrm{D}(S)\longrightarrow\mathrm{K}(S)

which identifies
\mathrm{D}(S)
with the full subcategory of
\mathrm{K}(S)
consisting of
K-injective complexes. In particular,
\mathrm{D}(S)
is locally small. It also follows
that right derived functors always exist:

Proposition 2.3.1
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be an abelian category and
F\colon\Rep_{k}(S)\longrightarrow\mathscr{A}
a left
exact functor. A right derived functor
\mathrm{R}F\colon\mathrm{D}(S)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{A})

exists and is given by the composition

\mathrm{R}F\colon\mathrm{D}(S)\xrightarrow{\ii}\mathrm{K}(S)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{K}(F)}\mathrm{K}(\mathscr{A})\xrightarrow{\q_{\mathscr{A}}}\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{A}),

where
\q_{\mathscr{A}}
denotes the localization functor and
\mathrm{K}(F)
the natural
extension of
F
to the homotopy categories.

Notation 2.3.2
.

If
F\colon\Rep_{k}(S)\longrightarrow\mathscr{A}
happens to be exact, we simply write
F\colon\mathrm{D}(S)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{A})
for the right derived functor of
F
. This is justified by
the fact that in this case the right adjoint can be computed by applying
F

termwise to the complex.

Here is a useful and well-known lemma:

Lemma 2.3.3
.

Let
L\colon\mathscr{A}\longrightarrow\mathscr{B}
be an exact functor between abelian categories
admitting a right adjoint
R
.

(a)

\mathrm{K}(R)\colon\mathrm{K}(\mathscr{B})\longrightarrow\mathrm{K}(\mathscr{A})
preserves
K-injective complexes.

(b)

Assume moreover that the localization functors

\q_{\mathscr{A}}\colon\mathrm{K}(\mathscr{A})\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{A})
and
\q_{\mathscr{B}}\colon\mathrm{K}(\mathscr{B})\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{B})
admit right adjoints
\ii_{\mathscr{A}}
and
\ii_{\mathscr{B}}
, respectively. Then there is
a natural isomorphism
\mathrm{K}(R)\ii_{\mathscr{B}}\xRightarrow{\cong}\ii_{\mathscr{A}}\mathrm{R}R

of functors
\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{B})\longrightarrow\mathrm{K}(\mathscr{A})
.

(c)

Under the hypotheses of

(b)
, the functor
\mathrm{R}R
is a right adjoint of

L\colon\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{A})\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{B})
.

Proof.

Note that
L\dashv R
extends to an adjunction
\mathrm{K}(L)\dashv\mathrm{K}(R)
.
Let
I\in\mathrm{K}(\mathscr{B})
be K-injective and
Y\in\mathrm{K}(\mathscr{A})
an acyclic complex.
Then
\mathrm{K}(L)(Y)
is acyclic and hence
\Hom_{\mathrm{K}(\mathscr{A})}(Y,\mathrm{K}(R)(I))\cong\Hom_{\mathrm{K}(\mathscr{B})}(\mathrm{K}(L)(Y),I)=0
. This proves
(a)
.

We now prove
(b)
. Note that the unit
\id_{\mathrm{K}(\mathscr{A})}\Longrightarrow\ii_{\mathscr{A}}\q_{\mathscr{A}}
is an isomorphism on the K-injective
complexes. Since
\mathrm{K}(R)
preserves K-injective complexes, it follows that the
map
\mathrm{K}(R)\ii_{\mathscr{B}}\Longrightarrow\ii_{\mathscr{A}}\q_{\mathscr{A}}\mathrm{K}(R)\ii_{\mathscr{B}}=\ii_{\mathscr{A}}\mathrm{R}R
is an isomorphism.

For
(c)
, note that
\mathrm{K}(R)
is right adjoint to
\mathrm{K}(L)\colon\mathrm{K}(\mathscr{A})\longrightarrow\mathrm{K}(\mathscr{B})
. There are natural bijections

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{A})}\bigl(\q_{\mathscr{A}}X,\mathrm{R}RY\bigr)

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathrm{K}(\mathscr{A})}\bigl(X,\ii_{\mathscr{A}}\mathrm{R}RY\bigr)

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathrm{K}(\mathscr{A})}\bigl(X,\mathrm{K}(R)\ii_{\mathscr{B}}Y\bigr)

(by

(b)
)

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathrm{K}(\mathscr{B})}\bigl(\mathrm{K}(L)X,\ii_{\mathscr{B}}Y\bigr)

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{B})}\bigl(\q_{\mathscr{B}}\mathrm{K}(L)X,Y\bigr)

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{B})}\bigl(L\q_{\mathscr{A}}X,Y\bigr)

for all
X\in\mathrm{K}(\mathscr{A})
and
Y\in\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{B})
. This implies

(c)
.
∎

Example 2.3.4
.

Let
K\subseteq S
be an open subgroup.

(a)

The functor
\Hom_{\Rep_{k}(K)}(\mathbf{1},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\colon\Rep_{k}(K)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Vect}_{k}
is
canonically isomorphic to
\mathrm{H}^{0}(K,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
. This extends to a natural isomorphism

\Hom_{\Rep_{k}(K)}^{\bullet}(\mathbf{1},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\xRightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{K}(\mathrm{H}^{0}(K,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}))
of functors

\mathrm{K}(K)\longrightarrow\mathrm{K}(\mathrm{Vect}_{k})
, which induces a natural isomorphism

\RHom_{\Rep_{k}(K)}(\mathbf{1},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\xRightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})

of functors
\mathrm{D}(K)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(k)
.

(b)

The functor
\mathrm{H}^{0}(K,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\Res^{S}_{K}\colon\Rep_{k}(S)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Vect}_{k}
of

K
-invariants is left exact and hence admits a right derived functor

\mathrm{R}\bigl(\mathrm{H}^{0}(K,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\Res^{S}_{K}\bigr)\colon\mathrm{D}(S)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(k)
. We observe that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(k)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{S}_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{R}(\mathrm{H}^{0}(K,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}}})\Res^{S}_{K})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}}})}

commutes. Indeed, since
K\subseteq S
is a subgroup,
\Res^{S}_{K}
admits an

exact
left adjoint
\ind_{K}^{S}
(Lemma
2.2.9
). By
Lemma
2.3.3
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\Res^{S}_{K}

\displaystyle=\q\mathrm{H}^{0}(K,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\ii\Res^{S}_{K}\cong\q\mathrm{H}^{0}(K,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\Res^{S}_{K}\ii

\displaystyle=\mathrm{R}\bigl(\mathrm{H}^{0}(K,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\Res^{S}_{K}\bigr).

For this reason, we often allow ourselves to omit
\Res^{S}_{K}
when it does not
lead to confusion.

Recall that, by definition, every smooth
S
-representation
V
satisfies
V=\varinjlim_{K}\Hom_{\Rep_{k}(K)}(\mathbf{1},V)
, where
K
runs through the compact open
subgroups of
S
. This property continues to hold on the level of derived
categories as well as the following proposition makes precise.

Proposition 2.3.5
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{D}(S)
. There exists a natural isomorphism

(2.3.6)

\varinjlim_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\leq S\\
\text{compact open}\end{subarray}}\RHom_{\Rep_{k}(K)}\bigl(\mathbf{1},\Res^{S}_{K}X\bigr)\xrightarrow{\cong}\Res^{S}_{1}X\qquad\text{in $\mathrm{D}(k)$.}

Proof.

Since taking filtered colimits is exact in
\mathrm{Vect}_{k}
, the maps

\displaystyle\varinjlim_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\leq S\\
\text{compact open}\end{subarray}}\RHom_{\Rep_{k}(K)}\bigl(\mathbf{1},\Res^{S}_{K}X\bigr)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}\varinjlim_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\leq S\\
\text{compact open}\end{subarray}}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K,X)

\displaystyle=\varinjlim_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\leq S\\
\text{compact open}\end{subarray}}\q\mathrm{H}^{0}(K,\ii X)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}\q\varinjlim_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\leq S\\
\text{compact open}\end{subarray}}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K,\ii X)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}\q\Res^{S}_{1}\ii X

\displaystyle\cong\Res^{S}_{1}X

are natural isomorphisms.
∎

Remark 2.3.7
.

If
S
is a locally profinite group, one can make sense of the left hand side
of (
2.3.6
) even as an object of
\mathrm{D}(S)
and show that there is
a natural isomorphism

\varinjlim_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\leq S\\
\text{compact open}\end{subarray}}\RHom_{\Rep_{k}(K)}(\mathbf{1},\Res^{S}_{K}X)\xrightarrow{\cong}X\qquad\text{in $\mathrm{D}(S)$.}

2.3.2.
Brown representability

We will now formulate the Brown representability theorems. We make the
assumption that
S
is a
p
-adic monoid and
k

has characteristic
p
. Then
S
admits a compact open subgroup
K

which carries the structure of a
p
-adic Lie group of dimension, say,
d
.
Replacing
K
by an open subgroup, we may even assume that
K
is torsion-free.
Then
K
is a Poincaré group by
[
Ser65
, Cor. (1)]
and

[
Laz65
, V.2.5.8]
. In
particular, the cohomology groups
\mathrm{H}^{i}(K,k)
are finite-dimensional, and

\mathrm{H}^{i}(K,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})=0
provided
i>d
.

Proposition 2.3.8
.

Let
S
be a
p
-adic monoid and let
K\subseteq S
be a torsion-free compact
open subgroup. Then
\mathrm{D}(S)
is compactly generated by
\ind_{K}^{S}\mathbf{1}
.

Proof.

This is precisely
[
Sch15
, Prop. 6]
in the case where
S
is
a
p
-adic Lie group, and the proof is general. We only have to observe that

\ind_{K}^{S}
is an exact functor, since
K
is a group, and hence the adjunction

\ind_{K}^{S}\dashv\Res^{S}_{K}
(Lemma
2.2.9
) extends to the derived
categories.
∎

Theorem 2.3.9

(Brown representability)
.

Denote by
\mathrm{(Ab)}
the category of abelian groups.

(a)

Let
H\colon\mathrm{D}(S)^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{(Ab)}
be a cohomological
functor commuting with small products.
3
3

3

This means that
H
sends small direct sums in
\mathrm{D}(S)
to products in
\mathrm{(Ab)}
.
Then
H
is representable, that is, there
exists an object
X\in\mathrm{D}(S)
and a natural isomorphism
\Hom_{\mathrm{D}(S)}({\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}},X)\xRightarrow{\cong}H
. In particular,
\mathrm{D}(S)
admits small
products.

(b)

Let
H\colon\mathrm{D}(S)\longrightarrow\mathrm{(Ab)}
be a cohomological functor
commuting with small direct sums. Then
H
is corepresentable, that is, there
exists an object
X\in\mathrm{D}(S)
and a natural isomorphism
\Hom_{\mathrm{D}(S)}(X,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\xRightarrow{\cong}H
.

Proof.

See
[
Nee01
, Thm. 8.3.3]
or
[
Kra02
, Thm. A]
for

(a)
and
[
Kra02
, Thm. B]
for
(b)
.
∎

The following corollary is a standard application of Brown representability.

Corollary 2.3.10
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a triangulated category and
F\colon\mathrm{D}(S)\longrightarrow\mathscr{C}
a
triangulated functor.

(a)

F
admits a triangulated right adjoint if and only if

F
commutes with small direct sums.

(b)

F
admits a triangulated left adjoint if and only if

F
commutes with small products.

Proof.

We only prove
(b)
and leave
(a)
as an exercise. It
is clear that
F
commutes with small products if
F
admits a left adjoint
(see Proposition
2.1.7
).

Conversely, if
F
commutes with
small products, the functor
\Hom_{\mathscr{C}}\bigl(X,F({\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\bigr)\colon\mathrm{D}(S)\longrightarrow\mathrm{(Ab)}
is cohomological and commutes with
small products. By Theorem
2.3.9
this functor is representable. From
Theorem
2.1.6
it follows that
F
admits a left adjoint,
which, by
[
Nee01
, Lem. 5.3.6]
, is even triangulated.
∎

2.3.3.
Tensor triangulated structure

The category
\mathrm{D}(S)
carries the structure of a closed symmetric monoidal
category, which was introduced and investigated in
[
SS23
]
when
S
is a group.
The tensor product of cochain complexes endows the triangulated category
\mathrm{K}(S)

with a symmetric monoidal structure. The
\otimes
-unit is given by
\mathbf{1}\coloneqq k[0]
, that is,
k
viewed as a complex concentrated in degree
0
.
Since, for any
X,Y\in\mathrm{K}(S)
, the complex

X\otimes_{k}Y
is acyclic
whenever either
X
or
Y
is acyclic, the induced symmetric monoidal structure on

\mathrm{D}(S)
is again given by
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}\otimes_{k}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}
.

Proposition 2.3.11
.

The symmetric monoidal category
\mathrm{D}(S)
is closed.

Proof.

Let
X\in\mathrm{D}(S)
. Then
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}\otimes_{k}X
is a triangulated functor commuting with
small direct sums. By Corollary
2.3.10
.
(a)
, it admits a
right adjoint.
∎

Remark 2.3.12
.

The internal Hom objects in
\mathrm{D}(S)
are explicitly given as follows: Let
X,Y\in\mathrm{C}(S)
be complexes and define a new complex
\iHom(X,Y)
in
\mathrm{C}(S)

by

\iHom^{n}(X,Y)\coloneqq\Bigl(\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\Hom_{k[S]}\bigl(k[S]\otimes_{k}X^{i},Y^{i+n}\bigr)\Bigr)^{\mathrm{sm}},

with differential
(df)^{i}=d_{Y}^{n+i}\circ f^{i}-(-1)^{n}f^{i+1}\circ(1\otimes d_{X}^{i})
, for
f\in\iHom^{n}(X,Y)
; here,
S
acts diagonally on
k[S]\otimes_{k}X^{i}
. This yields a
k
-linear bifunctor

(2.3.13)

\iHom\colon\mathrm{C}(S)^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{C}(S)\longrightarrow\mathrm{C}(S).

A direct computation shows

(2.3.14)

\mathrm{H}^{0}\bigl(\iHom(X,Y)\bigr)\cong\varinjlim_{\begin{subarray}{c}S^{\prime}\leq S\\
\text{open}\end{subarray}}\Hom_{\mathrm{K}(S)}\bigl(k[S/S^{\prime}]\otimes_{k}X,Y\bigr).

As in the proof of Proposition
2.2.17
one constructs a natural
isomorphism

\iHom\bigl(X\otimes_{k}Y,Z\bigr)\cong\iHom\bigl(X,\iHom(Y,Z)\bigr).

Taking cohomology and then
S
-invariants yields a natural isomorphism

(2.3.15)

\Hom_{\mathrm{K}(S)}\bigl(X\otimes_{k}Y,Z\bigr)\cong\Hom_{\mathrm{K}(S)}\bigl(X,\iHom(Y,Z)\bigr).

This has the following immediate consequences:

(i)

The bifunctor (
2.3.13
) descends to a bifunctor

\iHom\colon\mathrm{K}(S)^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{K}(S)\longrightarrow\mathrm{K}(S)

making
\mathrm{K}(S)
a closed symmetric monoidal category.

(ii)

As a right adjoint of
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}\otimes_{k}Y
, the functor

\iHom(Y,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
is triangulated, by
[
Nee01
, Lem. 5.3.6]
. It is also
true that
\iHom({\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}},Y)
preserves distinguished triangles, cf.
[
KS06
, Prop. 11.6.4.(ii)]
.

(iii)

If
Z
is K-injective, then the complex
\iHom(Y,Z)

again is K-injective.

(iv)

If
Z
is K-injective and
Y
is acyclic, then
\iHom(Y,Z)

is contractible and, in particular, acyclic.
Consequently,
\iHom({\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}},Z)
preserves quasi-isomorphisms.

The last property shows that the right derived functor

\mathrm{R}\iHom\colon\mathrm{D}(S)^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{D}(S)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(S)

exists and is computed by
\mathrm{R}\iHom(X,Y)\cong\iHom(X,\ii Y)
. Using

(iii)
above, one shows that there is a natural isomorphism

\Hom_{\mathrm{D}(S)}\bigl(X\otimes_{k}Y,Z\bigr)\cong\Hom_{\mathrm{D}(S)}\bigl(X,\mathrm{R}\iHom(Y,Z)\bigr).

Therefore,
\mathrm{R}\iHom(Y,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
is the right adjoint of
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}\otimes_{k}Y
. Moreover,
(
2.3.14
), applied to
\ii Y
, shows that there is a natural
isomorphism

(2.3.16)

\mathrm{H}^{0}\bigl(\mathrm{R}\iHom(X,Y)\bigr)\cong\varinjlim_{\begin{subarray}{c}S^{\prime}\leq S\\
\text{open}\end{subarray}}\Hom_{\mathrm{D}(S)}\bigl(k[S/S^{\prime}]\otimes_{k}X,Y\bigr).

If, in addition,
S
is a group, then Remark
2.2.18

implies that there is a natural isomorphism

(2.3.17)

\mathrm{H}^{0}\bigl(\mathrm{R}\iHom(X,Y)\bigr)\cong\varinjlim_{\begin{subarray}{c}S^{\prime}\leq S\\
\text{open}\end{subarray}}\Hom_{\mathrm{D}(S^{\prime})}(X,Y).

Notation 2.3.18
.

Given
X\in\mathrm{D}(S)
, we denote the right adjoint of
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}\otimes_{k}X
by

\hom_{\mathrm{D}(S)}(X,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
or just
\hom(X,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
if the category is clear from the
context.

We call
X^{\vee}\coloneqq\hom(X,\mathbf{1})
the
dual
of
X
in
\mathrm{D}(S)
.

Warning 2.3.19
.

The internal Hom complex
\hom_{\mathrm{D}(S)}(X,Y)\in\mathrm{D}(S)
should not be confused
with the
\RHom
complex
\RHom_{\Rep_{k}(S)}(X,Y)
, which is only an object
of
\mathrm{D}(k)
. In general, there is the relation

(2.3.20)

\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(S,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\circ\hom_{\mathrm{D}(S)}(X,Y)\cong\RHom_{\Rep_{k}(S)}(X,Y),

which follows from
(iii)
above,
i.e.
, the fact that
\iHom(X,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})

preserves K-injective complexes.

2.3.4.
Rigidly-compact generation

Let
K
be a compact torsion-free
p
-adic Lie group and
k
a field of
characteristic
p
. The next crucial observation allows us to appeal to the
results of
[
BDS16
]
.

Proposition 2.3.21
.

The category
\mathrm{D}(K)
is rigidly-compactly generated.

Proof.

We know from Proposition
2.3.8
that
\mathrm{D}(K)
is compactly
generated by
\mathbf{1}
. It remains to show that the set of compact objects coincides
with the set of rigid objects. Recall that
X\in\mathrm{D}(K)
is called
compact

if for every family
\{Y_{i}\}_{i\in I}
in
\mathrm{D}(K)
the natural map

\bigoplus_{i\in I}\Hom_{\mathrm{D}(K)}(X,Y_{i})\longrightarrow\Hom_{\mathrm{D}(K)}\Bigl(X,\bigoplus_{i\in I}Y_{i}\Bigr)

is bijective. Recall also that
X
is called
rigid
if for all
Y\in\mathrm{D}(K)
the natural map

X^{\vee}\otimes_{k}Y\longrightarrow\hom(X,Y)

is an isomorphism in
\mathrm{D}(K)
. Since
\mathbf{1}
is compact and

\Hom_{\mathrm{D}(K)}\bigl(\mathbf{1},\hom(X,Y)\bigr)=\Hom_{\mathrm{D}(K)}(X,Y)
, it is immediate
that rigid objects are compact. For the converse, it is easy to see that
\mathbf{1}
is
rigid and that the strictly full subcategory of rigid objects is triangulated and
closed under direct summands (closure under direct summands can either be checked directly or by observing that split idempotents are absolute equalizers
[
Bor94
, Prop. 6.5.4]
). Therefore, the strictly full, thick, triangulated
subcategory
\langle\mathbf{1}\rangle
generated by
\mathbf{1}
consists
of rigid objects. Now,
[
Nee92
, Lem. 2.2]
shows that
\langle\mathbf{1}\rangle
consists of all the compact objects in
\mathrm{D}(K)
.
∎

2.3.5.
Spectral sequences

Lacking a good reference, we explain how to construct spectral sequences using
the canonical
t
-structure on
\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{A})
, for
\mathscr{A}
an abelian category.
Everything in this paragraph is well-known and holds in much greater generality,
cf.
[
Lur12
, 1.2.2]
.

Our main reference is
[
Ben91b
, 3.2]
, which constructs spectral
sequences from filtered complexes. Our treatment is very similar, yet not the
same, and so we refer to
loc. cit.
for the proofs, which apply verbatim in our
case if the notation is reinterpreted accordingly.

First, let us recall the canonical
t
-structure on
\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{A})
, for an
abelian category
\mathscr{A}
. Given a cochain complex
(C,\{d^{i}\}_{i})\in\mathrm{C}(\mathscr{A})
, the truncated complexes
\tau^{\leq n}C
and
\tau^{\geq n}C
in

\mathrm{C}(\mathscr{A})
are defined by

\displaystyle\tau^{\leq n}C\colon

\displaystyle\qquad\dotsb\xrightarrow{d^{n-3}}C^{n-2}\xrightarrow{d^{n-2}}C^{n-1}\xrightarrow{d^{n-1}}\Ker(d^{n})\longrightarrow 0\longrightarrow\dotsb

and

\displaystyle\tau^{\geq n}C\colon

\displaystyle\qquad\dotsb\longrightarrow 0\longrightarrow\Coker(d^{n-1})\xrightarrow{d^{n}}C^{n+1}\xrightarrow{d^{n+1}}C^{n+2}\xrightarrow{d^{n+2}}\dotsb

The truncation functors
\tau^{\leq n}
and
\tau^{\geq n}
preserve
quasi-isomorphisms and hence give rise to truncation functors on
\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{A})
.
For each complex
C
there is a distinguished triangle

\tau^{\leq n}C\longrightarrow C\longrightarrow\tau^{\geq n+1}C\xrightarrow{+}

in
\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{A})
, and we have
\Hom_{\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{A})}(\tau^{\leq n}C,\tau^{\geq n+1}D)=0
, for all
C,D\in\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{A})
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. This defines the canonical

t
-structure on
\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{A})
. Note also that

\mathrm{H}^{n}(C)[-n]\cong\tau^{\leq n}\tau^{\geq n}C\cong\tau^{\geq n}\tau^{\leq n}C,\qquad\text{for each $C\in\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{A})$.}

The following version of the hypercohomology spectral sequence will be useful
later. For the notion of way-out functors, we refer to

[
Har66
, §I.7]

Lemma 2.3.22
.

Let
\mathscr{A},\mathscr{B}
be abelian categories and let
X\in\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{A})
.

(a)

Let
F\colon\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{A})\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{B})
be a
triangulated functor. Assume that one of the following properties is
satisfied:

\bullet

F
is way-out right (e.g., left
t
-exact) and
X
is left bounded;

\bullet

F
is way-out left (e.g., right
t
-exact) and
X
is right bounded;

\bullet

F
is way-out in both directions (e.g.,
t
-exact);

\bullet

X
is bounded.

Then there exists a convergent spectral sequence

E_{2}^{i,j}=(\mathrm{H}^{i}F)\bigl(\mathrm{H}^{j}(X)\bigr)\Longrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{i+j}(FX).

(b)

Let
F\colon\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{A})^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{B})
be a
triangulated functor. Assume that one of the following properties is satisfied:

\bullet

F
is way-out right and
X
is right bounded;

\bullet

F
is way-out left and
X
is left bounded;

\bullet

F
is way-out in both directions;

\bullet

X
is bounded.

Then there exists a convergent spectral sequence

E_{2}^{i,j}=(\mathrm{H}^{i}F)\bigl(\mathrm{H}^{-j}(X)\bigr)\Longrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{i+j}(FX).

Proof.

The following construction is adapted from
[
Ben91b
, §3.2]
although we use slightly different notation. We refer
to
loc. cit.
for details about the proofs.

(a)

Put
X(r)\coloneqq\tau^{\leq-r}X
for each

r\in\mathbb{Z}
. Given
s\geq r
, we define
X(r,s)
by the distinguished triangle
X(s)\longrightarrow X(r)\longrightarrow X(r,s)\xrightarrow{+}
in
\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{A})
; note that
X(r,r+1)\cong\mathrm{H}^{-r}(X)[r]
. Applying
F
, we obtain a distinguished triangle

FX(s)\longrightarrow FX(r)\longrightarrow FX(r,s)\xrightarrow{+}\quad\text{in $\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{B})$.}

For
s=r+1
we contemplate the induced long exact sequence in cohomology

\dotsb\longrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{n}\bigl(FX(r+1)\bigr)\xrightarrow{i_{1}}\mathrm{H}^{n}\bigl(FX(r)\bigr)\xrightarrow{j_{1}}\mathrm{H}^{n}\bigl(FX(r,r+1)\bigr)\xrightarrow{k_{1}}\mathrm{H}^{n+1}\bigl(FX(r+1)\bigr)\longrightarrow\dotsb.

Define
D_{1}^{r,s}\coloneqq\mathrm{H}^{r+s}\bigl(FX(r)\bigr)
and
E_{1}^{r,s}\coloneqq\mathrm{H}^{r+s}\bigl(FX(r,r+1)\bigr)
, for all
r,s\in\mathbb{Z}
. We obtain an exact couple

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{1}^{*,*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{1}^{*,*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{1}^{*,*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{1}}

that is, we have
\Image(i_{1})=\Ker(j_{1})
,
\Image(j_{1})=\Ker(k_{1})
, and

\Image(k_{1})=\Ker(i_{1})
. We denote by
d_{1}=j_{1}\circ k_{1}\colon E_{1}^{*,*}\longrightarrow E_{1}^{*+1,*}
the associated differential.
Taking derived couples yields a spectral sequence
\{(E_{n},d_{n})\}_{n\geq 1}
.

For each
n\geq 1
we put

\displaystyle Z_{n}^{r,s}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Image\bigl(\mathrm{H}^{r+s}\bigl(FX(r,r+n)\bigr)\longrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{r+s}\bigl(FX(r,r+1)\bigr)\bigr),

and

\displaystyle B_{n}^{r,s}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Ker\bigl(\mathrm{H}^{r+s}\bigl(FX(r,r+1)\bigr)\longrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{r+s}\bigl(FX(r-n+1,r+1)\bigr)\bigr)

and then
Z_{\infty}^{r,s}\coloneqq\bigcap_{n}Z_{n}^{r,s}
,
B_{\infty}^{r,s}\coloneqq\bigcup_{n}B_{n}^{r,s}
. Now,
[
Ben91b
, Prop. 3.2.4]
shows

E_{n}^{r,s}=Z_{n}^{r,s}/B_{n}^{r,s}
. Put
E_{\infty}^{r,s}\coloneqq Z_{\infty}^{r,s}/B_{\infty}^{r,s}
.
We define a descending filtration on
\mathrm{H}^{r+s}(FX)
by

\mathcal{F}\kern-1.5pt{\mathit{il}}^{r}\mathrm{H}^{r+s}(FX)\coloneqq\Image\bigl(\mathrm{H}^{r+s}(FX(r))\longrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{r+s}(FX)\bigr).

We now verify that
\{(E_{n}^{*,*},d_{n})\}_{n}
is
biregular
, that is:

(i)

For all
r,s\in\mathbb{Z}
there exists
n\gg 0
such that
Z_{\infty}^{r,s}=Z_{n}^{r,s}
and
B_{\infty}^{r,s}=B_{n}^{r,s}
.

(ii)

For all
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have
\mathcal{F}\kern-1.5pt{\mathit{il}}^{r}\mathrm{H}^{m}(FX)=0
, for
r\gg 0
, and

\mathcal{F}\kern-1.5pt{\mathit{il}}^{r}\mathrm{H}^{m}(FX)=\mathrm{H}^{m}(FX)
, for
r\ll 0
.

It then follows that the spectral sequence converges to
\mathrm{H}^{*}(FX)
.

\bullet

If
X
is left bounded, then we have
Z_{n}^{r,s}=Z_{\infty}^{r,s}
, for

n\gg 0
, and
\mathcal{F}\kern-1.5pt{\mathit{il}}^{r}\mathrm{H}^{m}(FX)=0
, for fixed
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and all
r\gg 0
. Indeed,
there exists
n\gg 0
such that
X(r+n)=0
. From
the distinguished triangle
X(r+n)\longrightarrow X(r)\longrightarrow X(r,r+n)\xrightarrow{+}
we deduce

X(r)\cong X(r,r+n)
. This implies
\mathrm{H}^{r+s}(FX(r,r+n))\cong\mathrm{H}^{r+s}(FX(r))
and hence
Z_{n}^{r,s}=Z_{\infty}^{r,s}
, for
n\gg 0
. Moreover,
for all
r\gg 0
, we have
X(r)=0
and hence
\mathrm{H}^{m}(FX(r))=0
, that is,

\mathcal{F}\kern-1.5pt{\mathit{il}}^{r}\mathrm{H}^{m}(FX(r))=0
, for
r\gg 0
.

\bullet

If
X
is right bounded, then we have
B_{n}^{r,s}=B_{\infty}^{r,s}
, for

n\gg 0
, and
\mathcal{F}\kern-1.5pt{\mathit{il}}^{r}\mathrm{H}^{m}(FX)=\mathrm{H}^{m}(FX)
, for fixed
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and all

r\ll 0
. Indeed, there exists
n\gg 0
such that
X(r-n+1)\cong X(r-n^{\prime}+1)
, for all
n^{\prime}\geq n
. This implies
\mathrm{H}^{r+s}(FX(r-n+1,r+1))\cong\mathrm{H}^{r+s}(FX(r-n^{\prime}+1,r+1))
and hence
