B_{n}^{r,s}=B_{\infty}^{r,s}
. Moreover, for
all
r\gg 0
we have
X(r)=X
and hence
\mathcal{F}\kern-1.5pt{\mathit{il}}^{r}\mathrm{H}^{m}(FX)=\mathrm{H}^{m}(FX)
.

\bullet

If
F
is way-out right, then we have
B_{n}^{r,s}=B_{\infty}^{r,s}
, for

n\gg r+s
, and
\mathcal{F}\kern-1.5pt{\mathit{il}}^{r}\mathrm{H}^{m}(FX)=\mathrm{H}^{m}(FX)
, for all
r\ll-m
. Indeed,
we have
\mathrm{H}^{r+s}(FX(r-n^{\prime}+1,r-n+1))=0
whenever
n^{\prime}\geq n\gg r+s
. Hence, from
the distinguished triangle

FX(r-n^{\prime}+1,r+1)\longrightarrow FX(r-n+1,r+1)\longrightarrow FX(r-n+1,r-n^{\prime}+1)\xrightarrow{+},

we deduce an isomorphism

\mathrm{H}^{r+s}\bigl(FX(r-n+1,r+1)\bigr)\cong\mathrm{H}^{r+s}\bigl(FX(r-n^{\prime}+1,r+1)\bigr),

which shows
B_{n}^{r,s}=B_{\infty}^{r,s}
. Similarly, we have
\mathrm{H}^{m}(FX(r))\cong\mathrm{H}^{m}(FX)
, whenever
r\ll-m
, and hence
\mathcal{F}\kern-1.5pt{\mathit{il}}^{r}\mathrm{H}^{m}(FX)=\mathrm{H}^{m}(FX)
.

\bullet

If
F
is way-out left, then we have
Z_{n}^{r,s}=Z_{\infty}^{r,s}
, for
all
n\gg-2r-s
, and
\mathcal{F}\kern-1.5pt{\mathit{il}}^{r}\mathrm{H}^{m}(FX)=0
, for
r\gg-m
.

Indeed, we have
\mathrm{H}^{r+s}(FX(r+n,r+n^{\prime}))=0
whenever
n^{\prime}\geq n\gg-2r-s
.
From the distinguished triangle

FX(r+n,r+n^{\prime})\longrightarrow FX(r,r+n^{\prime})\longrightarrow FX(r,r+n)\xrightarrow{+}

we deduce an isomorphism
\mathrm{H}^{r+s}(FX(r,r+n^{\prime}))\cong\mathrm{H}^{r+s}(FX(r,r+n))
, which
shows
Z_{n}^{r,s}=Z_{\infty}^{r,s}
. Moreover, for
r\gg-m
we have
\mathrm{H}^{m}(FX(r))=0
, so that
\mathcal{F}\kern-1.5pt{\mathit{il}}^{r}\mathrm{H}^{m}(FX)=0
.

By
[
Ben91b
, Thm. 3.2.9]
we have

\mathcal{F}\kern-1.5pt{\mathit{il}}^{r}\mathrm{H}^{r+s}(FX)/\mathcal{F}\kern-1.5pt{\mathit{il}}^{r+1}\mathrm{H}^{r+s}(FX)\cong E_{\infty}^{r,s}.

Therefore, the spectral sequence indeed computes
\mathrm{H}^{*}(FX)
. Note that

E_{1}^{r,s}=\mathrm{H}^{r+s}\bigl(FX(r,r+1)\bigr)=(\mathrm{H}^{r+s}F)\bigl(\mathrm{H}^{-r}(X)[r]\bigr)=(\mathrm{H}^{2r+s}F)\bigl(\mathrm{H}^{-r}(X)\bigr).

Hence, reindexing
(i,j)\coloneqq(2r+s,-r)
yields the desired spectral
sequence
E_{2}^{i,j}=E_{1}^{r,s}=(\mathrm{H}^{i}F)\bigl(\mathrm{H}^{j}(X)\bigr)\Longrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{i+j}(FX)
.

(b)

Of course, this follows by duality from

(a)
, but let us nevertheless give the construction in this
case. Put
X(r)\coloneqq\tau^{\geq r}X
for each

r\in\mathbb{Z}
. Given
r\leq s
, we have a distinguished triangle
X(r,s)\longrightarrow X(r)\longrightarrow X(s)\xrightarrow{+}
in
\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{A})
; note that
X(r,r+1)\cong\mathrm{H}^{r}(X)[-r]
.
Applying
F
, we obtain a distinguished triangle

FX(s)\longrightarrow FX(r)\longrightarrow FX(r,s)\xrightarrow{+}\quad\text{in $\mathrm{D}(\mathscr{B})$.}

Put
D_{1}^{r,s}\coloneqq\mathrm{H}^{r+s}(FX(r))
and
E_{1}^{r,s}\coloneqq\mathrm{H}^{r+s}(FX(r,r+1))
.
As in
(a)
this gives rise to a spectral sequence

\{(E_{n},d_{n})\}_{n}
which converges to
\mathrm{H}^{*}(FX)
. We compute

E_{1}^{r,s}=\mathrm{H}^{r+s}\bigl(FX(r,r+1)\bigr)=(\mathrm{H}^{r+s}F)\bigl(\mathrm{H}^{r}(X)[-r]\bigr)=(\mathrm{H}^{2r+s}F)\bigl(\mathrm{H}^{r}(X)\bigr).

Again, reindexing
(i,j)\coloneqq(2r+s,-r)
yields the desired spectral
sequence
E_{2}^{i,j}=E_{1}^{r,s}=(\mathrm{H}^{i}F)\bigl(\mathrm{H}^{-j}(X)\bigr)\Longrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{i+j}(FX)
.

∎

3.
The left adjoint of derived inflation

From now on,
k
denotes a field of characteristic
p>0
.

3.1.
The compact case

Let
f\colon K_{P}\longrightarrow\hskip-11.99998pt\rightarrow\hskip 1.99997ptK_{M}
be a surjective homomorphism of
torsion-free, compact
p
-adic Lie groups with kernel
K_{U}
. Note that
f
is
automatically open as a surjection between profinite groups. Being
torsion-free
p
-adic Lie groups, these groups are pro-
p
, and

[
Ser65
, Cor. (1)]

combined with
[
Laz65
, V.2.5.8]
shows that the groups
K_{P},K_{M}
, and

K_{U}
are Poincaré, cf.
[
Ser13
, Ch. I, §4.5]
. Recall that a pro-
p

group
K
is called
Poincaré
of dimension
n
if
\mathrm{H}^{i}(K,\mathbb{F}_{p})
is
finite for all
i
,
\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\mathrm{H}^{n}(K,\mathbb{F}_{p})=1
, and the cup-product

\mathrm{H}^{i}(K,\mathbb{F}_{p})\times\mathrm{H}^{n-i}(K,\mathbb{F}_{p})\longrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{n}(K,\mathbb{F}_{p})
is a non-degenerate
bilinear form.

Notation 3.1.1
.

If
K^{\prime}_{P}\subseteq K_{P}
is an open subgroup, we write
K^{\prime}_{M}\coloneqq f(K^{\prime}_{P})

and
K^{\prime}_{U}\coloneqq K_{U}\cap K^{\prime}_{P}
.

By Proposition
2.3.21
the categories
\mathrm{D}(K_{P})
and
\mathrm{D}(K_{M})

are rigidly-compactly generated. Note that inflation along
f

yields an exact functor
\Inf^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}\colon\Rep_{k}(K_{M})\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(K_{P})
that is strictly monoidal, which means

\Inf^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}(\mathbf{1})=\mathbf{1}
and

\Inf^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}(V\otimes_{k}W)\cong\Inf^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}(V)\otimes_{k}\Inf^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}(W)
, for all
V,W\in\Rep_{k}(K_{M})
. The right adjoint is given
by the functor of invariants
\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
.

Passing to the derived categories, we obtain a strictly monoidal functor

\Inf^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}\colon\mathrm{D}(K_{M})\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(K_{P})

with right adjoint
\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
. For later use we record the following
result:

Lemma 3.1.2

(Projection formula)
.

The natural map

X\otimes_{k}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},Y)\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}\bigl(K_{U},\Inf^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}(X)\otimes_{k}Y\bigr)

is an isomorphism, for all
X\in\mathrm{D}(K_{M})
and
Y\in\mathrm{D}(K_{P})
. In particular,

X\otimes_{k}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},\mathbf{1})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}\bigl(K_{U},\Inf^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}X\bigr)
, for all
X\in\mathrm{D}(K_{M})
.

Proof.

This follows from
[
BDS16
, 2.15. Prop.]
together with
Proposition
2.3.21
.
∎

Our goal in this section is to prove the existence of the left adjoint of

\Inf^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}
and to determine it explicitly. To start, we observe
that
\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
admits a right adjoint.

Lemma 3.1.3
.

The functor
\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\colon\mathrm{D}(K_{P})\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(K_{M})
admits a right adjoint,
denoted
F^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}
. More precisely, there is a natural isomorphism

(3.1.4)

\RHom_{\Rep_{k}(K_{P})}\bigl(X,F^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}(Y)\bigr)\cong\RHom_{\Rep_{k}(K_{M})}\bigl(\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},X),Y\bigr)

in
\mathrm{D}(k)
, for all
X\in\mathrm{D}(K_{P})
and
Y\in\mathrm{D}(K_{M})
.

Further, for each open subgroup
K^{\prime}_{P}\subseteq K_{P}
, the following diagram of
functors commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(K_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(K_{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(K^{\prime}_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(K^{\prime}_{P}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{K_{M}}_{K^{\prime}_{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{K_{P}}_{K^{\prime}_{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{K^{\prime}_{M}}_{K^{\prime}_{P}}}

Proof.

The functor
\Inf^{K_{M}}_{K_{M}}
satisfies
[
BDS16
, Hyp. 1.2]
and hence

[
BDS16
, Cor. 2.14]
shows that the right adjoint
F^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}
of

\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
exists.
Then
[
BDS16
, (2.18)]
shows that there is a natural isomorphism

\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}\bigl(K_{U},\hom_{\mathrm{D}(K_{P})}(X,F^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}(Y))\bigr)\xrightarrow{\cong}\hom_{\mathrm{D}(K_{M})}\bigl(\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},X),Y\bigr)

in
\mathrm{D}(K_{M})
. Applying
\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{M},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
, and using
\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{M},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\circ\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\cong\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{P},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
and (
2.3.20
), yields the
isomorphism (
3.1.4
).

Let now
K^{\prime}_{P}\subseteq K_{P}
be an open subgroup. It is obvious that there is a
natural isomorphism
\Inf^{K^{\prime}_{M}}_{K^{\prime}_{P}}\Res^{K_{M}}_{K^{\prime}_{M}}\xRightarrow{\cong}\Res^{K_{P}}_{K^{\prime}_{P}}\Inf^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}
. Passing to the right adjoints, see
Example
2.1.13
, yields a natural isomorphism
\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\Ind_{K^{\prime}_{P}}^{K_{P}}\xRightarrow{\cong}\Ind_{K^{\prime}_{M}}^{K_{M}}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K^{\prime}_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
. Since

K^{\prime}_{P}\subseteq K_{P}
is open, the functor
\Res^{K_{P}}_{K^{\prime}_{P}}
is also right
adjoint to
\Ind_{K^{\prime}_{P}}^{K_{P}}
. Similarly,
\Res^{K_{M}}_{K^{\prime}_{M}}
is the right
adjoint of
\Ind_{K^{\prime}_{M}}^{K_{M}}
. Hence, passing to the right adjoints again
yields a natural isomorphism
F^{K^{\prime}_{M}}_{K^{\prime}_{P}}\Res^{K_{M}}_{K^{\prime}_{M}}\xRightarrow{\cong}\Res^{K_{P}}_{K^{\prime}_{P}}F^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}
.
∎

Lemma 3.1.5
.

Let
f^{\prime}\colon K_{M}\longrightarrow\hskip-11.99998pt\rightarrow\hskip 1.99997ptK_{L}
be another surjective homomorphism of
torsion-free
p
-adic Lie groups. Then the following diagram of functors
commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(K_{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(K_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(K_{P})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{K_{L}}_{K_{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{K_{L}}_{K_{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}}

Proof.

Clearly, we have a natural isomorphism
\Inf^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}\Inf^{K_{L}}_{K_{M}}\xRightarrow{\cong}\Inf^{K_{L}}_{K_{P}}
. Passing to the right adjoints,
cf. Example
2.1.13
, yields an isomorphism
\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(\Ker(f^{\prime}\circ f),{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\xRightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(\Ker(f^{\prime}),{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\circ\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(\Ker(f),{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
. The statement
follows by passing to the right adjoints again.
∎

Proposition 3.1.6
.

The functor
\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\colon\mathrm{D}(K_{P})\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(K_{M})
preserves compact objects.

Proof.

Since
K_{U}
a Poincaré group, the object
\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},\mathbf{1})\in\mathrm{D}(K_{M})
is
represented by a bounded complex with finite-dimensional cohomologies. Since

\mathbf{1}\in\mathrm{D}(K_{M})
is compact by Proposition
2.3.8
, and
because the subcategory of compact objects is triangulated, it
suffices to show that
\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},\mathbf{1})
is contained in the strictly full
triangulated subcategory
\langle\mathbf{1}\rangle
of
\mathrm{D}(K_{M})
generated by
\mathbf{1}
.
This follows from a standard argument which we recall for the benefit of the
reader. Let
X\in\mathrm{D}(K_{M})
be bounded with finite-dimensional cohomology groups.
There exist integers
a\leq b
with
\tau^{\leq i}X\cong 0
for
i<a
and

\tau^{\geq i}X\cong 0
for
i>b
. Then
\tau^{\geq b}X\cong\mathrm{H}^{b}(X)[-b]
is
finite-dimensional and concentrated in degree
b
. We obtain a distinguished
triangle
\tau^{\leq b-1}X\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{b}(X)[-b]\xrightarrow{+}
.
Hence, by induction on
b-a
we are reduced to the case where
X
is a
finite-dimensional smooth
K_{M}
-representation concentrated in one degree,
say
X\cong V[i]
. Then
\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{M},V)
is non-zero (since
K_{M}
is a pro-
p

group) and contained in
\langle\mathbf{1}\rangle
, because
\langle\mathbf{1}\rangle
is
closed under finite direct sums. By induction on
\dim_{k}V
also the quotient

V/\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{M},V)
is contained in
\langle\mathbf{1}\rangle
. As
\langle\mathbf{1}\rangle

is triangulated and
\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{M},V)\longrightarrow V\longrightarrow V/\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{M},V)\xrightarrow{+}
is a
distinguished triangle in
\mathrm{D}(K_{M})
, it follows that
V
, hence also
X
, is
contained in
\langle\mathbf{1}\rangle
.
∎

We draw two important consequences of Proposition
3.1.6
,
but first we make a definition.

Definition
.

The object
\omega_{K_{P}}\coloneqq F^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}(\mathbf{1})
in
\mathrm{D}(K_{P})
is called
the
dualizing object
.

Corollary 3.1.7

(Grothendieck duality)
.

For all
X,Y\in\mathrm{D}(K_{M})
there is a natural isomorphism

F^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}(X)\otimes_{k}\Inf^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}(Y)\xrightarrow{\cong}F^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}(X\otimes_{k}Y).

In particular, we have a natural isomorphism
\omega_{K_{P}}\otimes_{k}\Inf^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}(Y)\xrightarrow{\cong}F^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}(Y)
.

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
3.1.6
and

[
BDS16
, 3.2. Prop.]
.
∎

Corollary 3.1.8
.

The functor
\Inf^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}\colon\mathrm{D}(K_{M})\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(K_{P})
preserves small
products. In particular, it admits a left adjoint
\mathrm{L}_{K_{U}}\colon\mathrm{D}(K_{P})\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(K_{M})
. Moreover, there is a natural isomorphism

(3.1.9)

\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},\omega_{K_{P}}\otimes_{k}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\xRightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{L}_{K_{U}}.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.1.6
the adjunction
\Inf^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}\dashv\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
restricts to an adjunction on the full subcategories of
compact objects
\mathrm{D}(K_{M})^{\mathrm{c}}\subseteq\mathrm{D}(K_{M})
and
\mathrm{D}(K_{P})^{\mathrm{c}}\subseteq\mathrm{D}(K_{P})
. The argument in
[
BDS16
, Lem. 3.1]
shows that the inflation
functor
\mathrm{D}(K_{M})^{\mathrm{c}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(K_{P})^{\mathrm{c}}
admits a left adjoint,
and then
[
BDS16
, Lem. 2.6(b)]
shows that
\Inf^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}
preserves
small products. By the
Corollary
2.3.10
.
(b)
to Brown representability it follows
that the left adjoint
\mathrm{L}_{K_{U}}
exists. The isomorphism
(
3.1.9
) is the ur-Wirthmüller isomorphism

[
BDS16
, (3.10)]
.
∎

To finish our discussion of
\mathrm{L}_{K_{U}}
we determine the dualizing complex

\omega_{K_{P}}
explicitly.

Proposition 3.1.10
.

One has
\omega_{K_{P}}\cong k[\dim K_{U}]
in
\mathrm{D}(K_{P})
. In particular,

\omega_{K_{P}}
is
\otimes
-invertible.

Proof.

We apply Lemma
3.1.5
to the surjection
f^{\prime}\colon K_{M}\longrightarrow\{1\}
, and together with Corollary
3.1.7
we obtain isomorphisms

F^{1}_{K_{P}}(\mathbf{1})\cong F^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}\bigl(F^{1}_{K_{M}}(\mathbf{1})\bigr)\cong\omega_{K_{P}}\otimes_{k}\Inf^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}\bigl(F^{1}_{K_{M}}(\mathbf{1})\bigr).

Hence, if we can show
F^{1}_{K_{P}}(\mathbf{1})\cong k[\dim K_{P}]
and
F^{1}_{K_{M}}(\mathbf{1})\cong k[\dim K_{M}]
, the assertion follows from
\dim K_{P}=\dim K_{M}+\dim K_{U}
,
cf.
[
DDMS99
, 4.8 Thm.]
.

It remains to prove that
F^{1}_{K}(\mathbf{1})\cong k[\dim K]
provided
K
is a
torsion-free
p
-adic Lie group.

We have the following isomorphisms in
\mathrm{D}(k)
:

\displaystyle\Res^{K}_{1}F^{1}_{K}(\mathbf{1})

\displaystyle\cong\varinjlim_{K^{\prime}\leq K}\RHom_{\Rep_{k}(K^{\prime})}\bigl(\mathbf{1},\Res^{K}_{K^{\prime}}F^{1}_{K}(\mathbf{1})\bigr)

(Prop.
2.3.5
)

\displaystyle\cong\varinjlim_{K^{\prime}\leq K}\RHom_{\Rep_{k}(K^{\prime})}\bigl(\mathbf{1},F^{1}_{K^{\prime}}(\mathbf{1})\bigr)

(Lem.
3.1.3
)

\displaystyle\cong\varinjlim_{K^{\prime}\leq K}\RHom_{\mathrm{Vect}_{k}}\bigl(\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K^{\prime},\mathbf{1}),\mathbf{1}\bigr)

(by (
3.1.4
)).

Passing to the
i
-th cohomology yields isomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{i}\bigl(F^{1}_{K}(\mathbf{1})\bigr)

\displaystyle\cong\varinjlim_{K^{\prime}\leq K}\Hom_{\mathrm{D}(k)}(\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K^{\prime},\mathbf{1}),\mathbf{1}[i]\bigr)

\displaystyle\cong\varinjlim_{K^{\prime}\leq K}\Hom_{k}\bigl(\mathrm{H}^{-i}(K^{\prime},\mathbf{1}),k\bigr).

Here, the transition maps are induced by the corestriction

\cores\colon\mathrm{H}^{-i}(K^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{1})\longrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{-i}(K^{\prime},\mathbf{1}),

for
K^{\prime\prime}\subseteq K^{\prime}
. Now, (5) in the proof of
[
Ser13
, Ch. I,
Prop. 30]
implies
\mathrm{H}^{i}\bigl(F^{1}_{K}(\mathbf{1})\bigr)=0
provided

i\neq-\dim K
. By (4) in the proof of
loc. cit.
the above corestriction maps are
isomorphisms for
i=-\dim K
. Therefore, we have
F^{1}_{K}(\mathbf{1})\cong\mathrm{H}^{\dim K}(K,\mathbf{1})^{*}[\dim K]
. Since
\mathrm{H}^{\dim K}(K,\mathbf{1})
is one-dimensional,
it follows that
F^{1}_{K}(\mathbf{1})\cong\delta[\dim K]
, for some smooth character

\delta\colon K\longrightarrow k^{\times}
. Since
K
is a pro-
p
group and
\Char k=p
, it follows that
\delta
is the trivial character, which proves

F^{1}_{K}(\mathbf{1})\cong k[\dim K]
.
∎

Proposition
3.1.10
verifies condition (W4) in
[
BDS16
, 1.9.
Thm.]
. Precisely, this means:

Corollary 3.1.11
.

Put
f^{(n)}\coloneqq\omega_{K_{P}}^{\otimes n}\otimes_{k}\Inf^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}
and

f_{(n)}\coloneqq\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}\bigl(K_{U},\omega_{K_{P}}^{\otimes n}\otimes_{k}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}\bigr)
,
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. There is an infinite chain of adjunctions

\dotsb\dashv f^{(-1)}\dashv f_{(1)}\dashv f^{(0)}\dashv f_{(0)}\dashv f^{(1)}\dashv f_{(-1)}\dashv\dotsb

3.2.
The general case

In this section we fix an open surjective morphism
f\colon S\longrightarrow\hskip-11.99998pt\rightarrow\hskip 1.99997ptT
of
p
-adic monoids with kernel
U
. The functor

\Inf^{T}_{S}\colon\mathrm{D}(T)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(S)
is strictly monoidal and admits the right
adjoint
\mathrm{R}\Pi_{U}
, cf. Proposition
2.2.13
and
Lemma
2.3.3
.
(c)
. The goal of this
section is to show that
\Inf^{T}_{S}
also admits a left adjoint and study its
properties. We make the following observation:

Proposition 3.2.1
.

For all
X\in\mathrm{D}(S)
and
Y\in\mathrm{D}(T)
the natural map

\hom_{\mathrm{D}(T)}\bigl(Y,\mathrm{R}\Pi_{U}(X)\bigr)\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{R}\Pi_{U}\bigl(\hom_{\mathrm{D}(S)}(\Inf^{T}_{S}Y,X)\bigr)

is an isomorphism in
\mathrm{D}(T)
.

Proof.

Let
W\in\mathrm{D}(T)
. Since
\Inf^{T}_{S}
is strictly monoidal, the natural
transformation

(\Inf^{T}_{S}Y\otimes_{k}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\circ\Inf^{T}_{S}\xRightarrow{\cong}\Inf^{T}_{S}\circ(Y\otimes_{k}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})

is an isomorphism of functors
\mathrm{D}(T)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(S)
. Passing to the right adjoints,
see Example
2.1.13
, shows that the natural map

\hom_{\mathrm{D}(T)}(Y,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\circ\mathrm{R}\Pi_{U}\xRightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{R}\Pi_{U}\circ\hom_{\mathrm{D}(S)}(\Inf^{T}_{S}Y,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})

is an isomorphism of functors
\mathrm{D}(S)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(T)
.
∎

Lemma 3.2.2
.

Let
K_{S}\subseteq S
be an open subgroup. The restriction functor

\Res^{S}_{K_{S}}\colon\mathrm{D}(S)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(K_{S})
is conservative and preserves small
products and small coproducts.

Proof.

Note that if
\varphi
is a morphism in
\mathrm{C}(S)
, then

\Res^{S}_{K_{S}}(\varphi)
is a quasi-isomorphism if and only if
\varphi
is a
quasi-isomorphism. This implies that
\Res^{S}_{K_{S}}\colon\mathrm{D}(S)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(K_{S})
is
conservative.

Since
K_{S}\subseteq S
is an open subgroup, the functor

\Res^{S}_{K_{S}}\colon\Rep_{k}(S)\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(K_{S})
admits an exact left adjoint

\ind_{K_{S}}^{S}
and a left exact right adjoint
\Ind_{K_{S}}^{S}
, by
Lemmas
2.2.9
and
2.2.5
, respectively. But then
the induced functors
\ind_{K_{S}}^{S},\RInd_{K_{S}}^{S}\colon\mathrm{D}(K_{S})\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(S)
are
left adjoint, resp. right adjoint, to
\Res^{S}_{K_{S}}
. By
Proposition
2.1.7
,
\Res^{S}_{K_{S}}
preserves small products
and small coproducts.
∎

Theorem 3.2.3
.

The functor
\Inf^{T}_{S}\colon\mathrm{D}(T)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(S)
preserves small products. In
particular, it admits a left adjoint, denoted
\mathrm{L}_{U}
.

Proof.

Let
K_{S}\subseteq S
be an open, compact, torsion-free
p
-adic Lie group such
that
K_{T}\coloneqq f(K_{S})\subseteq T
is open and torsion-free.

Let
I
be a set, and take
Y_{i}\in\mathrm{D}(T)
, for
i\in I
. We show that the
natural map

\alpha_{S}\colon\Inf^{T}_{S}\prod_{i\in I}Y_{i}\longrightarrow\prod_{i\in I}\Inf^{T}_{S}Y_{i}.

is an isomorphism in
\mathrm{D}(S)
. Note that
\Res^{S}_{K_{S}}\Inf^{T}_{S}\cong\Inf^{K_{T}}_{K_{S}}\Res^{T}_{K_{T}}
as functors
\mathrm{D}(T)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(K_{S})
. By
Lemma
3.2.2
the functors
\Res^{S}_{K_{S}}
and
\Res^{T}_{K_{T}}

preserve small products. Now, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{S}_{K_{S}}\Inf^{T}_{S}\prod_{i\in I}Y_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{S}_{K_{S}}\prod_{i\in I}\Inf^{T}_{S}Y_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Inf^{K_{T}}_{K_{S}}\prod_{i\in I}\Res^{T}_{K_{T}}Y_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i\in I}\Inf^{K_{T}}_{K_{S}}\Res^{S}_{K_{S}}Y_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{S}_{K_{S}}(\alpha_{S})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{K_{S}}}

is commutative. By Corollary
3.1.8
the bottom map is an
isomorphism in
\mathrm{D}(K_{S})
. Therefore, the top map
\Res^{S}_{K_{S}}(\alpha_{S})
is an
isomorphism in
\mathrm{D}(K_{S})
. By Lemma
3.2.2
again,

\Res^{S}_{K_{S}}
is conservative, which shows that
\alpha_{S}
is an isomorphism
in
\mathrm{D}(S)
. Hence,
\Inf^{T}_{S}
preserves small products. By the
Corollary
2.3.10
.
(b)
of Brown representability, it
follows that
\Inf^{T}_{S}
admits a left adjoint.
∎

In fact, the adjunction
\mathrm{L}_{U}\dashv\Inf^{T}_{S}
holds on the level of
\RHom

complexes:

Corollary 3.2.4
.

There is a natural isomorphism

(3.2.5)

\RHom_{\Rep_{k}(T)}\bigl(\mathrm{L}_{U}(X),Y\bigr)\xrightarrow{\cong}\RHom_{\Rep_{k}(S)}\bigl(X,\Inf^{T}_{S}Y\bigr)\qquad\text{in $\mathrm{D}(k)$.}

Proof.

Since
\Inf^{T}_{S}
is an exact functor, it induces a natural morphism

(3.2.6)

\RHom_{\Rep_{k}(T)}(Y_{1},Y_{2})\longrightarrow\RHom_{\Rep_{k}(S)}\bigl(\Inf^{T}_{S}Y_{1},\Inf^{T}_{S}Y_{2}\bigr),

for all
Y_{1},Y_{2}\in\mathrm{D}(T)
. Let now
X\in\mathrm{D}(S)
and
Y\in\mathrm{D}(T)
, and denote

\eta_{X}\colon X\longrightarrow\Inf^{T}_{S}\mathrm{L}_{U}(X)
the unit of the adjunction.
Then (
3.2.5
) is given as the composite

\displaystyle\RHom_{\Rep_{k}(T)}\bigl(\mathrm{L}_{U}(X),Y\bigr)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\eqref{eq:RHom-Inf}}\RHom_{\Rep_{k}(S)}\bigl(\Inf^{T}_{S}\mathrm{L}_{U}(X),\Inf^{T}_{S}Y\bigr)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{(\eta_{V})^{*}}\RHom_{\Rep_{k}(S)}\bigl(X,\Inf^{T}_{S}Y\bigr).

On the
n
-th cohomology it is given by the adjunction isomorphism

\Hom_{\mathrm{D}(T)}\bigl(\mathrm{L}_{U}(X),Y[n]\bigr)\xrightarrow{\cong}\Hom_{\mathrm{D}(S)}\bigl(X,\Inf^{T}_{S}Y[n]\bigr).

Therefore, (
3.2.5
) is an isomorphism in
\mathrm{D}(k)
.
∎

Notation 3.2.7
.

Given
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we denote by
\mathrm{L}^{n}_{U}\colon\Rep_{k}(S)\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(T)
the
n
-th
cohomology functor of
\mathrm{L}_{U}
,
i.e.
,

\mathrm{L}^{n}_{U}(V)\coloneqq\mathrm{H}^{n}(\mathrm{L}_{U}V[0]),\qquad\text{for all $V\in\Rep_{k}(S)$.}

Proposition 3.2.8
.

The functor
\mathrm{L}_{U}\colon\mathrm{D}(S)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(T)
is right
t
-exact. More precisely, we
have
\mathrm{L}^{n}_{U}=0
for all
n>0
, and
\mathrm{L}^{0}_{U}
is the left adjoint of

\Inf^{T}_{S}\colon\Rep_{k}(T)\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(S)
.

Proof.

The right
t
-exactness is a formal consequence of the (left)
t
-exactness of

\Inf^{T}_{S}
. Let us show that
\mathrm{L}^{0}_{U}
is the left adjoint of
\Inf^{T}_{S}
. Let

\mathrm{D}^{\geq 0}(T)
be the full subcategory of
\mathrm{D}(T)
consisting of complexes
Y

with
\mathrm{H}^{n}(Y)=0
for all
n<0
. The truncation functor
\tau^{\geq 0}\colon\mathrm{D}(T)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}^{\geq 0}(T)
is left adjoint to the inclusion. Hence, given
V\in\Rep_{k}(S)
and
W\in\Rep_{k}(T)
, we compute

\displaystyle\Hom_{\Rep_{k}(T)}\bigl(\mathrm{L}^{0}_{U}(V),W\bigr)

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathrm{D}^{\geq 0}(T)}\bigl(\tau^{\geq 0}\mathrm{L}_{U}(V),W\bigr)

\displaystyle=\Hom_{\mathrm{D}(T)}\bigl(\mathrm{L}_{U}(V),W\bigr)

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathrm{D}(S)}\bigl(V,\Inf^{T}_{S}W\bigr)

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\Rep_{k}(S)}(V,\Inf^{T}_{S}W).\qed

Remark
.

Proposition
3.2.8
implies that there is a natural transformation

\mathrm{L}_{U}\circ\q_{S}\Longrightarrow\q_{T}\circ\mathrm{L}^{0}_{U}

of functors
\mathrm{K}(S)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(T)
.
I do not know whether the left derived functor of
\mathrm{L}^{0}_{U}
exists and, if it
does exist, whether it coincides with
\mathrm{L}_{U}
.

Corollary 3.2.9
.

Given
V\in\Rep_{k}(S)
and
W\in\Rep_{k}(T)
, there is a convergent
first-quadrant spectral sequence

E_{2}^{i,j}=\Ext^{i}_{\Rep_{k}(T)}\bigl(\mathrm{L}^{-j}_{U}(V),W\bigr)\Longrightarrow\Ext^{i+j}_{\Rep_{k}(S)}\bigl(V,\Inf^{T}_{S}W\bigr).

In particular, there is a five-term exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext^{1}_{\Rep_{k}(T)}\bigl(\mathrm{L}^{0}_{U}(V),W\bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext^{1}_{\Rep_{k}(S)}\bigl(V,\Inf^{T}_{S}W\bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\Rep_{k}(T)}\bigl(\mathrm{L}^{-1}_{U}(V),W\bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext^{2}_{\Rep_{k}(T)}\bigl(\mathrm{L}^{0}_{U}(V),W\bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext^{2}_{\Rep_{k}(S)}\bigl(V,\Inf^{T}_{S}W\bigr).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}^{0,1}}

Proof.

The functor
\RHom_{\Rep_{k}(T)}({\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}},W)
is left
t
-exact and
\mathrm{L}_{U}(V)
is
right bounded by Proposition
3.2.8
. Now,
Lemma
2.3.22
.
(b)
together with
Corollary
3.2.4
yields the desired spectral sequence.
∎

Proposition 3.2.10
.

Let
f^{\prime}\colon T\longrightarrow\hskip-11.99998pt\rightarrow\hskip 1.99997ptT^{\prime}
be another open surjective
morphism of
p
-adic monoids. Write
U^{\prime}=\Ker(f^{\prime})
and
U^{\prime\prime}=\Ker(f^{\prime}\circ f)
.

(a)

The diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(T^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}_{U^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}_{U^{\prime}}}

is commutative.

(b)

Given
V\in\Rep_{k}(S)
, there is a convergent
third-quadrant spectral sequence

E_{2}^{i,j}=\mathrm{L}^{i}_{U^{\prime}}\bigl(\mathrm{L}^{j}_{U}(V)\bigr)\Longrightarrow\mathrm{L}^{i+j}_{U^{\prime\prime}}(V).

In particular, there is a five-term exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}^{-2}_{U^{\prime\prime}}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}^{-2}_{U^{\prime}}\bigl(\mathrm{L}^{0}_{U}(V)\bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}^{0}_{U^{\prime}}\bigl(\mathrm{L}^{-1}_{U}(V)\bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}^{-1}_{U^{\prime\prime}}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}^{-1}_{U^{\prime}}\bigl(\mathrm{L}^{0}_{U}(V)\bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{-2,0}}

Proof.

(a)
follows from the natural isomorphism
\Inf^{T}_{S}\Inf^{T^{\prime}}_{T}\xRightarrow{\cong}\Inf^{T^{\prime}}_{S}
by passing to the left adjoints,
see Example
2.1.13
.
Now,
(b)
follows from
(a)
by
applying Lemma
2.3.22
.
(a)
and observing that the
functors
\mathrm{L}_{U^{\prime}}
and
\mathrm{L}_{U}
are right
t
-exact by
Proposition
3.2.8
.
∎

Proposition 3.2.11
.

Let
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
be an open submonoid and put
T^{\prime}\coloneqq f(S^{\prime})\subseteq T
. Put
U^{\prime}\coloneqq U\cap S^{\prime}
. Assume further that
k[S]
is flat over

k[S^{\prime}]
and
k[T]
is flat over
k[T^{\prime}]
. The following diagram is
commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(S^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(T^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(T).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ind_{S^{\prime}}^{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}_{U^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ind_{T^{\prime}}^{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}_{U}}

In particular, there is, for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, a natural isomorphism
\ind_{T^{\prime}}^{T}\mathrm{L}^{n}_{U^{\prime}}\xRightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{L}^{n}_{U}\ind_{S^{\prime}}^{S}
of functors

\Rep_{k}(S^{\prime})\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(T)
.

Proof.

Clearly there is a natural isomorphism
\Res^{S}_{S^{\prime}}\Inf^{T}_{S}\xRightarrow{\cong}\Inf^{T^{\prime}}_{S^{\prime}}\Res^{T}_{T^{\prime}}
. Passing to the left adjoints, see
Example
2.1.13
, yields the first assertion. The second assertion follows
from the first by passing to the
n
-th cohomology and using that
\ind_{T^{\prime}}^{T}

and
\ind_{S^{\prime}}^{S}
are exact.
∎

Proposition 3.2.12
.

Let
\chi\colon T\longrightarrow k^{\times}
be a smooth character viewed as a character of

S
via inflation along
f
. There is a natural isomorphism
\mathrm{L}_{U}(\chi\otimes_{k}V)\xrightarrow{\cong}\chi\otimes_{k}\mathrm{L}_{U}(V)
, for each
V\in\mathrm{D}(S)
.

Proof.

The functor
\chi\otimes_{k}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}
is an equivalence of triangulated categories with
quasi-inverse

\chi^{-1}\otimes_{k}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}
. In particular,
\chi\otimes_{k}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}
is left
adjoint to
\chi^{-1}\otimes_{k}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}
. Moreover,
there is a natural isomorphism
\Inf^{T}_{S}(\chi^{-1}\otimes_{k}W)\xrightarrow{\cong}\chi^{-1}\otimes_{k}\Inf^{T}_{S}(W)
, for
W\in\mathrm{D}(T)
. Passing
to the left adjoints, see Example
2.1.13
, yields the result.
∎

Remark 3.2.13
.

If
S
and
T
are
p
-adic Lie groups, we will obtain in
Corollary
3.3.5
a stronger version of the above
proposition.

Next, we will study how
\mathrm{L}_{U}
behaves under restriction. This requires some
preparatory lemmas. We will employ a version of the Mackey decomposition for
derived categories.

Notation 3.2.14
.

Let
G
be locally profinite group and
S\subseteq G
a closed submonoid. Given

g\in G
and
V\in\Rep_{k}(S)
, we define a smooth representation of
gSg^{-1}

on
V
via
(gsg^{-1})\star v\coloneqq sv
, for all
s\in S
,
v\in V
. We
denote this modified representation by
g_{*}V
. In this way, we obtain a functor

g_{*}\colon\Rep_{k}(S)\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(gSg^{-1})

which is easily seen to be an equivalence of categories. The induced equivalence
on the derived categories is again denoted
g_{*}
.

Lemma 3.2.15

(derived Mackey decomposition)
.

Assume that
S
is a locally profinite monoid. Let

K,H\subseteq S
be two subgroups with
K
open and
H
closed.

(a)

There is a natural
isomorphism of functors
\mathrm{D}(H)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(K)
,

\Res^{S}_{K}\RInd_{H}^{S}\xRightarrow{\cong}\prod_{s\in H\backslash S/K}\Ind_{s^{-1}Hs\cap K}^{K}s_{*}^{-1}\Res^{H}_{H\cap sKs^{-1}}.

(b)

There is a natural isomorphism of functors

\mathrm{D}(K)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(H)
,

\Res^{S}_{H}\ind_{K}^{S}\xLeftarrow{\cong}\bigoplus_{s\in H\backslash S/K}\ind_{H\cap sKs^{-1}}^{H}s_{*}\Res^{K}_{s^{-1}Hs\cap K}.

Proof.

Note that
\Ind^{K}_{s^{-1}Hs\cap K}
is exact by Remark
2.2.6
.
Both statements follow from the Mackey decomposition,

[
Yam22
, Thm. 1.1]
, by passing to the derived categories. For

(a)
we observe that the functors
\Res^{H}_{H\cap sKs^{-1}}
,
s_{*}^{-1}
, and
\Ind_{s^{-1}Hs\cap K}^{K}
preserve
K-injective complexes by Lemma
2.3.3
.
(b)
,
since they all admit exact left adjoints.
∎

Recall that we denoted by
U
the kernel of
f\colon S\longrightarrow\hskip-11.99998pt\rightarrow\hskip 1.99997ptT
.

Lemma 3.2.16
.

Let
H_{S}\subseteq S
be a subgroup and put
H_{T}\coloneqq f(H_{S})
. Assume that

f
induces a bijection
H_{S}\backslash S\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{T}\backslash T
.

(a)

One has
sU\subseteq H_{S}s
, for all
s\in S
. In
particular,
U\subseteq H_{S}
is a group.

(b)

Let
K_{S}\subseteq S
be a subgroup and put

K_{T}\coloneqq f(K_{S})
. The map
f
induces a
bijection
H_{S}\backslash S/K_{S}\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{T}\backslash T/K_{T}
.

Proof.

The first assertion in
(a)
is immediate from the
hypothesis, because
f(sU)=\{f(s)\}\subseteq H_{T}f(s)
is contained in a
single coset. For
s=1
this shows
U\subseteq H_{S}
. Thus,
U=U\cap H_{S}
is
the kernel of the group homomorphism
f\big|_{H_{S}}
, hence a group.
For
(b)
, note that
K_{S}
acts on
H_{S}\backslash S
by
right multiplication. By
(a)
, this action factors through

K_{T}
, and this implies the assertion.
∎

Lemma 3.2.17
.

Let
K_{P}
be a profinite group and
K_{U}\subseteq K^{\prime}_{P}\subseteq K_{P}
closed
subgroups with
K_{U}
normal in
K_{P}
. Write
K_{M}\coloneqq K_{P}/K_{U}
and

K^{\prime}_{M}\coloneqq K^{\prime}_{P}/K_{U}
.

The natural transformation

(3.2.18)

\Inf^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}\Ind_{K^{\prime}_{M}}^{K_{M}}\xRightarrow{\cong}\Ind_{K^{\prime}_{P}}^{K_{P}}\Inf^{K^{\prime}_{M}}_{K^{\prime}_{P}}

is an isomorphism of functors
\mathrm{D}(K^{\prime}_{M})\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(K_{P})
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that (
3.2.18
) is an isomorphism of functors

\Rep_{k}(K^{\prime}_{M})\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(K_{P})
, since by Remark
2.2.6
, the
underived functors
\Ind_{K^{\prime}_{M}}^{K_{M}}
and
\Ind_{K^{\prime}_{P}}^{K_{P}}
are exact. But
this can be checked directly or by observing that
the natural map
\mathrm{L}^{0}_{K_{U}}\Res^{K_{P}}_{K^{\prime}_{P}}\xRightarrow{\cong}\Res^{K_{M}}_{K^{\prime}_{M}}\mathrm{L}^{0}_{K_{U}}
is obviously an isomorphism of functors

\Rep_{k}(K_{P})\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(K^{\prime}_{M})
, and then passing to the right adjoints,
see Example
2.1.13
.
∎

For the rest of this section we assume that
S
and
T
are open submonoids of

p
-adic Lie groups
P
and
M
, respectively, and that
f
arises from a
continuous surjection
P\longrightarrow\hskip-11.99998pt\rightarrow\hskip 1.99997ptM
. This condition will be
satisfied for the
p
-adic monoids we consider. Note that, since
M
with the
quotient topology is a
p
-adic Lie group,
[
DDMS99
, Thm. 9.6 (ii)]
, and the

p
-adic analytic structure on
M
is unique,
[
DDMS99
, Cor. 9.5]
, it
follows that
P\longrightarrow\hskip-11.99998pt\rightarrow\hskip 1.99997ptM
is a quotient map of topological
groups, hence is open. Therefore,
f
is automatically open.

Proposition 3.2.19
.

Let
H_{S}\subseteq S
be a closed subgroup and put
H_{T}\coloneqq f(H_{S})\subseteq T
. Assume that
f
induces a bijection
H_{S}\backslash S\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{T}\backslash T
. The diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(H_{T})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(H_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(H_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(H_{T})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Inf^{H_{T}}_{H_{S}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\RInd_{H_{T}}^{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\RInd_{H_{S}}^{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{S}_{H_{S}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{T}_{H_{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Inf^{T}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}_{U}}

commute.

Proof.

Note that
H_{T}
is closed, since
Lemma
3.2.16
.
(a)
implies
U\subseteq H_{S}

so that
T\smallsetminus H_{T}=f(S\smallsetminus H_{S})
is open.
Consider the natural transformation

(3.2.20)

\beta\colon\Inf^{T}_{S}\RInd_{H_{T}}^{T}\Longrightarrow\RInd_{H_{S}}^{S}\Inf^{H_{T}}_{H_{S}},

which arises as the right mate of the natural map
\Res^{S}_{H_{S}}\Inf^{T}_{S}\xRightarrow{\cong}\Inf^{H_{T}}_{H_{S}}\Res^{T}_{H_{T}}
by
Proposition
2.1.9
. It suffices to show that
\beta
is an
isomorphism, because then its mate
\mathrm{L}_{U}\Res^{S}_{H_{S}}\Longrightarrow\Res^{T}_{H_{T}}\mathrm{L}_{U}
is an isomorphism as well by Example
2.1.13
, and
hence both diagrams commute.

Fix an open profinite subgroup
K_{S}\subseteq S
so that
K_{T}\coloneqq f(K_{S})

is open in
T
. Since
\Res^{S}_{K_{S}}
is conservative by
Lemma
3.2.2
,
it suffices to show that
\Res^{S}_{K_{S}}(\beta)
is an isomorphism. We compute
the source and target of
\Res^{S}_{K_{S}}(\beta)
using the derived Mackey
decomposition, Lemma
3.2.15
, and the fact that

\Inf^{K_{T}}_{K_{S}}
preserves small products,
Theorem
3.2.3
:

\displaystyle\Res^{S}_{K_{S}}\Inf^{T}_{S}\RInd^{T}_{H_{T}}

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\cong}\Inf^{K_{T}}_{K_{S}}\Res^{T}_{K_{T}}\RInd^{T}_{H_{T}}

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\cong}\Inf^{K_{T}}_{K_{S}}\prod_{t\in H_{T}\backslash T/K_{T}}\Ind^{K_{T}}_{t^{-1}H_{T}t\cap K_{T}}t_{*}^{-1}\Res^{H_{T}}_{H_{T}\cap tK_{T}t^{-1}}

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\cong}\prod_{t\in H_{T}\backslash T/K_{T}}\Inf^{K_{T}}_{K_{S}}\Ind^{K_{T}}_{t^{-1}H_{T}t\cap K_{T}}t_{*}^{-1}\Res^{H_{T}}_{H_{T}\cap tK_{T}t^{-1}}

and

\displaystyle\Res^{S}_{K_{S}}\RInd_{H_{S}}^{S}\Inf^{H_{T}}_{H_{S}}

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\cong}\prod_{s\in H_{S}\backslash S/K_{S}}\Ind_{s^{-1}H_{S}s\cap K_{S}}^{K_{S}}s_{*}^{-1}\Res^{H_{S}}_{H_{S}\cap sK_{S}s^{-1}}\Inf^{H_{T}}_{H_{S}}

By Lemma
3.2.16
we may identify the double coset spaces

H_{S}\backslash S/K_{S}
and
H_{T}\backslash T/K_{T}
.

Let now
s\in S
and write
\overline{s}\coloneqq f(s)\in T
. By
Lemma
3.2.16
.
(a)
, we have
U\cap K_{S}\subseteq s^{-1}H_{S}s\cap K_{S}
, so that
f(s^{-1}H_{S}s\cap K_{S})=\overline{s}^{-1}H_{T}\overline{s}\cap K_{T}
. To shorten the notation, we write

\displaystyle\Psi_{s}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Ind^{K_{S}}_{s^{-1}H_{S}s\cap K_{S}}s_{*}^{-1}\Res^{H_{S}}_{H_{S}\cap sK_{S}s^{-1}}

and

\displaystyle\Psi_{\overline{s}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Ind^{K_{T}}_{\overline{s}^{-1}H_{T}\overline{s}\cap K_{T}}\overline{s}_{*}^{-1}\Res^{H_{T}}_{H_{T}\cap\overline{s}K_{T}\overline{s}^{-1}}.

It follows from Lemma
3.2.17
that the natural map

\beta_{s}\colon\Inf^{K_{T}}_{K_{S}}\Psi_{\overline{s}}\xRightarrow{\cong}\Psi_{s}\Inf^{H_{T}}_{H_{S}}

is an isomorphism. Let us verify that

\Res^{S}_{K_{S}}(\beta)
corresponds to
\prod_{s\in H_{S}\backslash S/K_{S}}\beta_{s}
under the above identifications; it then follows that

\Res^{S}_{K_{S}}(\beta)
is an isomorphism, which finishes the proof.

It remains to show that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{S}_{K_{S}}\Inf^{T}_{S}\RInd^{T}_{H_{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Inf^{K_{T}}_{K_{S}}\Res^{T}_{K_{T}}\RInd^{T}_{H_{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{s\in H_{S}\backslash S/K_{S}}\Inf^{K_{T}}_{K_{S}}\Psi_{\overline{s}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{S}_{K_{S}}\RInd_{H_{S}}^{S}\Inf^{H_{T}}_{H_{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{s\in H_{S}\backslash S/K_{S}}\Psi_{s}\Inf^{H_{T}}_{H_{S}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{S}_{K_{S}}(\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{s}\beta_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

commutes. The left vertical map
\gamma
is defined so as to make the triangle
commute. The commutativity of the square can be checked componentwise.
Therefore, it suffices to show that the diagram

(3.2.21)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Inf^{K_{T}}_{K_{S}}\bigl(\Res^{T}_{K_{T}}\RInd^{T}_{H_{T}}\bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Inf^{K_{T}}_{K_{S}}\Psi_{\overline{s}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigl(\Res^{S}_{K_{S}}\RInd^{S}_{H_{S}}\bigr)\Inf^{H_{T}}_{H_{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{s}\Inf^{H_{T}}_{H_{S}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Inf^{K_{T}}_{K_{S}}\rho_{\overline{s}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\rho_{s})_{\Inf^{H_{T}}_{H_{S}}}}

commutes, where
\rho_{\overline{s}}
and
\rho_{s}
are the natural maps coming from the
Mackey decomposition. The left mate of
\rho_{s}
is the map

\lambda_{s}\colon\Phi_{s}\coloneqq\ind_{H_{S}\cap sK_{S}s^{-1}}^{H_{S}}s_{*}\Res^{K_{S}}_{s^{-1}H_{S}s\cap K_{S}}\Longrightarrow\Res^{S}_{H_{S}}\ind_{K_{S}}^{S}

that is the
s
-th component of the isomorphism in the Mackey decomposition for
compact induction. The analogous statement holds for the left mate

\lambda_{\overline{s}}\colon\Phi_{\overline{s}}\coloneqq\ind_{H_{T}\cap\overline{s}K_{T}\overline{s}^{-1}}^{H_{T}}\overline{s}_{*}\Res^{K_{T}}_{\overline{s}^{-1}H_{T}\overline{s}\cap K_{T}}\Longrightarrow\Res^{T}_{H_{T}}\ind_{K_{T}}^{T}

of
\rho_{\overline{s}}
. By Example
2.1.12
the commutativity of
(
3.2.21
) is equivalent to the commutativity of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigl(\Res^{S}_{H_{S}}\ind_{K_{S}}^{S}\bigr)\Inf^{K_{T}}_{K_{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{s}\Inf^{K_{T}}_{K_{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Inf^{H_{T}}_{H_{S}}\bigl(\Res^{T}_{H_{T}}\ind_{K_{T}}^{T}\bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Inf^{H_{T}}_{H_{S}}\Phi_{\overline{s}},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\lambda_{s})_{\Inf^{K_{T}}_{K_{S}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Inf^{H_{T}}_{H_{S}}\lambda_{\overline{s}}}

where
\alpha
(resp.
\alpha_{s}
) is the left mate of
\gamma
(resp.
\beta_{s}
).
But this can be checked explicitly on the underived level, because
all functors involved are exact.
∎

Remark 3.2.22
.

Under the hypotheses of Proposition
3.2.19
it follows that the
natural map

\mathrm{R}\bigl(\Ind_{H_{S}}^{S}\Inf^{H_{T}}_{H_{S}}\bigr)\xRightarrow{\cong}\RInd_{H_{S}}^{S}\Inf^{H_{T}}_{H_{S}}

is an isomorphism. Thus, if
I\in\Rep_{k}(H_{T})
is injective, then

\Inf^{H_{T}}_{H_{S}}I
is acyclic for
\Ind_{H_{S}}^{S}
.

Remark 3.2.23
.

One can show the following analog of Proposition
3.2.19
: Let
K_{S}\subseteq S
be an open subgroup and
K_{T}\coloneqq f(K_{S})\subseteq T
. Assume
that
f
induces a bijection
S/K_{S}\xrightarrow{\cong}T/K_{T}
. Then the
diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(K_{T})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(K_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(K_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(K_{T})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Inf^{K_{T}}_{K_{S}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ind_{K_{T}}^{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ind_{K_{S}}^{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{S}_{K_{S}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{R}\Pi_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{T}_{K_{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Inf^{T}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{R}\Pi_{U}}

commute. Indeed, the bijection
S/K_{S}\xrightarrow{\cong}T/K_{T}
implies that
U

is a subgroup of
K_{S}
and that the natural map

k[S]\otimes_{k[K_{S}]}\Inf^{K_{T}}_{K_{S}}({\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\xRightarrow{\cong}\Inf^{T}_{S}\circ(k[T]\otimes_{k[K_{T}]}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})

is an isomorphism of functors
\Rep_{k}(K_{T})\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(S)
. As all functors
involved are exact, we deduce that the left diagram commutes. The commutativity
of the right diagram then follows by passing to the right adjoints, see
Example
2.1.13
.

Warning 3.2.24
.

Let
H_{S}\subseteq S
be a subgroup and put
H_{T}\coloneqq f(H_{S})
. Then

H_{S}\backslash S\xrightarrow[\cong]{f}H_{T}\backslash T
does
not
imply
(and is not implied by)
S/H_{S}\xrightarrow[\cong]{f}T/H_{T}
.

For example, this generally fails for the
p
-adic monoids considered in
§
3.4
. Let us make this explicit:
Consider the
p
-adic submonoid
S=P^{+}
of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})

consisting of upper triangular matrices
\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
0&d\end{pmatrix}
satisfying
\val_{p}(a)\geq\val_{p}(d)
and
\val_{p}(b)\geq\val_{p}(d)
, where

\val_{p}
denotes the
p
-adic valuation on
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. Let
T=M^{+}
be the
p
-adic
submonoid of
P^{+}
consisting of diagonal matrices. The canonical projection

f\colon P^{+}\longrightarrow\hskip-11.99998pt\rightarrow\hskip 1.99997ptM^{+}
(given by forgetting the upper right
entry) is surjective with kernel
U=\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathbb{Z}_{p}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
. Now,
P^{+}=UM^{+}
and
hence
U\backslash P^{+}\xrightarrow{\cong}M^{+}
via
f
. On the other hand,
for any
m=\diag(a,d)
in
M^{+}
with
\val_{p}(a)>\val_{p}(d)
we have
mUm^{-1}\subsetneqq U
, from which it follows that the map

P^{+}/U=UM^{+}/U\cong\bigsqcup_{m\in M^{+}}U/mUm^{-1}\longrightarrow\hskip-11.99998pt\rightarrow\hskip 1.99997pt\bigsqcup_{m\in M^{+}}\{m\}=M^{+}

is (surjective but) not injective.

Corollary 3.2.25
.

Assume that
U
is a group and that
f
induces a bijection

U\backslash S\xrightarrow{\cong}T
. There is a natural isomorphism

\Hom_{\mathrm{D}(k)}\bigl(\Res^{T}_{1}(\mathrm{L}_{U}X),Y\bigr)\cong\Hom_{\mathrm{D}(U)}\bigl(\Res^{S}_{U}X,\Inf^{1}_{U}Y\bigr),

for all
X\in\mathrm{D}(S)
and
Y\in\mathrm{D}(k)
.
In particular, whenever
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
V\in\Rep_{k}(S)
, and
W\in\mathrm{Vect}_{k}
, there
is a natural isomorphism

\Hom_{k}\bigl(\mathrm{L}^{-n}_{U}(V),W\bigr)\cong\Ext^{n}_{U}(V,W).

Proof.

Applying Proposition
3.2.19
with
H_{S}=U
and
H_{T}=\{1\}
and
Theorem
3.2.3
yields natural isomorphisms

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathrm{D}(k)}\bigl(\Res^{T}_{1}(\mathrm{L}_{U}X),Y\bigr)

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathrm{D}(k)}\bigl(\mathrm{L}_{U}(\Res^{S}_{U}X),Y\bigr)

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathrm{D}(U)}\bigl(\Res^{S}_{U}X,\Inf^{1}_{U}Y\bigr),

for each
X\in\mathrm{D}(S)
and
Y\in\mathrm{D}(k)
. The last statement follows from the
first by taking
X=V[0]
and
Y=W[n]
for
V\in\Rep_{k}(S)
and
W\in\mathrm{Vect}_{k}
.
∎

Corollary 3.2.26
.

Retain the hypotheses of Proposition
3.2.19
and suppose that
H_{S}
and

H_{T}
are compact, open, and torsion-free. Then
\mathrm{L}_{U}\colon\mathrm{D}(S)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(T)

preserves small products and, in particular, is also a right adjoint.

Proof.

Note that Lemma
3.2.16
implies that
U\subseteq H_{S}
is compact
and torsion-free.
Let
I
be a set, and take
X_{i}\in\mathrm{D}(S)
, for
i\in I
. We show that the
natural map

\alpha\colon\mathrm{L}_{U}\prod_{i\in I}X_{i}\longrightarrow\prod_{i\in I}\mathrm{L}_{U}(X_{i})

is an isomorphism in
\mathrm{D}(T)
. By Proposition
3.2.19
there is a
natural isomorphism

\Res^{T}_{H_{T}}\mathrm{L}_{U}\xRightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{L}_{U}\Res^{S}_{H_{S}}.

Now observe that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{T}_{H_{T}}\mathrm{L}_{U}\prod_{i\in I}X_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}_{U}\prod_{i\in I}\Res^{S}_{H_{S}}X_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{T}_{H_{T}}\prod_{i\in I}\mathrm{L}_{U}X_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i\in I}\mathrm{L}_{U}\Res^{S}_{H_{S}}X_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{T}_{H_{T}}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

is commutative, where
\alpha^{\prime}
is the canonical map.
Since
\Res^{S}_{H_{S}}
and
\Res^{T}_{H_{T}}
are conservative and preserve small
products, by Lemma
3.2.2
, it suffices to verify that the
natural map

\alpha^{\prime}\colon\mathrm{L}_{U}\prod_{i\in I}\Res^{S}_{H_{S}}(X_{i})\longrightarrow\prod_{i\in I}\mathrm{L}_{U}\bigl(\Res^{S}_{H_{S}}X_{i}\bigr)

is an isomorphism in
\mathrm{D}(H_{T})
. But this follows from the fact that
\mathrm{L}_{U}\colon\mathrm{D}(H_{S})\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(H_{T})
admits a left adjoint (Corollary
3.1.11
), which
finishes the proof.
∎

3.3.
The case of
p
-adic Lie groups

We fix a continuous surjection
f\colon P\longrightarrow\hskip-11.99998pt\rightarrow\hskip 1.99997ptM
of
p
-adic
Lie groups with kernel
U
.

The crucial result we need is the following:

Lemma 3.3.1
.

Let
G
be a locally profinite group and
K\subseteq G
an open subgroup. The
natural map

(3.3.2)

\Res^{G}_{K}\bigl(\hom_{\mathrm{D}(G)}(X,Y)\bigr)\xrightarrow{\cong}\hom_{\mathrm{D}(K)}\bigl(\Res^{G}_{K}X,\Res^{G}_{K}Y\bigr)

is an isomorphism, for all
X,Y\in\mathrm{D}(G)
.

Proof.

The map (
3.3.2
) is an isomorphism on cohomology by
(
2.3.17
), hence an isomorphism.
∎

Proposition 3.3.3
.

For all
X,Y\in\mathrm{D}(M)
the natural map

\alpha_{X,Y}\colon\Inf^{M}_{P}\hom_{\mathrm{D}(M)}(X,Y)\xrightarrow{\cong}\hom_{\mathrm{D}(P)}\bigl(\Inf^{M}_{P}X,\Inf^{M}_{P}Y\bigr)

is an isomorphism in
\mathrm{D}(P)
.

Proof.

The natural transformation
\alpha_{X,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}}\colon\Inf^{M}_{P}\circ\hom_{\mathrm{D}(M)}(X,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\Longrightarrow\hom_{D(P)}(\Inf^{M}_{P}X,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\circ\Inf^{M}_{P}
arises as the mate of the canonical
isomorphism

(\Inf^{M}_{P}X\otimes_{k}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\circ\Inf^{M}_{P}\xRightarrow{\cong}\Inf^{M}_{P}\circ(X\otimes_{k}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}),

see Proposition
2.1.9
.

Let
K_{P}\subseteq P
be a compact open subgroup such that
K_{M}\coloneqq f(K_{P})
is torsion-free. By Lemma
3.2.2
the functor

\Res^{P}_{K_{P}}
is conservative.
Therefore, it suffices to show that
\Res^{P}_{K_{P}}(\alpha_{X,Y})=\alpha_{\Res^{M}_{K_{M}}\!X,\Res^{M}_{K_{M}}\!\!Y}
is an isomorphism in
\mathrm{D}(K_{P})
.

Thus, we may assume from the beginning that
P
is compact and
M
is
torsion-free and compact. Let

\mathscr{T}
be the full subcategory of
\mathrm{D}(M)
with objects those
X\in\mathrm{D}(M)

for which
\alpha_{X,Y}
is an isomorphism for all
Y\in\mathrm{D}(M)
. It is clear
that
\mathscr{T}
is triangulated. Since
\Inf^{M}_{P}
preserves small products, by
Theorem
3.2.3
, and small coproducts, it easily
follows that
\mathscr{T}
is localizing,
i.e.
, closed under small coproducts. Now,

\hom_{\mathrm{D}(M)}(\mathbf{1},Y)\cong Y
and
\hom_{\mathrm{D}(P)}(\Inf^{M}_{P}\mathbf{1},\Inf^{M}_{P}Y)\cong\Inf^{M}_{P}Y
. Under these identifications we have
\alpha_{\mathbf{1},Y}=\id_{\Inf^{M}_{P}Y}
, which shows
\mathbf{1}\in\mathscr{T}
. By
Proposition
2.3.8
,
\mathrm{D}(M)
is compactly generated by

\mathbf{1}
. This implies
\mathscr{T}=\mathrm{D}(M)
, which proves the assertion.
∎

Warning 3.3.4
.

Despite the result in Proposition
3.3.3
, the functor
\Inf^{M}_{P}
is
in general
not
fully faithful. For example, the counit
\mathrm{L}_{U}(\mathbf{1})=\mathrm{L}_{U}(\Inf^{M}_{P}\mathbf{1})\longrightarrow\mathbf{1}
is not an isomorphism by
Corollary
3.1.7
provided
P
and
M
are compact and torsion-free.

Corollary 3.3.5

(Projection formula)
.

For all
X\in\mathrm{D}(P)
and
Y\in\mathrm{D}(M)
the natural map

\mathrm{L}_{U}\bigl(\Inf^{M}_{P}X\otimes_{k}Y\bigr)\xrightarrow{\cong}X\otimes_{k}\mathrm{L}_{U}(Y)

is an isomorphism in
\mathrm{D}(M)
. In particular,
\mathrm{L}_{U}\Inf^{M}_{P}Y\cong\mathrm{L}_{U}(\mathbf{1})\otimes_{k}Y
for all
Y\in\mathrm{D}(M)
.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.3.3
the natural map

\Inf^{M}_{P}\circ\hom_{\mathrm{D}(M)}(Y,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\xRightarrow{\cong}\hom_{\mathrm{D}(P)}(\Inf^{M}_{P}Y,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\circ\Inf^{M}_{P}

is an isomorphism of functors
\mathrm{D}(M)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(P)
. Passing to the left adjoints,
see Example
2.1.13
, shows that the induced map

\mathrm{L}_{U}\circ(\Inf^{M}_{P}Y\otimes_{k}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\xRightarrow{\cong}(Y\otimes_{k}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\circ\mathrm{L}_{U}

is an isomorphism of functors
\mathrm{D}(P)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(M)
.
∎

Proposition 3.3.6
.

Given
X\in\mathrm{D}(P)
and
Y\in\mathrm{D}(M)
, the natural map

\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}\bigl(U,\hom_{\mathrm{D}(P)}(X,\Inf^{M}_{P}Y)\bigr)\xrightarrow{\cong}\hom_{\mathrm{D}(M)}(\mathrm{L}_{U}X,Y)

is an isomorphism in
\mathrm{D}(M)
.

Proof.

The natural map

\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\circ\hom_{\mathrm{D}(P)}(X,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\circ\Inf^{M}_{P}\Longrightarrow\hom_{\mathrm{D}(M)}\bigl(\mathrm{L}_{U}(X),{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}\bigr)

arises from
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}\otimes_{k}\mathrm{L}_{U}(X)\xRightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{L}_{U}\circ({\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}\otimes_{k}X)\circ\Inf^{M}_{P}
by passing to the left adjoints, see Example
2.1.13
. The
latter map is an isomorphism by Corollary
3.3.5
, hence so
is the first.
∎

Recall that we denote by
X^{\vee}=\hom(X,\mathbf{1})
the dual object, see
Notation
2.3.18
.

Corollary 3.3.7
.

There is a natural isomorphism
\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(U,X^{\vee})\cong(\mathrm{L}_{U}X)^{\vee}
for all

X\in\mathrm{D}(P)
.

Proof.

Apply Proposition
3.3.6
with
Y=\mathbf{1}
.
∎

3.4.
Computing the left adjoint using positive
monoids

We suppose now that
P=U\rtimes M
is a semidirect product of
p
-adic Lie
groups. Let
f\colon P\longrightarrow\hskip-11.99998pt\rightarrow\hskip 1.99997ptM
be the canonical projection. We
fix a compact open torsion-free subgroup
K_{P}
of
P
. As before, we write
K_{U}=K_{P}\cap U
and
K_{M}=f(K_{P})
. We make the following hypothesis:

Hypothesis 3.4.1
.

There exists a
strictly positive
element
z\in M
, i.e., an element
z
in
the center of
M
such that
zK_{U}z^{-1}\subseteq K_{U}
and

\bigcup_{n>0}z^{-n}K_{U}z^{n}=U.

We fix a strictly positive element
z\in M
.
Note that the existence of a strictly positive element necessitates that
U
be
the union of its compact open subgroups.

Example 3.4.2
.

The example one should have in mind is the following:
Let
\mathfrak{F}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
be a finite extension and
\mathbf{G}
a connected reductive group
defined over
\mathfrak{F}
. Fix a parabolic subgroup
\mathbf{P}
of
\mathbf{G}
with
unipotent radical
\mathbf{U}
. Let
\overline{\mathbf{P}}
be the opposite parabolic subgroup
with respect to
\mathbf{P}
, that is, the unique parabolic subgroup
\overline{\mathbf{P}}

of
\mathbf{G}
such that
\mathbf{M}\coloneqq\mathbf{P}\cap\overline{\mathbf{P}}
is a Levi
subgroup for
\mathbf{P}
and
\overline{\mathbf{P}}
. Denote
\overline{\mathbf{U}}
the unipotent
radical of
\overline{\mathbf{P}}
. Let
K\subseteq\mathbf{G}(\mathfrak{F})
be a
compact open subgroup with an Iwahori decomposition

K=(K\cap\mathbf{U}(\mathfrak{F}))(K\cap\mathbf{M}(\mathfrak{F}))(K\cap\overline{\mathbf{U}}(\mathfrak{F})).

By
[
BK98
, (6.14)]
there
exists a strongly positive element for the choice
K_{P}\coloneqq(K\cap\mathbf{U}(\mathfrak{F}))(K\cap\mathbf{M}(\mathfrak{F}))
. Note that the strongly positive elements in
the sense of
op. cit.
are strictly positive in our sense.

The following definition is standard, cf.
[
BK98
, (6.5)]
,

[
Vig98
, II.4]
, or
[
Eme10a
, §3.1]
.

Definition
.

An element
m\in M
is called
positive
if
mK_{U}m^{-1}\subseteq K_{U}
. We
denote
M^{+}
the monoid of positive elements in
M
. Note that
M^{+}
is a

p
-adic monoid, since
K_{P}\cap M\subseteq M^{+}
.

The
p
-adic monoid
P^{+}\coloneqq K_{U}M^{+}
satisfies
K_{U}\backslash P^{+}\cong M^{+}
.

Remark 3.4.3
.

We allow for
U
to be compact. In this case, Hypothesis
3.4.1

amounts to saying that
U=K_{U}
is torsion-free and
M=M^{+}
.

Our goal in this section is to give a more precise description of the functor

\mathrm{L}_{U}
using positive monoids. We first collect some elementary facts.

Lemma 3.4.4
.

The following assertions hold:

(a)

M
(resp.
P
) is generated as a monoid by
M^{+}
and

z^{-1}
(resp.
P^{+}
and
z^{-1}
).

(b)

Let
S=\{z^{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
be the multiplicative set
generated by
z
. Then
k[M]=S^{-1}k[M^{+}]
is the left Ore localization of

k[M^{+}]
at
S
. In particular, the ring extension
k[M^{+}]\subseteq k[M]
is flat.

Similarly,
k[P]=S^{-1}k[P^{+}]
is the left Ore localization of
k[P^{+}]
at
S
,
and hence the ring extension
k[P^{+}]\subseteq k[P]
is flat.

(c)

The functors
\ind_{M^{+}}^{M}\colon\Rep_{k}(M^{+})\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(M)
and
\ind_{P^{+}}^{P}\colon\Rep_{k}(P^{+})\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(P)
are exact.

Proof.

(a)

Let
m\in M
. Since
mK_{U}m^{-1}
is a compact
subgroup of
U=\bigcup_{n>0}z^{-n}K_{U}z^{n}
, there exists
n
such that

mK_{U}m^{-1}\subseteq z^{-n}K_{U}z^{n}
. Then
z^{n}m\in M^{+}
, proving the
assertion for
M
. Similarly, let
u\in U
and
m\in M
be arbitrary. Let

n\gg 0
such that
u\in z^{-n}K_{U}z^{n}
and
z^{n}m\in M^{+}
. Then
z^{n}um=z^{n}uz^{-n}\cdot z^{n}m\in P^{+}
, proving the assertion for
P
.

(b)

We prove the assertion for
k[P^{+}]
; the case of

k[M^{+}]
is similar but easier. Note that
k[P^{+}]
embeds into the bigger
ring
k[P]
in which the elements of
S
are invertible. Moreover,
S
satisfies
the left Ore condition, meaning that for all
s\in S
and

r\in k[P^{+}]
there exist
s^{\prime}\in S
and
r^{\prime}\in k[P^{+}]
such that
s^{\prime}r=r^{\prime}s
;
indeed this is clear for
s^{\prime}\coloneqq s
and
r^{\prime}\coloneqq srs^{-1}\in k[P^{+}]
.
Now,
[
MR01
, Lem. 2.1.13.(ii)]
shows that
S
is a left
denominator set in
k[P^{+}]
.
Then
(a)
implies that
k[P]
is
the left Ore localization of
k[P^{+}]
with respect to
S
. The
flatness assertion follows from
op. cit.
Proposition 2.1.16.

(c)

This follows immediately from
(b)
.

∎

Recall that
\Res^{P}_{P^{+}}\colon\mathrm{D}(P)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(P^{+})
admits a left adjoint

\ind_{P^{+}}^{P}
(by Lemma
3.4.4
.
(c)
) and a right
adjoint
\RInd_{P^{+}}^{P}
.

Lemma 3.4.5
.

The following equivalent assertions hold:

(i)

The unit
\eta\colon\id_{\mathrm{D}(P)}\xRightarrow{\cong}\RInd_{P^{+}}^{P}\Res^{P}_{P^{+}}
is an isomorphism.

(ii)

\Res^{P}_{P^{+}}\colon\mathrm{D}(P)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(P^{+})
is fully
faithful.

(iii)

The counit
\varepsilon\colon\ind_{P^{+}}^{P}\Res^{P}_{P^{+}}\xRightarrow{\cong}\id_{\mathrm{D}(P)}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

The fact that
(i)
,
(ii)
, and

(iii)
are equivalent follows from
Lemma
2.1.5
. Since both
\ind_{P^{+}}^{P}
and

\Res^{P}_{P^{+}}
are exact, it suffices for
(iii)
to show that the
counit

\varepsilon\colon\ind_{P^{+}}^{P}\Res^{P}_{P^{+}}\Longrightarrow\id_{\Rep_{k}(P)}

of the underived adjunction is an isomorphism. But this follows from the fact
that
\ind_{P^{+}}^{P}=k[P]\otimes_{k[P^{+}]}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}
is a localization functor by
Lemma
3.4.4
.
(b)
.
∎

By Theorem
3.2.3
the functor
\Inf^{M^{+}}_{P^{+}}\colon\mathrm{D}(M^{+})\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(P^{+})
admits a left adjoint
\mathrm{L}_{K_{U}}
.

Proposition 3.4.6
.

There is a natural isomorphism

\ind_{M^{+}}^{M}\mathrm{L}_{K_{U}}\Res^{P}_{P^{+}}\xRightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{L}_{U}

of functors
\mathrm{D}(P)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(M)
.

Proof.

The functor
\ind_{M^{+}}^{M}\colon\mathrm{D}(M^{+})\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(M)
exists by
Lemma
3.4.4
.
(c)
and is left adjoint to

\Res^{M}_{M^{+}}
. From Lemma
3.4.5
it follows that the composite

\Inf^{M}_{P}\xRightarrow[\eta]{\cong}\RInd_{P^{+}}^{P}\Res^{P}_{P^{+}}\Inf^{M}_{P}\xRightarrow{\cong}\RInd_{P^{+}}^{P}\Inf^{M^{+}}_{P^{+}}\Res^{M}_{M^{+}}

is a natural isomorphism. The assertion follows by passing to the left adjoints,
cf. Example
2.1.13
.
∎

Corollary 3.4.7
.

The functors
\mathrm{L}^{-n}_{U}\colon\Rep_{k}(P)\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(M)
vanish for

n\notin\{0,1,\dotsc,\dim U\}
. In particular,
\mathrm{L}_{U}
preserves bounded
complexes.

Proof.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}\smallsetminus\{0,1,\dotsc,\dim U\}
. Since
\dim K_{U}=\dim U
,
Corollary
3.1.8
and Proposition
3.1.10
imply

\mathrm{L}_{K_{U}}^{-n}=\mathrm{H}^{\dim K_{U}-n}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})=0.

Now, Proposition
3.4.6
shows
\mathrm{L}^{-n}_{U}=\ind_{M^{+}}^{M}\mathrm{L}^{-n}_{K_{U}}\Res^{P}_{P^{+}}=0
.
∎

Corollary 3.4.8
.

The functors
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}_{U}\colon\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(M):\!\Inf^{M}_{P}}
define an
adjunction on the derived categories of bounded complexes.

Proof.

This is immediate from Corollary
3.4.7
.
∎

We finish this section by giving another description of
\mathrm{L}_{K_{U}}\colon\mathrm{D}(P^{+})\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(M^{+})
similar to Corollary
3.1.8
. To start, let

V\in\Rep_{k}(P^{+})
be a smooth
P^{+}
-representation. There is an action of
M^{+}

on
\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},V)
called the
Hecke action
, cf.

[
Eme10a
, 3.1.3 Def.]
. Concretely,
m\in M^{+}
acts on
\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},V)
as the
composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{0}(mK_{U}m^{-1},V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},V),}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces mv}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\star v\coloneqq\sum_{u\in K_{U}/mK_{U}m^{-1}}umv;}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\conj_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cores}

note that the restriction of the Hecke
M^{+}
-action to
K_{M}
gives the usual
action.
We obtain a left exact functor
\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\colon\Rep_{k}(P^{+})\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(M^{+})
,
hence also a right derived functor

(3.4.9)

\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\colon\mathrm{D}(P^{+})\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(M^{+}).

Warning 3.4.10
.

The functor (
3.4.9
) is
not
the right adjoint of

\Inf^{M^{+}}_{P^{+}}
, see also Remark
2.2.14
.

The following lemma resolves the ambiguity in the notation of
\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
:

Lemma 3.4.11
.

The diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(P^{+})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(M^{+})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(K_{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(K_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(K_{U})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(k)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{P^{+}}_{K_{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{M^{+}}_{K_{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{K_{P}}_{K_{U}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{K_{M}}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}}})}

is commutative.

Proof.

Observe that
\Res^{P^{+}}_{K_{P}}
admits an exact left adjoint, by
Lemma
3.4.4
.
(c)
and hence preserves K-injective
complexes, see Lemma
2.3.3
.
(a)
. Thus, the
commutativity of the top square can be checked on the underived level, where it
is clear.

Since
\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\colon\Rep_{k}(K_{P})\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(K_{M})
and

\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\colon\Rep_{k}(K_{U})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Vect}_{k}
have finite cohomological
dimension,
[
Har66
, Cor. 5.3.
\gamma
]
shows that both derived
functors can be computed using acyclic resolutions. As
\Res^{K_{P}}_{K_{U}}

preserves
\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
-acyclic objects, see,
e.g.
,
[
NSW13
, (1.3.6)
Prop. (ii)]
, it follows that also the bottom square commutes.
∎

Lemma 3.4.12
.

The functor (
3.4.9
) admits a right adjoint
F^{M^{+}}_{P^{+}}\colon\mathrm{D}(M^{+})\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(P^{+})
.

Proof.

By Brown representability, Corollary
2.3.10
, it suffices to show that

\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
commutes with small direct sums. So let
\{Y_{i}\}_{i\in I}
in

\mathrm{D}(P^{+})
be a family. The inclusions
Y_{j}\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{i\in I}Y_{i}
induce a
canonical map

\alpha\colon\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},Y_{i})\longrightarrow\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}\Bigl(K_{U},\bigoplus_{i\in I}Y_{i}\Bigr).

By Lemma
3.4.11
, and because
\Res^{M^{+}}_{K_{M}}
and
\Res^{P^{+}}_{K_{P}}

commute with small direct sums, we obtain a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{M^{+}}_{K_{M}}\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},Y_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{M^{+}}_{K_{M}}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}\bigl(K_{U},\bigoplus_{i\in I}Y_{i}\bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}\bigl(K_{U},\Res^{P^{+}}_{K_{P}}Y_{i}\bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}\bigl(K_{U},\bigoplus_{i\in I}\Res^{P^{+}}_{K_{P}}Y_{i}\bigr).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{M^{+}}_{K_{M}}\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

The lower horizontal map is an isomorphism, since
\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\colon\mathrm{D}(K_{P})\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(K_{M})
admits a right adjoint by Lemma
3.1.3
. Therefore,

\Res^{M^{+}}_{K_{M}}\alpha
is an isomorphism. As
\Res^{M^{+}}_{K_{M}}
is
conservative by Lemma
3.2.2
, it follows that
\alpha
is an
isomorphism.
∎

Lemma 3.4.13
.

The diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(M^{+})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(P^{+})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(K_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(K_{P})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{M^{+}}_{P^{+}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{M^{+}}_{K_{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{P^{+}}_{K_{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}}

is commutative.

Proof.

Lemma
3.4.11
gives a natural isomorphism
\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\Res^{P^{+}}_{K_{P}}\xRightarrow{\cong}\Res^{M^{+}}_{K_{M}}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
. Passing
to the left mate, see Proposition
2.1.9
, we obtain a natural
transformation

(3.4.14)

\alpha\colon\ind_{K_{M}}^{M^{+}}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\Longrightarrow\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\ind_{K_{P}}^{P^{+}}.

We claim that
\alpha
is an isomorphism. Since
\Res^{M^{+}}_{1}
is
conservative, it suffices to check that

(3.4.15)

\Res^{M^{+}}_{1}\ind_{K_{M}}^{M^{+}}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\xRightarrow{\Res^{M^{+}}_{1}\alpha}\Res^{M^{+}}_{1}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\ind_{K_{P}}^{P^{+}}\xRightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\Res^{P^{+}}_{K_{U}}\ind_{K_{P}}^{P^{+}}

is an isomorphism.
Since
P^{+}=K_{U}M^{+}
, the inclusion
M^{+}\subseteq P^{+}
induces a
bijection
M^{+}/K_{M}\cong K_{U}\backslash P^{+}/K_{P}
. Let
\Gamma\subseteq M^{+}
be a complete representing system for
K_{U}\backslash P^{+}/K_{P}
. By
the Mackey decomposition
3.2.15
, we have an isomorphism

(3.4.16)

\bigoplus_{m\in\Gamma}\ind_{mK_{U}m^{-1}}^{K_{U}}\Res^{mK_{P}m^{-1}}_{mK_{U}m^{-1}}m_{*}\xRightarrow{\cong}\Res^{P^{+}}_{K_{U}}\ind_{K_{P}}^{P^{+}}

of functors
\mathrm{D}(K_{P})\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(K_{U})
, where we have used
K_{U}\cap mK_{P}m^{-1}=mK_{U}m^{-1}
. Similarly, we have
\bigoplus_{m\in\Gamma}\Res^{mK_{M}m^{-1}}_{1}m_{*}\xRightarrow{\cong}\Res^{M^{+}}_{1}\ind_{K_{M}}^{M^{+}}
.
Since
\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
has finite cohomological dimension, it can be computed by

\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
-acyclic complexes, see
[
Har66
, Cor. 5.3
\gamma
]
.
Now, each of the functors
m_{*}
,
\Res^{mK_{P}m^{-1}}_{mK_{U}m^{-1}}
, and

\ind_{mK_{U}m^{-1}}^{K_{U}}
preserves injective objects, since each admits an
exact left adjoint. As every
\mathrm{H}^{i}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
commutes with small direct sums by
Lemma
3.4.12
, it follows from (
3.4.16
) that

\Res^{P^{+}}_{K_{U}}\ind_{K_{P}}^{P^{+}}
sends injective objects to

\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
-acyclic objects. It follows that (
3.4.15
) arises from
the natural map

\displaystyle\beta\colon\ind_{K_{M}}^{M^{+}}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},V)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{0}\bigl(K_{U},\ind_{K_{P}}^{P^{+}}V\bigr),

\displaystyle[m,v]

\displaystyle\longmapsto m\star[1,v]=\sum_{u\in K_{U}/mK_{U}m^{-1}}[um,v]

by passing to the right derived functors. Thus, it suffices to show that
\beta

is an isomorphism. Using the Mackey decomposition, we have a commutative diagram

(3.4.17)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ind_{K_{M}}^{M^{+}}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{0}\bigl(K_{U},\ind_{K_{P}}^{P^{+}}V\bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{m\in\Gamma}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{m\in\Gamma}\mathrm{H}^{0}\bigl(K_{U},\ind_{mK_{U}m^{-1}}^{K_{U}}m_{*}V\bigr)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus_{m}\beta_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

of
k
-linear maps, where each
\beta_{m}\colon\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},V)\longrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},\ind_{mK_{U}m^{-1}}^{K_{U}}m_{*}V)
is defined by

\beta_{m}(v)\coloneqq\sum_{u\in K_{U}/mK_{U}m^{-1}}[u,v].

Now, it is easy to check that the map induced by
f\mapsto f(1)
is the inverse
of
\beta_{m}
, hence
\beta_{m}
is an isomorphism. We deduce from
(
3.4.17
) that
\beta
and then that
\alpha
is an isomorphism.
Finally, passing to the right adjoints in (
3.4.14
), we obtain
the natural isomorphism
\Res^{P^{+}}_{K_{P}}F^{M^{+}}_{P^{+}}\xRightarrow{\cong}F^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}\Res^{M^{+}}_{K_{M}}
.
∎

Proposition 3.4.18
.

There is a natural isomorphism

F^{M^{+}}_{P^{+}}(X)\otimes_{k}\Inf^{M^{+}}_{P^{+}}(Y)\xRightarrow{\cong}F^{M^{+}}_{P^{+}}(X\otimes Y).

In particular,
F^{M^{+}}_{P^{+}}\cong\omega_{P^{+}}\otimes_{k}\Inf^{M^{+}}_{P^{+}}
,
where
\omega_{P^{+}}\coloneqq F^{M^{+}}_{P^{+}}(\mathbf{1})
is a smooth character of

P^{+}
sitting in cohomological degree
\dim K_{U}
.

Proof.

Note that, for
V\in\Rep_{k}(P^{+})
and
W\in\Rep_{k}(M^{+})
the natural map

(3.4.19)

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},V)\otimes_{k}W

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{0}\bigl(K_{U},V\otimes_{k}\Inf^{M^{+}}_{P^{+}}W\bigr),

\displaystyle v\otimes w

\displaystyle\longmapsto v\otimes w

is an isomorphism in
\Rep_{k}(M^{+})
. Indeed, bijectivity is clear, and for every

v\in\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},V)
,
w\in W
, and
m\in M^{+}
we have

m\star v\otimes mw=\sum_{u\in K_{U}/mK_{U}m^{-1}}umv\otimes mw=\sum_{u\in K_{U}/mK_{U}m^{-1}}um(v\otimes w)=m\star(v\otimes w)
, which shows that (
3.4.19
) is
M^{+}
-equivariant.

The map (
3.4.19
) extends to a natural isomorphism

(3.4.20)

\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},X)\otimes_{k}Y\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{H}^{0}\bigl(K_{U},X\otimes_{k}\Inf^{M^{+}}_{P^{+}}Y\bigr)

of bifunctors
\mathrm{K}(P^{+})\times\mathrm{K}(M^{+})\longrightarrow\mathrm{K}(M^{+})
. For any
Y\in\mathrm{D}(M^{+})
we
obtain a natural transformation

\displaystyle\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\otimes_{k}Y

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{R}\bigl(\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})({\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}\otimes_{k}\Inf^{M^{+}}_{P^{+}}Y)\bigr)

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\;\alpha}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\circ({\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}\otimes_{k}\Inf^{M^{+}}_{P^{+}}Y).

We claim that the map denoted
\alpha
is an isomorphism. Recall (
e.g.
, from the
proof of Lemma
3.4.11
) that

\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
can be computed using
\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
-acyclic resolutions. Note
that, if
V\in\Rep_{k}(P^{+})
is
\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
-acyclic and
W\in\Rep_{k}(M^{+})
is
arbitrary, then
V\otimes_{k}\Inf^{M^{+}}_{P^{+}}W
is
\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
-acyclic: indeed,
by Lemma
3.4.11
this can be checked after applying
\Res^{P^{+}}_{K_{U}}
,
in which case we may identify
\Res^{P^{+}}_{K_{U}}(V\otimes_{k}\Inf^{M^{+}}_{P^{+}}W)\cong V^{\oplus I}
, where
I
parametrizes a
k
-basis of
W
, and then the
claim follows from the fact that each
\mathrm{H}^{i}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
commutes with small direct
sums (Lemma
3.4.12
). It follows that if
X\in\mathrm{K}(P^{+})
is a complex
consisting of
\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
-acyclic objects and
Y\in\mathrm{K}(M^{+})
is arbitrary,
then
X\otimes_{k}\Inf^{M^{+}}_{P^{+}}Y
consists of
\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
-acyclic objects.
We deduce that
\alpha
is an isomorphism, cf.
[
KS06
, Cor. 14.3.5]
.

Thus, we have constructed a natural isomorphism
