\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\circ({\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}\otimes_{k}\Inf^{M^{+}}_{P^{+}}Y)\xLeftarrow{\cong}({\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}\otimes_{k}Y)\circ\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}).

Passing to the right mate yields a natural map

\beta\colon F^{M^{+}}_{P^{+}}X\otimes_{k}\Inf^{M^{+}}_{P^{+}}Y\Longrightarrow F^{M^{+}}_{P^{+}}(X\otimes Y)
. Now, we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{P^{+}}_{K_{P}}\bigl(F^{M^{+}}_{P^{+}}X\otimes_{k}\Inf^{M^{+}}_{P^{+}}Y\bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{P^{+}}_{K_{P}}\bigl(F^{M^{+}}_{P^{+}}(X\otimes Y)\bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}\Res^{M^{+}}_{K_{M}}X\otimes_{k}\Inf^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}\Res^{M^{+}}_{K_{M}}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}\bigl(\Res^{M^{+}}_{K_{M}}X\otimes_{k}\Res^{M^{+}}_{K_{M}}Y\bigr),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{P^{+}}_{K_{P}}\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

where the vertical maps are isomorphisms by Lemma
3.4.13
and the
bottom horizontal map is the isomorphism from Corollary
3.1.7
.
Hence, the top horizontal map is an isomorphism. As
\Res^{P^{+}}_{K_{P}}
is
conservative by Lemma
3.2.2
, we deduce that
\beta
is an
isomorphism.

Finally, Lemma
3.4.13
and Proposition
3.1.10

show
\Res^{P^{+}}_{K_{P}}(\omega_{P^{+}})=\omega_{K_{P}}=k[\dim K_{U}]
, which proves
the last statement.
∎

Corollary 3.4.21
.

There is a natural isomorphism
\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},\omega_{P^{+}}\otimes_{k}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\xRightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{L}_{K_{U}}
of functors
\mathrm{D}(P^{+})\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(M^{+})
.

Proof.

The natural map in the assertion, say,
\alpha
arises, by passing to the left
mate, from the natural isomorphism
(\omega_{P^{+}}\otimes_{k}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\Inf^{M^{+}}_{P^{+}}\xRightarrow{\cong}F^{M^{+}}_{P^{+}}
. We then have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{M^{+}}_{K_{M}}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},\omega_{P^{+}}\otimes_{k}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{M^{+}}_{K_{M}}\mathrm{L}_{K_{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},\omega_{K_{P}}\otimes_{k}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\Res^{P^{+}}_{K_{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}_{K_{U}}\Res^{P^{+}}_{K_{P}},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{M^{+}}_{K_{M}}\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

where the left vertical map is an isomorphism by Lemmas
3.4.11

and
3.4.13
, the right vertical map is an isomorphism by
Proposition
3.2.19
, and the bottom horizontal map is an isomorphism
by Corollary
3.1.8
. Hence,
\Res^{M^{+}}_{K_{M}}\alpha
is an
isomorphism. As
\Res^{M^{+}}_{K_{M}}
is conservative by
Lemma
3.2.2
, it follows that
\alpha
is an isomorphism.
∎

Remark 3.4.22
.

One can show
\omega_{P^{+}}=\Res^{P}_{P^{+}}\omega_{P}
, where
\omega_{P}=\delta_{P}[\dim U]
and
\delta_{P}
is a smooth character of
P
(which is
necessarily trivial on
U
). Hence,
\omega_{P^{+}}
is in fact

\otimes
-invertible. (Note that this fails for general characters of
monoids.)

In fact,
\delta_{P}
is the (inflation to
P
of the) character
\alpha^{u}

defined in
[
Eme10b
, 3.6]
.

Corollary 3.4.23
.

One has a natural isomorphism

\ind_{M^{+}}^{M}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},\omega_{P^{+}}\otimes_{k}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\Res^{P}_{P^{+}}\xRightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{L}_{U}

of functors
\mathrm{D}(P)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(M)
.

Corollary 3.4.24
.

Let
f\colon P\longrightarrow\hskip-11.99998pt\rightarrow\hskip 1.99997ptM
be a continuous surjection of
p
-adic
Lie groups with kernel
U
, and let
K_{U}\subseteq U
be a torsion-free compact
open subgroup.
Assume there is an automorphism
\varphi\colon U\xrightarrow{\cong}U
of
topological groups such that
\bigcap_{n\geq 0}\varphi^{n}(K_{U})=\{0\}
and

\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\varphi^{-n}(K_{U})=U
. The following equivalent properties
are satisfied:

(i)

\Inf^{M}_{P}\colon\mathrm{D}(M)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(P)
is fully faithful;

(ii)

The counit
\varepsilon\colon\mathrm{L}_{U}\Inf^{M}_{P}\xRightarrow{\cong}\id_{\mathrm{D}(M)}
is an isomorphism;

(iii)

\mathrm{L}_{U}(\mathbf{1}_{P})\cong\mathbf{1}_{M}
in
\mathrm{D}(M)
;

(iv)

\mathrm{L}_{U}(\mathbf{1}_{U})\cong\mathbf{1}
in
\mathrm{D}(k)
.

Proof.

For
(i)
\iff
(ii)
, see
Lemma
2.1.5
, and the equivalence

(ii)
\iff
(iii)
follows from the
projection formula
3.3.5
; note that

(iii)
is satisfied if and only if
\varepsilon_{\mathbf{1}_{P}}
is
an isomorphism. The equivalence

(iii)
\iff
(iv)
follows from
Proposition
3.2.19
.

The hypothesis on
\varphi
shows that
there exists
n_{0}\geq 0
with
\varphi^{n_{0}}(K_{U})\subseteq K_{U}
. Hence, replacing

\varphi^{n_{0}}
by
\varphi
if necessary, we may assume that

\varphi
is
positive
, meaning that
\varphi(K_{U})\subseteq K_{U}
.
By
(iii)
\iff
(iv)
, we may assume
P=U\rtimes M
, where
M\cong\mathbb{Z}
is generated by an
element
z
acting through conjugation on
U
via
\varphi
. Let
M^{+}
be the
monoid generated by
z
and put
P^{+}=K_{U}M^{+}
.

If
U
has the discrete topology, the compact group
K_{U}
is finite. As

\varphi
is positive, we must have
\varphi^{n}(K_{U})=K_{U}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
hence the hypothesis shows
K_{U}=\{1\}
and then
U=\{1\}
, in which case the
assertion is trivial.

If
U
is not discrete, the hypothesis implies that
U
contains no compact open
subgroup which is normalized by
M
. In particular,
U
is not compact. By
Corollary
3.4.23
, there is a natural isomorphism

\ind_{M^{+}}^{M}\mathrm{H}^{d+i}(K_{U},\delta_{P^{+}})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{L}_{U}^{i}(\mathbf{1}),

where
\omega_{P^{+}}=\delta_{P^{+}}[d]
and
d=\dim U
. Fix
i\neq 0
. As
K_{U}

is a Poincaré group, the
k
-vector space
\mathrm{H}^{-i}(K_{U},\mathbf{1})
is
finite-dimensional, and the cup product
\mathrm{H}^{-i}(K_{U},\mathbf{1})\times\mathrm{H}^{d+i}(K_{U},\mathbf{1})\longrightarrow\mathbf{1}
is a perfect pairing. Therefore,
\mathrm{H}^{-i}(K_{U},\mathbf{1})

identifies with the
k
-linear dual of
\mathrm{H}^{d+i}(K_{U},\mathbf{1})
, and for each
m\in M^{+}
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{d+i}(mK_{U}m^{-1},\mathbf{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{-i}(mK_{U}m^{-1},\mathbf{1})^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{d+i}(K_{U},\mathbf{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{-i}(K_{U},\mathbf{1})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cores}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\res^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

is commutative. As
\mathrm{H}^{-i}(K_{U},\mathbf{1})
is finite-dimensional and
i\neq 0
, we
find (by the hypothesis) some
n\gg 0
such that for
m\coloneqq z^{n}
the right
vertical map vanishes. Hence, also the left vertical map is zero. As the
Hecke-action of
m
on
\mathrm{H}^{d+i}(K_{U},\delta_{P^{+}})
is given by the composite

\mathrm{H}^{d+i}(K_{U},\delta_{P^{+}})\xrightarrow{\delta_{P^{+}}(m)\cdot\conj_{m}}\mathrm{H}^{d+i}(mK_{U}m^{-1},\delta_{P^{+}})\xrightarrow{\cores}\mathrm{H}^{d+i}(K_{U},\delta_{P^{+}}),

where
\conj_{m}
is the conjugation by
m
on cohomology, we deduce that

\ind_{M^{+}}^{M}\mathrm{H}^{d+i}(K_{U},\delta_{P^{+}})=\{0\}
. Therefore,
\mathrm{L}_{U}^{i}(\mathbf{1})=\{0\}
. Finally, it is clear that
\mathrm{L}_{U}^{0}(\mathbf{1})=\mathbf{1}

(cf. Proposition
3.2.8
).
∎

Example 3.4.25
.

If
U
is the group of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-points of a connected unipotent linear algebraic
group, then
the functor
\Inf^{M}_{P}\colon\mathrm{D}(M)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(P)
is fully faithful. Indeed, by
Proposition
3.2.10
.
(a)
the class of
groups
U^{\prime}
satisfying
\mathrm{L}_{U^{\prime}}(\mathbf{1}_{U^{\prime}})\cong\mathbf{1}
is closed under extensions.
Now,
U
admits a composition series with components isomorphic to
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, so we
may assume
U=\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. But then the hypothesis of Corollary
3.4.24

is satisfied for
K_{U}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
\varphi
being multiplication by
p
.

4.
The left adjoint of derived parabolic induction

As in §
3
we fix a field
k
of characteristic
p>0
.

4.1.
General results

Let
G
be a
p
-adic reductive group,
i.e.
, the group of
\mathfrak{F}
-points of a
connected reductive group defined over a finite field extension
\mathfrak{F}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.
Let
P
be a parabolic subgroup of
G
with Levi quotient
M
and
unipotent radical
U
.

The functor of
parabolic induction

i_{M}^{G}\coloneqq\Ind_{P}^{G}\circ\Inf^{M}_{P}\colon\Rep_{k}(M)\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(G)

is exact and hence defines a functor
i_{M}^{G}\colon\mathrm{D}(M)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(G)
on the derived
categories.

Theorem 4.1.1
.

The derived parabolic induction
i_{M}^{G}
admits a left adjoint, denoted

\mathrm{L}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
. In fact, there is a natural isomorphism

\RHom_{\Rep_{k}(M)}\bigl(\mathrm{L}(U,X),Y\bigr)\xrightarrow{\cong}\RHom_{\Rep_{k}(G)}\bigl(X,i_{M}^{G}(Y)\bigr)\qquad\text{in $\mathrm{D}(k)$,}

for all
X\in\mathrm{D}(G)
and
Y\in\mathrm{D}(M)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.1.3
and
Theorem
3.2.3
it follows that
\mathrm{L}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})=\mathrm{L}_{U}\circ\Res^{G}_{P}
is the left adjoint of
i_{M}^{G}
. The last statement is analogous to
Corollary
3.2.4
(use that
\Ind_{P}^{G}
is exact).
∎

Notation 4.1.2
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
we denote by
\mathrm{L}^{n}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\colon\Rep_{k}(G)\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(M)
the

n
-th cohomology functor of
\mathrm{L}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
, that is,

\mathrm{L}^{n}(U,V)\coloneqq\mathrm{H}^{n}\bigl(\mathrm{L}(U,V[0])\bigr),\qquad\text{for $V\in\Rep_{k}(G)$.}

We record the following consequence of Theorem
4.1.1
:

Corollary 4.1.3
.

Given
V\in\Rep_{k}(G)
and
W\in\Rep_{k}(M)
, there is a convergent
first-quadrant spectral sequence

E_{2}^{i,j}=\Ext_{\Rep_{k}(M)}^{i}\bigl(\mathrm{L}^{-j}(U,V),W\bigr)\Longrightarrow\Ext_{\Rep_{k}(G)}^{i+j}\bigl(V,i_{M}^{G}W\bigr).

In particular, there is a five-term exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext_{\Rep_{k}(M)}^{1}\bigl(\mathrm{L}^{0}(U,V),W\bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext_{\Rep_{k}(G)}^{1}\bigl(V,i_{M}^{G}W\bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\Rep_{k}(M)}\bigl(\mathrm{L}^{-1}(U,V),W\bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext_{\Rep_{k}(M)}^{2}\bigl(\mathrm{L}^{0}(U,V),W\bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext_{\Rep_{k}(G)}^{2}\bigl(V,i_{M}^{G}W\bigr).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}^{0,1}}

Proof.

The functor
\RHom_{\Rep_{k}(M)}({\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}},W)
is left
t
-exact and
\mathrm{L}(U,V)
is right
bounded. We obtain the spectral sequence from
Lemma
2.3.22
.
(b)
combined with
Theorem
4.1.1
.
∎

Proposition 4.1.4
.

Let
Q\subseteq P
be a parabolic subgroup of
G
with Levi quotient
L
and
unipotent radical
U^{\prime}
.

(a)

The diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(L)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}(U^{\prime},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}(U^{\prime}/U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}}})}

is commutative.

(b)

Given
V\in\Rep_{k}(G)
, there is a
convergent third-quadrant spectral sequence

E_{2}^{i,j}=\mathrm{L}^{i}\bigl(U^{\prime}/U,\mathrm{L}^{j}(U,V)\bigr)\Longrightarrow\mathrm{L}^{i+j}(U^{\prime},V).

In particular, there is a five-term exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}^{-2}(U^{\prime},V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}^{-2}\bigl(U^{\prime}/U,\mathrm{L}^{0}(U,V)\bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}^{0}\bigl(U^{\prime}/U,\mathrm{L}^{-1}(U,V)\bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}^{-1}(U^{\prime},V\bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}^{-1}\bigl(U^{\prime}/U,\mathrm{L}^{0}(U,V)\bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{-2,0}}

Proof.

(a)

Clearly, there is a natural isomorphism

\mathrm{L}^{0}(U^{\prime}/U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\circ\mathrm{L}^{0}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\xRightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{L}^{0}(U^{\prime},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
of functors

\Rep_{k}(G)\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(L)
. Passing to the right adjoints, see
Example
2.1.13
, yields a natural isomorphism

i_{L}^{G}\xRightarrow{\cong}i_{M}^{G}\circ i_{L}^{M}
. But this extends to a natural
isomorphism of functors
\mathrm{D}(L)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(G)
. Hence, passing to the left adjoints
yields a natural isomorphism
\mathrm{L}(U^{\prime}/U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\circ\mathrm{L}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\xRightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{L}(U^{\prime},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
.

(b)

The functors
\mathrm{L}(U^{\prime}/U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
and
\mathrm{L}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})

are right
t
-exact (as left adjoints of
t
-exact functors). Now,
Lemma
2.3.22
.
(a)
applied to

(a)
yields the desired spectral sequence.

∎

Fix a compact, open, torsion-free subgroup
K
of
G
. For any closed subgroup

H
of
G
we write
K_{H}\coloneqq K\cap H
.
Let
M^{+}
and
P^{+}
be the positive monoids of §
3.4

associated with
K_{P}
. Then
[
BK98
, (6.14)]

shows that Hypothesis
3.4.1
is satisfied.

Proposition 4.1.5
.

There is a natural isomorphism

\ind_{M^{+}}^{M}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}\bigl(K_{U},\omega_{P^{+}}\otimes_{k}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}\bigr)\Res^{G}_{P^{+}}\xRightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{L}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})

of functors
\mathrm{D}(G)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(M)
. In particular,
\mathrm{L}^{-n}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
vanishes
provided
n\notin\{0,1,\dotsc,\dim K_{U}\}
.

Proof.

Immediate from Corollary
3.4.23
.
∎

Remark 4.1.6
.

From Corollary
3.4.23
and
[
Eme10b
, Thm. 3.4.7(1) and
Lem. 3.2.1(1)]
it follows that on (locally) admissible
representations the
\delta
-functor
\mathrm{L}^{\bullet}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
is isomorphic to the

\delta
-functor
\mathrm{H}_{\bullet}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})=\mathrm{H}^{\dim U-\bullet}\Ord_{P}({\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\otimes\delta_{P}
considered in
[
Hau18
, §4.2]
.

Corollary 4.1.7
.

There are adjunctions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\colon\mathrm{D}^{+}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}^{+}(M):\!i_{M}^{G},}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\colon\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(M):\!i_{M}^{G}.}

Proof.

This is immediate from Proposition
4.1.5
.
∎

As an immediate consequence of Corollary
3.2.25
we have the following
result:

Proposition 4.1.8
.

There is a natural isomorphism

\Hom_{\mathrm{D}(k)}\bigl(\Res^{M}_{1}\mathrm{L}(U,X),Y\bigr)\cong\Hom_{\mathrm{D}(U)}\bigl(\Res^{G}_{U}X,\Inf^{1}_{U}Y\bigr)

for all
X\in\mathrm{D}(G)
,
Y\in\mathrm{D}(k)
. In particular, for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
there
exists a natural
k
-linear isomorphism

\Hom_{k}\bigl(\mathrm{L}^{-n}(U,V),W\bigr)\xrightarrow{\cong}\Ext_{\Rep_{k}(U)}^{n}(V,\Inf^{1}_{U}W),

for all
V\in\Rep_{k}(G)
and
W\in\mathrm{Vect}_{k}
.

4.2.
Preservation of global admissibility

Retain the notation of §
4.1
. Let
\overline{P}
be the
parabolic
subgroup of
G
opposite
P
with unipotent radical
\overline{U}
and Levi quotient

M
. We make the further assumption that the compact, open, torsion-free
subgroup
K\subseteq G
admits an Iwahori decomposition:

K=K_{\overline{U}}K_{M}K_{U}.

Recall from
[
SS23
, Def. 4.2]
the following definition:

Definition
.

Let
H
be a
p
-adic Lie group and
H^{\prime}\subseteq H
a compact, open,
torsion-free subgroup. A complex
X\in\mathrm{D}(H)
is called
globally
admissible
if for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
the
k
-vector space
\mathrm{H}^{i}(H^{\prime},X)
is
finite-dimensional.
By
[
SS23
, Cor. 4.6]
, this definition is independent of the
choice of
H^{\prime}
.

The strictly full triangulated subcategory of
\mathrm{D}(H)
consisting of the globally
admissible complexes is denoted
\mathrm{D}(H)^{\mathrm{a}}
.

In this section we prove the following result:

Theorem 4.2.1
.

There is an adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\colon\mathrm{D}(G)^{\mathrm{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(M)^{\mathrm{a}}:\!i_{M}^{G}.}

We will prove separately that
\mathrm{L}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
and
i_{M}^{G}
preserve globally
admissible complexes. Before turning to the proofs, let us observe an immediate
consequence.

Given a
p
-adic Lie group
H
, we denote by
\mathrm{D}^{+}_{\mathrm{adm}}(H)
, resp.
\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{adm}}(H)
, the strictly full triangulated subcategory of

\mathrm{D}^{+}(H)
, resp.
\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(H)
, consisting of those complexes
X
such that

\mathrm{H}^{i}(X)
is admissible, for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Corollary 4.2.2
.

There are adjunctions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\colon\mathrm{D}^{+}_{\mathrm{adm}}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}^{+}_{\mathrm{adm}}(M):\!i_{M}^{G},}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\colon\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{adm}}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{adm}}(M):\!i_{M}^{G}.}

Proof.

By
[
SS23
, Prop. 4.9]
we
have
\mathrm{D}^{+}_{\mathrm{adm}}(G)=\mathrm{D}^{+}(G)\cap\mathrm{D}(G)^{\mathrm{a}}
and,
a fortiori
,

\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{adm}}(G)=\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(G)\cap\mathrm{D}(G)^{\mathrm{a}}
; similarly with

G
replaced by
M
. As
\mathrm{L}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
and
i_{M}^{G}
preserve bounded complexes, the
claim follows from Theorem
4.2.1
.
∎

4.2.1.
Parabolic induction preserves global
admissibility

It is well-known that the parabolic induction functor
i_{M}^{G}\colon\Rep_{k}(M)\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(G)
preserves admissibility,
[
Vig96
, II.2.1]
. In order to
prove that derived parabolic induction
i_{M}^{G}=\Ind_{P}^{G}\Inf^{M}_{P}
preserves
global admissibility, we show separately that
\Inf^{M}_{P}
and
\Ind_{P}^{G}
preserve
global admissibility.

Lemma 4.2.3
.

One has
\Ind_{P}^{G}\bigl(\mathrm{D}(P)^{\mathrm{a}}\bigr)\subseteq\mathrm{D}(G)^{\mathrm{a}}
.

Proof.

Let
X\in\mathrm{D}(P)^{\mathrm{a}}
be globally admissible. We need to show that

\mathrm{H}^{i}(K,\Ind_{P}^{G}X)
is finite-dimensional, for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. Note that, for
every
g\in G
the subgroup
P\cap gKg^{-1}\subseteq P
is compact, open, and
torsion-free. Applying the derived Mackey decomposition,
Lemma
3.2.15
.
(a)
, we deduce

\displaystyle\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K,\Ind_{P}^{G}X)

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}\Bigl(K,\prod_{g\in P\backslash G/K}\Ind_{g^{-1}Pg\cap K}^{K}g_{*}^{-1}\Res^{P}_{P\cap gKg^{-1}}X\Bigr)

\displaystyle\cong\prod_{g\in P\backslash G/K}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}\bigl(K,\Ind^{K}_{g^{-1}Pg\cap K}g_{*}^{-1}\Res^{P}_{P\cap gKg^{-1}}X\bigr)

\displaystyle\cong\prod_{g\in P\backslash G/K}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}\bigl(g^{-1}Pg\cap K,g_{*}^{-1}\Res^{P}_{P\cap gKg^{-1}}X\bigr)

\displaystyle\cong\prod_{g\in P\backslash G/K}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}\bigl(P\cap gKg^{-1},\Res^{P}_{P\cap gKg^{-1}}X\bigr).

Note that the product is finite, because
P\backslash G
is compact and
K
is
open. The second isomorphism holds, since
\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
is an additive functor.
For the third isomorphism, observe that there is a natural isomorphism

\Inf^{1}_{g^{-1}Pg\cap K}\xRightarrow{\cong}\Res^{K}_{g^{-1}Pg\cap K}\Inf^{1}_{K}
,
so that passing to the right adjoints (see Example
2.1.13
) yields an
isomorphism
\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\Ind_{g^{-1}Pg\cap K}^{K}\xRightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(g^{-1}Pg\cap K,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
. The last isomorphism is given by conjugation.

As
X
is globally admissible, the right hand side in the above computation has
finite-dimensional cohomology, hence so does the left hand side. Consequently,

\Ind_{P}^{G}X
is globally admissible.
∎

Before proving that
\Inf^{M}_{P}
preserves global admissibility, we recall a
well-known general fact.

Lemma 4.2.4
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a tensor triangulated category which is compactly generated by
the tensor unit
\mathbf{1}
. Let
\mathscr{T}
be a full triangulated subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
which is closed under direct summands. One has
T\otimes X\in\mathscr{T}
for
every
T\in\mathscr{T}
and every compact
X\in\mathscr{C}
.

Proof.

Consider the full subcategory
\mathscr{S}
of
\mathscr{C}
consisting of
those
X
such that for all
T\in\mathscr{T}
it holds that
T\otimes X\in\mathscr{T}
.
As
\mathscr{T}
is triangulated and closed under direct summands, it is clear that

\mathscr{S}
is triangulated and closed under direct summands. By

[
Nee92
, Lem. 2.2]
the full triangulated subcategory
\langle\mathbf{1}\rangle
generated by
\mathbf{1}
and closed under direct summands contains
precisely the compact objects. Since
\mathbf{1}\in\mathscr{S}
, we deduce
\langle\mathbf{1}\rangle\subseteq\mathscr{S}
, that is,

\mathscr{S}
contains all compact objects. This implies the assertion.
∎

Lemma 4.2.5
.

One has
\Inf^{M}_{P}\bigl(\mathrm{D}(M)^{\mathrm{a}}\bigr)\subseteq\mathrm{D}(P)^{\mathrm{a}}
.

Proof.

Given
X\in\mathrm{D}(M)
, we observe that
\Res^{M}_{K_{M}}(X)
is globally admissible if
and only if
X
is; similarly with
M
replaced by
P
. Therefore, it suffices
to prove
\Inf^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}\bigl(\mathrm{D}(K_{M})^{\mathrm{a}}\bigr)\subseteq\mathrm{D}(K_{P})^{\mathrm{a}}
. Let
X\in\mathrm{D}(K_{M})^{\mathrm{a}}
be globally admissible. By
Proposition
3.1.6
we know that
\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},\mathbf{1})
is
compact. Now, Lemma
4.2.4
implies
X\otimes_{k}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},\mathbf{1})\in\mathrm{D}(K_{M})^{\mathrm{a}}
. Applying the projection formula,
Lemma
3.1.2
, we deduce that

\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}\bigl(K_{P},\Inf^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}X\bigr)\cong\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}\bigl(K_{M},\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},\Inf^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}X)\bigr)\cong\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{M},X\otimes_{k}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},\mathbf{1})\bigr)

has finite-dimensional cohomology. Therefore,
\Inf^{K_{M}}_{K_{P}}X
is globally
admissible.
∎

Proposition 4.2.6
.

Derived parabolic induction restricts to a functor
i_{M}^{G}\colon\mathrm{D}(M)^{\mathrm{a}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(G)^{\mathrm{a}}
.

Proof.

Combine Lemmas
4.2.3
and
4.2.5
.
∎

4.2.2.
\mathrm{L}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
preserves global
admissibility

Fix a positive central element
z_{0}\in M
satisfying the conditions in

[
BK98
, (6.14)]
. In
particular,
z_{0}
is strictly positive and satisfies
z_{0}K_{\overline{U}}z_{0}^{-1}\supsetneq K_{\overline{U}}
. Let
C
be the central subgroup of
M
generated by

z_{0}
. Then
C^{+}\coloneqq M^{+}\cap C
is the submonoid generated by
z_{0}
. Note
that
K_{M}C
is a direct product, since
C\cap K_{M}=\{1\}
.

Lemma 4.2.7
.

The diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(K_{M}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(K_{P}C^{+})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(C^{+})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(C)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{G}_{K_{P}C^{+}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{M}_{K_{M}C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}_{K_{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}_{K_{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ind_{C^{+}}^{C}}

is commutative. In particular, there is a natural
k
-linear isomorphism

\mathrm{H}^{i}\bigl(K_{M},\mathrm{L}(U,X)\bigr)\cong\ind_{C^{+}}^{C}\mathrm{H}^{i+\dim K_{U}}(K_{P},X)

for every
X\in\mathrm{D}(G)
.

Proof.

We compute

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}_{K_{M}}\Res^{M}_{K_{M}C}\mathrm{L}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{L}_{K_{M}}\Res^{M}_{K_{M}C}\mathrm{L}_{U}\Res^{G}_{P}

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{L}_{K_{M}}\mathrm{L}_{U}\Res^{P}_{UK_{M}C}\Res^{G}_{P}

(by Prop.
3.2.19
)

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{L}_{K_{M}}\ind_{K_{M}C^{+}}^{K_{M}C}\mathrm{L}_{K_{U}}\Res^{UK_{M}C}_{K_{P}C^{+}}\Res^{G}_{UK_{M}C}

(by Prop.
3.4.6
)

\displaystyle\cong\ind_{C^{+}}^{C}\mathrm{L}_{K_{M}}\mathrm{L}_{K_{U}}\Res^{G}_{K_{P}C^{+}}

(by
Prop.
3.2.11
)

\displaystyle\cong\ind_{C^{+}}^{C}\mathrm{L}_{K_{P}}\Res^{G}_{K_{P}C^{+}}

(by
Prop.
3.2.10
)

which proves the first assertion. Now, applying
Corollary
3.4.21
, we deduce a

C
-equivariant natural isomorphism

\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}\bigl(K_{M},\omega_{K_{M}C}\otimes\mathrm{L}(U,X)\bigr)\cong\ind_{C^{+}}^{C}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}\bigl(K_{P},\omega_{UK_{M}C^{+}}\otimes X\bigr).

Restricting to
k
and passing to cohomology yields the final assertion, since

\omega_{K_{M}C}
is concentrated in degree
-\dim K_{M}
and
\omega_{UK_{M}C^{+}}
is
concentrated in degree
-\dim K_{P}
, and because
\dim K_{P}-\dim K_{M}=\dim K_{U}
.
∎

We recall the Hecke action of
C^{+}
on
\mathrm{H}^{0}(K,V)
, for
V\in\Rep_{k}(G)
:
Note first that the inclusion
K_{U}\subseteq K
induces, for each
z\in C^{+}
, a
bijection

(4.2.8)

K_{U}/zK_{U}z^{-1}\cong K/K_{\overline{P}}zK_{U}z^{-1},

since
K=K_{U}K_{\overline{P}}
. (By our choice of
z_{0}
,
K_{\overline{P}}zK_{U}z^{-1}=K\cap zKz^{-1}
is an open subgroup of
K
.) Then the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z\star({\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\colon}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{0}(K,V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{0}\bigl(K_{\overline{P}}zK_{U}z^{-1},V\bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{0}(K,V),}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces zv}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sum_{u\in K_{U}/zK_{U}z^{-1}}uzv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cores}

defines the Hecke action of
C^{+}
on
\mathrm{H}^{0}(K,V)
such that the natural
inclusion
\mathrm{H}^{0}(K,V)\subseteq\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{P},V)
is
C^{+}
-equivariant. We obtain a
left exact functor
\mathrm{H}^{0}(K,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\colon\Rep_{k}(G)\longrightarrow\Rep_{k}(C^{+})
and hence a
derived functor

\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\colon\mathrm{D}(G)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(C^{+})

on the derived categories.

Lemma 4.2.9
.

The inclusion
\mathrm{H}^{0}(K,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{P},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
induces a natural
isomorphism

\ind_{C^{+}}^{C}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\xRightarrow{\cong}\ind_{C^{+}}^{C}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{P},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\Res^{G}_{K_{P}C^{+}}.

Proof.

We first show that
\Res^{G}_{K_{P}C^{+}}
sends injective objects to

\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{P},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
-acyclic objects. As
K_{P}
is open in
K_{P}C^{+}
, it follows that

\Res^{K_{P}C^{+}}_{K_{P}}
admits an exact left adjoint and hence preserves injective
objects (Lemma
2.1.8
). As
\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{P},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
has finite
cohomological dimension, the functor
\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{P},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
can be computed using
acyclic objects, see
[
Har66
, Cor. 5.3
\gamma
]
. Hence, there is
an isomorphism

\Res^{C^{+}}_{1}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{P},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\cong\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{P},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\Res^{K_{P}C^{+}}_{K_{P}}.

Now, if
J
in
\Rep_{k}(G)
is injective, then so is
\Res^{G}_{K_{P}}(J)
by

[
Eme10b
, Prop. 2.1.11]
and therefore,

\Res^{C^{+}}_{1}\mathrm{H}^{i}\bigl(K_{P},\Res^{G}_{K_{P}C^{+}}(J)\bigr)=\mathrm{H}^{i}\bigl(K_{P},\Res^{G}_{K_{P}}(J)\bigr)=0,\qquad\text{for all $i>0$.}

Hence,
\Res^{G}_{K_{P}C^{+}}(J)
is
\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{P},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
-acyclic.

We are thus reduced to showing that the natural map

(4.2.10)

\ind_{C^{+}}^{C}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K,V)\longrightarrow\ind_{C^{+}}^{C}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{P},V)

is bijective, for all
V\in\Rep_{k}(G)
. As
\ind_{C^{+}}^{C}
is exact, the
injectivity is clear. The surjectivity follows from the same argument as in

[
Eme10b
, Lem. 3.4.5]
which we recall here for the benefit of the reader:
Let
v\in\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{P},V)
be arbitrary. As
V
is smooth, there exists
z\in C^{+}

such that
v
is fixed by
z^{-1}K_{\overline{U}}zK_{P}=z^{-1}Kz\cap K
. Now, we have

z\star v=\sum_{u\in K_{U}/zK_{U}z^{-1}}uzv\in\mathrm{H}^{0}(K,V),

because
zv
is fixed by
K_{\overline{P}}zK_{U}z^{-1}
and
u
runs through a
representing system of
K/K_{\overline{P}}zK_{U}z^{-1}
, see (
4.2.8
).
Therefore,
z^{-1}\otimes z\star v
maps to
1\otimes v\in\ind_{C^{+}}^{C}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{P},V)
under (
4.2.10
). This finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.2.11
.

Let
V\in\Rep_{k}(C^{+})
be a finite-dimensional representation. The canonical map

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\hskip-11.99998pt\rightarrow\hskip 1.99997pt\ind_{C^{+}}^{C}V,

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\longmapsto 1\otimes v

is surjective.

Proof.

This is immediate from the proof of
[
Eme10b
, Lem. 3.2.1 (1)]
.
∎

Proposition 4.2.12
.

The functor
\mathrm{L}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
restricts to a functor
\mathrm{D}(G)^{\mathrm{a}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(M)^{\mathrm{a}}
.

Proof.

Let
X
in
\mathrm{D}(G)^{\mathrm{a}}
be a globally admissible complex and let
i\in\mathbb{Z}
be
arbitrary. Since
\Res^{C^{+}}_{1}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\cong\mathrm{R}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\Res^{G}_{K}
, we know that
\mathrm{H}^{i}(K,X)
is a finite-dimensional smooth

C^{+}
-representation. Now, the sequence of maps

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{i}(K,X)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\hskip-11.99998pt\rightarrow\hskip 1.99997pt\ind_{C^{+}}^{C}\mathrm{H}^{i}(K,X)

(Lemma
4.2.11
)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}\ind_{C^{+}}^{C}\mathrm{H}^{i}(K_{P},X)

(Lemma
4.2.9
)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{H}^{i-\dim K_{U}}\bigl(K_{M},\mathrm{L}(U,X)\bigr)

(Lemma
4.2.7
)

shows that
\mathrm{H}^{i-\dim K_{U}}\bigl(K_{M},\mathrm{L}(U,X)\bigr)
is finite-dimensional.
Therefore
\mathrm{L}(U,X)
is globally admissible.
∎

Finally, Propositions
4.2.6
and
4.2.12
together
imply Theorem
4.2.1
.

4.3.
On the Satake homomorphism

The functor
\mathrm{L}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\colon\mathrm{D}(G)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(M)
can be used to define a derived
version of the Satake homomorphism, which we will now describe.

We fix an open compact mod center subgroup
K\subseteq G
satisfying the
Iwasawa decomposition
G=PK
and
K_{P}=K_{U}K_{M}
. Writing
\mathrm{L}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\coloneqq\mathrm{L}_{K_{U}}\circ\Res^{K}_{K_{P}}\colon\mathrm{D}(K)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(K_{M})
, there are natural isomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\ind_{K}^{G}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{L}_{U}\Res^{G}_{P}\ind_{K}^{G}\xRightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{L}_{U}\ind_{K_{P}}^{P}\Res^{K}_{K_{P}}\xRightarrow{\cong}\ind_{K_{M}}^{M}\mathrm{L}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}),

where the first isomorphism follows from the Iwasawa decomposition and the last
is the one from Proposition
3.2.11
.

Definition
.

Given
X\in\mathrm{D}(K)
, we call the
k
-algebra homomorphism

\mathcal{S}_{X}\colon\End_{\mathrm{D}(G)}\bigl(\ind_{K}^{G}X\bigr)\longrightarrow\End_{\mathrm{D}(M)}\bigl(\ind_{K_{M}}^{M}\mathrm{L}(K_{U},X)\bigr)

induced by
\mathrm{L}(U,{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
the
derived Satake homomorphism
.

If
V\in\Rep_{k}(K)
, composing
\mathcal{S}_{V}
with the cohomology functor

\mathrm{H}^{-n}
yields a homomorphism

\mathcal{S}_{V}^{n}\colon\End_{\Rep_{k}(G)}\bigl(\ind_{K}^{G}V\bigr)\longrightarrow\End_{\Rep_{k}(M)}\bigl(\ind_{K_{M}}^{M}\mathrm{L}^{-n}(K_{U},V)\bigr)

of
k
-algebras, which we call the
n
-th Satake homomorphism
.
(As usual, we write
\mathrm{L}^{-n}(K_{U},V)\coloneqq\mathrm{H}^{-n}(\mathrm{L}(K_{U},V[0]))
.)
Note that

\mathcal{S}_{V}^{n}
is zero whenever
n\notin\{0,1,\dotsc,\dim K_{U}\}
.

Remark 4.3.1
.

One could also define a variant of the derived Satake homomorphism via

\RHom_{\Rep_{k}(G)}\bigl(\ind_{K}^{G}V,\ind_{K}^{G}V\bigr)\longrightarrow\RHom_{\Rep_{k}(M)}\bigl(\ind_{K_{M}}^{M}\mathrm{L}(K_{U},V),\ind_{K_{M}}^{M}\mathrm{L}(K_{U},V)\bigr).

It might be interesting to relate the induced map on the cohomology algebras to
Ronchetti’s derived Satake homomorphism,
[
Ron19
]
.

The aim of this section is to show that
\mathcal{S}^{0}_{V}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\dim K_{U}}_{V}

are the well-known variants of the Satake homomorphism that were introduced by
Herzig,
[
Her11b
]
, and extensively studied by Herzig and
Henniart–Vignéras,
[
Her11a
,
HV15
,
HV12
]
.

Theorem 4.3.2
.

Let
V\in\Rep_{k}(K)
.

(a)

Denote
\eta\colon V\longrightarrow\hskip-11.99998pt\rightarrow\hskip 1.99997pt\mathrm{L}^{0}(K_{U},V)
the natural projection map. The map
\mathcal{S}^{0}_{V}
is explicitly
given by

\displaystyle\End_{\Rep_{k}(G)}(\ind_{K}^{G}V)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\End_{\Rep_{k}(M)}\bigl(\ind_{K_{M}}^{M}\mathrm{L}^{0}(K_{U},V)\bigr),

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{0}_{V}(\Phi)([1,\eta(v)])(m)

\displaystyle=\eta\Bigl(\sum_{u\in K_{U}\backslash U}\Phi([1,v])(um)\Bigr)

for all
\Phi\in\End_{\Rep_{k}(G)}(\ind_{K}^{G}V)
,
v\in V
, and
m\in M
.

(b)

The map
\mathcal{S}^{\dim K_{U}}_{V}
is explicitly given by

\displaystyle\End_{\Rep_{k}(G)}(\ind_{K}^{G}V)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\End_{\Rep_{k}(M)}\bigl(\ind_{K_{M}}^{M}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},V)\bigr),

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{V}^{\dim K_{U}}(\Phi)([1,v])(m)

\displaystyle=\sum_{u\in U/K_{U}}\Phi([1,v])(mu)

for all
\Phi\in\End_{\Rep_{k}(G)}(\ind_{K}^{G}V)
,
v\in\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},V)
, and
m\in M
.

Explanation 4.3.3
.

Since
\delta_{P}\otimes{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}}
is an equivalence of categories, there are
natural isomorphisms
\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\circ(\delta_{P}\otimes{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\xRightarrow{\cong}(\delta_{P}\otimes{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\circ\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
and
(\delta_{P}\otimes{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})\circ\ind_{K_{M}}^{M}\xRightarrow{\cong}\ind_{K_{M}}^{M}\circ(\delta_{P}\otimes{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
. By
Corollary
3.4.21
we have a natural isomorphism
\mathrm{L}^{-\dim K_{U}}(K_{U},V)\cong\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},\delta_{P}\otimes V)\cong\delta_{P}\otimes\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},V)
.
Consequently, the target of
\mathcal{S}_{V}^{\dim K_{U}}
may be identified with

\End_{\Rep_{k}(M)}\bigl(\ind_{K_{M}}^{M}\mathrm{L}^{-\dim K_{U}}(K_{U},V)\bigr)\cong\End_{\Rep_{k}(M)}\bigl(\ind_{K_{M}}^{M}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},V)\bigr),

which justifies the formulation in
Theorem
4.3.2
.
(b)
.

The proof of Theorem
4.3.2
.
(b)
needs some
preparation and is deferred until the end of the section. Statement

(a)
is well-known. Since I could not find a precise
reference to the literature, I will recall the easy proof for the convenience of
the reader.

Proof of Theorem
4.3.2
.
(a)
.

By definition the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ind_{K}^{G}V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}^{0}(U,\ind_{K}^{G}V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ind_{K_{M}}^{M}\mathrm{L}^{0}(K_{U},V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ind_{K}^{G}V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}^{0}(U,\ind_{K}^{G}V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ind_{K_{M}}^{M}\mathrm{L}^{0}(K_{U},V)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}^{0}(U,\Phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}_{V}^{0}(\Phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

is commutative, where the horizontal compositions are given explicitly by

[g,v]\longmapsto[\pr_{M}(g),\eta(v)],\qquad\text{for $g\in P$ and $v\in V$.}

(Recall that
G=PK
.) Fix a complete representing
system
\mathcal{M}
of
K_{M}\backslash M
in
M
. Then the map

\displaystyle K_{U}\backslash U\times\mathcal{M}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}K_{P}\backslash P,

\displaystyle(K_{U}u,m)

\displaystyle\longmapsto K_{P}um

is bijective. Using
\Phi([1,v])=\sum_{u\in K_{U}\backslash U}\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}}\bigl[(um)^{-1},\Phi([1,v])(um)\bigr]
, we compute

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{0}_{V}(\Phi)\bigl([1,\eta(v)]\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{u\in K_{U}\backslash U}\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}}\bigl[m^{-1},\eta\bigl(\Phi([1,v])(um)\bigr)\bigr]

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}}\Bigl[m^{-1},\eta\Bigl(\sum_{u\in K_{U}\backslash U}\Phi([1,v])(um)\Bigr)\Bigr],

which shows
\mathcal{S}^{0}_{V}(\Phi)\bigl([1,\eta(v)]\bigr)(m)=\eta\bigl(\sum_{u\in K_{U}\backslash U}\Phi([1,v])(um)\bigr)
for all
m\in M
.
∎

4.3.1.
Finishing the proof of
Theorem
4.3.2
.
(b)

Fix
W\in\Rep_{k}(K_{P})
and recall from Corollary
3.4.23
the
isomorphism

\mathrm{L}^{-\dim K_{U}}_{U}(\ind_{K_{P}}^{P}W)\cong\ind_{M^{+}}^{M}\mathrm{H}^{0}\bigl(K_{U},\delta_{P}\otimes\ind_{K_{P}}^{P}W\bigr)\cong\delta_{P}\otimes\ind_{M^{+}}^{M}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},\ind_{K_{P}}^{P}W).

As was already remarked, the character
\delta_{P}
can be neglected for the
computation of the Satake homomorphism. The
M^{+}
-action on
\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},\ind_{K_{P}}^{P}W)
is given by

m\star f\coloneqq\sum_{u\in K_{U}/mK_{U}m^{-1}}umf,

for
m\in M^{+}
and
f\in\ind_{K_{P}}^{P}W
. By the Mackey decomposition,
Lemma
3.2.15
, we have a natural isomorphism

(4.3.4)

\mathrm{H}^{0}\bigl(K_{U},\ind_{K_{P}}^{P}W\bigr)\cong\bigoplus_{g\in K_{U}\backslash P/K_{P}}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U}^{g}\cap K_{P},W),

where
K_{U}^{g}\coloneqq g^{-1}K_{U}g
. We deduce that
\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},\ind_{K_{P}}^{P}W)
is
generated by elements of the form

[K_{U}g,w]\coloneqq\sum_{u\in K_{U}/K_{U}\cap K_{P}^{g^{-1}}}[ug,w],\quad\text{for
$w\in\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U}^{g}\cap K_{P},W)$,}

where
g
runs through a representing system for
K_{U}\backslash P/K_{P}
. In order
to describe the
M^{+}
-action on the
[K_{U}g,w]
we make the following definition:

Definition
.

Given
m\in M^{+}
and
g\in P
, we define the “projection map”

\displaystyle\mu_{m,g}\colon\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U}^{g}\cap K_{P},W)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U}^{mg}\cap K_{P},W),

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle\longmapsto\sum_{u\in K_{U}^{mg}\cap K_{P}/K_{U}^{g}\cap K_{P}}uw.

As
m
is positive, we have
K_{U}^{mg}\cap K_{P}\supseteq K_{U}^{g}\cap K_{P}
, and
hence
\mu_{m,g}
is well-defined.

Lemma 4.3.5
.

Let
g\in P
and
m,m_{1},m_{2}\in M^{+}
.

(a)

\mu_{m,g}=\id_{\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},W)}
provided
g\in P^{+}=K_{U}M^{+}
.

(b)

\mu_{m_{1},g}=\mu_{m_{2},g}
provided

m_{1}g,m_{2}g\in P^{+}
.

(c)

\mu_{1,g}=\id_{\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U}^{g}\cap K_{P},W)}
.

(d)

\mu_{m_{1}m_{2},g}=\mu_{m_{1},m_{2}g}\circ\mu_{m_{2},g}
.

(e)

\mu_{m,ugh}(w)=h^{-1}\mu_{m,g}(hw)
for each

u\in K_{U}
,
h\in K_{P}
,
w\in\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U}^{ugh},W)
.

Proof.

Note that, if
h\in P^{+}
, then
K_{U}^{h}\cap K_{P}=K_{U}
. Now,

(a)
follows by applying this observation to
h=g
and
h=mg
, and
(b)
follows by applying it to
h=m_{1}g
and
h=m_{2}g
.

(c)
is trivial and
(d)
is
straightforward.
For
(e)
note that
K_{U}^{u}=K_{U}
and
K_{U}^{mu}=K_{U}^{m}
,
because
u,mum^{-1}\in K_{U}
.
∎

Notation 4.3.6
.

Given
g\in P
, we put

\mu_{g}\coloneqq\mu_{m,g}\colon\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U}^{g}\cap K_{P},W)\longrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},W),

where
m\in M^{+}
is any element satisfying
mg\in P^{+}
. (Such elements exist by
Hypothesis
3.4.1
.) By
Lemma
4.3.5
.
(b)
the definition of
\mu_{g}
is
independent of the chosen
m
.

Lemma 4.3.7
.

For all
g\in P
,
w\in\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U}^{g}\cap K_{P},W)
and
m\in M^{+}
we have

m\star[K_{U}g,w]=[K_{U}mg,\mu_{m,g}(w)]\qquad\text{in $\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},\ind_{K_{P}}^{P}W)$.}

Proof.

We compute

\displaystyle m\star[K_{U}g,w]

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in K_{U}/K_{U}^{m^{-1}}}\sum_{u\in K_{U}/K_{U}\cap K_{P}^{g^{-1}}}[nmug,w]

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in K_{U}^{mg}/K_{U}^{g}}\sum_{u\in K_{U}^{g}/K_{U}^{g}\cap K_{P}}[mgnu,w]

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in K_{U}^{mg}/K_{U}^{g}\cap K_{P}}[mgn,w]

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in K_{U}^{mg}/K_{U}^{mg}\cap K_{P}}\sum_{u\in K_{U}^{mg}\cap K_{P}/K_{U}^{g}\cap K_{P}}[mgnu,w]

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in K_{U}/K_{U}\cap K_{P}^{(mg)^{-1}}}\Bigl[nmg,\sum_{u\in K_{U}^{mg}\cap K_{P}/K_{U}^{g}\cap K_{P}}uw\Bigr]

\displaystyle=[K_{U}mg,\mu_{m,g}(w)].

∎

Proposition 4.3.8
.

The natural map

\displaystyle\ind_{M^{+}}^{M}\mathrm{H}^{0}\bigl(K_{U},\ind_{K_{P}}^{P}W\bigr)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}\ind_{K_{M}}^{M}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},W),

\displaystyle 1\otimes[K_{U}g,w]

\displaystyle\longmapsto\bigl[\pr_{M}(g),\mu_{g}(w)\bigr]

is an
M
-equivariant isomorphism.

Proof.

We first observe that the inclusion
\ind_{K_{P}}^{P^{+}}W\subseteq\ind_{K_{P}}^{P}W

induces an isomorphism

\ind_{M^{+}}^{M}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},\ind_{K_{P}}^{P^{+}}W)\xrightarrow{\cong}\ind_{M^{+}}^{M}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},\ind_{K_{P}}^{P}W).

Indeed, injectivity is clear, since
\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}{\scalebox{.7}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}{\scalebox{.5}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$-$}}}})
and
\ind_{M^{+}}^{M}
are left
exact. The surjectivity follows from the fact that for every
g\in P
there
exists
m\in M^{+}
such that
mg\in P^{+}
. Concretely, the inverse map is given by

(4.3.9)

\displaystyle\ind_{M^{+}}^{M}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},\ind_{K_{P}}^{P}W)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\ind_{M^{+}}^{M}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},\ind_{K_{P}}^{P^{+}}W),

(4.3.10)

\displaystyle 1\otimes[K_{U}g,w]

\displaystyle\longmapsto m^{-1}\otimes[K_{U}mg,\mu_{m,g}(w)],

where
m\in M^{+}
is chosen such that
mg\in P^{+}
. (Note that the same
m
works
if
g
is replaced by any other element in
K_{U}g
.)

We now prove that the map

(4.3.11)

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},\ind_{K_{P}}^{P^{+}}W)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}\ind_{K_{M}}^{M^{+}}H^{0}(K_{U},W),

(4.3.12)

\displaystyle[K_{U}m,w]

\displaystyle\longmapsto[m,w],

where
m\in M^{+}
, is an
M^{+}
-equivariant isomorphism. The
M^{+}
-equivariance
of the map follows from Lemma
4.3.7
together with

\mu_{m^{\prime},m}=\id
,
for all
m^{\prime},m\in M^{+}
(Lemma
4.3.5
.
(a)
). As
for (
4.3.4
) the
Mackey decomposition provides a canonical isomorphism

\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},\ind_{K_{P}}^{P^{+}}W)\cong\bigoplus_{m\in K_{U}\backslash P^{+}/K_{P}}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},W).

Since the projection map
K_{U}\backslash P^{+}/K_{P}\longrightarrow M^{+}/K_{M}
is
bijective, we deduce that (
4.3.11
) is an isomorphism.

The map in the proposition is now given by (
4.3.9
) composed with

\ind_{M^{+}}^{M}\eqref{eq:ind-H^0-2}
and hence is an isomorphism.
∎

Lemma 4.3.13
.

The following assertions hold:

(a)

K_{P}gK_{U}=\bigsqcup_{u\in K_{U}/K_{U}\cap gK_{U}g^{-1}}K_{M}ugK_{U},\qquad\text{for all $g\in P$.}

(b)

K_{M}\backslash P/K_{U}\cong K_{M}\backslash M\times U/K_{U}.

Proof.

(a)
is easy and
(b)
is trivial.
∎

Finally, we finish the proof of Theorem
4.3.2
.

Proof of Theorem
4.3.2
.
(b)
.

Let
V\in\Rep_{k}(K)
and
\Phi\in\End_{\Rep_{k}(G)}(\ind_{K}^{G}V)
. Recall that we
want to show

\mathcal{S}^{\dim K_{U}}_{V}(\Phi)([1,v])(m)=\sum_{u\in U/K_{U}}\Phi([1,v])(mu)

for all
v\in\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},V)
and
m\in M
. Recall also that the Iwasawa
decomposition
G=PK
allows us to identify
\ind_{K}^{G}V
with
\ind_{K_{P}}^{P}V
.

Note that
K_{P}gK_{U}=\bigsqcup_{u\in K_{U}\cap K_{P}^{g}\backslash K_{U}}K_{P}gu
.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\Phi([1,v])

\displaystyle=\sum_{g\in K_{P}\backslash P}[g^{-1},\Phi([1,v])(g)]

\displaystyle=\sum_{g\in K_{P}\backslash P/K_{U}}\sum_{u\in K_{U}\cap K_{P}^{g}\backslash K_{U}}\bigl[u^{-1}g^{-1},\Phi([1,v])(gu)\bigr]

\displaystyle=\sum_{g\in K_{P}\backslash P/K_{U}}\sum_{u\in K_{U}\cap K_{P}^{g}\backslash K_{U}}\bigl[u^{-1}g^{-1},\Phi([1,v])(g)\bigr],

where the last equality uses
v\in\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},V)
and the
K_{U}
-equivariance of

\Phi
.
Viewing
\mathcal{S}^{\dim K_{U}}_{V}(\Phi)
as an endomorphism on
\ind_{M^{+}}^{M}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},\ind_{K_{P}}^{P}V)
we deduce

\mathcal{S}^{\dim K_{U}}_{V}(\Phi)\bigl(1\otimes[K_{U},v]\bigr)=\sum_{g\in K_{P}\backslash P/K_{U}}1\otimes[K_{U}g^{-1},\Phi([1,v])(g)].

Now, applying the isomorphism in Proposition
4.3.8
, we view

\mathcal{S}^{\dim K_{U}}_{V}(\Phi)
as an endomorphism of
\ind_{K_{M}}^{M}\mathrm{H}^{0}(K_{U},V)

and compute

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\dim K_{U}}_{V}(\Phi)([1,v])

\displaystyle=\sum_{g\in K_{P}\backslash P/K_{U}}\bigl[\pr_{M}(g^{-1}),\mu_{g^{-1}}(\Phi([1,v])(g))\bigr]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g\in K_{P}\backslash P/K_{U},\\
u\in K_{U}/K_{U}\cap gK_{U}g^{-1}\end{subarray}}\bigl[\pr_{M}(g^{-1}),\Phi([1,v])(ug)\bigr]

(def. of
\mu_{g^{-1}}
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{g\in K_{M}\backslash P/K_{U}}\bigl[\pr_{M}(g^{-1}),\Phi([1,v])(g)\bigr]

(Lem.
4.3.13
.
(a)
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\in K_{M}\backslash M}\Bigl[m^{-1},\sum_{u\in U/K_{U}}\Phi([1,v])(mu)\Bigr]

\displaystyle\text{(Lem.\penalty\ \mbox{\ref{lem:Satake}}.\mbox{\ref{lem:Satake-b}})},

where for the second equality we have used
K_{P}\cap gK_{U}g^{-1}=K_{U}\cap gK_{U}g^{-1}
(recall that
U
is normalized by
P
). This implies the claim.
∎

5.
The example of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})

In this section we put
G\coloneqq\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
and let
B
be the Borel
subgroup of upper triangular matrices with unipotent radical
U
and
T

the Levi subgroup of diagonal matrices. Further, we fix the maximal compact open
subgroup
K\coloneqq\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
and denote by
Z
the center of

\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. We fix an algebraically closed coefficient field
k
of
characteristic
p
.

We will compute
\mathrm{L}^{\bullet}(U,V)
for all
irreducible smooth representations
V
of
G
.

5.1.
The weights of
K

The irreducible smooth representations of
K
have been classified by
Barthel–Livné in
[
BL94
, Prop. 4]
. We recall the
classification and set up some notation.

Definition
.

Given
0\leq r\leq p-1
and
0\leq e<p-1
, we consider

(5.1.1)

\Sym^{r}(k^{2})\otimes{\det}^{e}.

Every irreducible smooth representation of
K
is isomorphic to some
representation of the form (
5.1.1
).
We identify
\Sym^{r}(k^{2})
with the subvector space of homogeneous polynomials of
degree
r
in
k[x,y]
. Under this identification,
K
acts through its quotient

\GL_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{p})
via

\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\cdot f(x,y)\coloneqq f(ax+cy,bx+dy),\quad\text{for all
$\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{p})$.}

Thus, a
k
-basis of
\Sym^{r}(k^{2})
is given by
x^{r},x^{r-1}y,\dotsc,y^{r}
, and
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{0}\bigl(K_{U},\Sym^{r}(k^{2})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\spann_{k}\{x^{r}\},

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}^{0}\bigl(K_{U},\Sym^{r}(k^{2})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\Sym^{r}(k^{2})/\spann_{k}\{x^{r},x^{r-1}y,\dotsc,xy^{r-1}\},

where
\spann_{k}\{\ldots\}
denotes the
k
-linear span.

5.2.
Irreducible smooth representations of

\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})

We view
\Sym^{r}(k^{2})
as a smooth
ZK
-representation by letting
p
act
trivially.
Denote by
X
(resp.
Y
) the
k
-linear projection of
\Sym^{r}(k^{2})
onto

\spann_{k}\{x^{r}\}
(resp.
\spann_{k}\{y^{r}\}
) with respect to the basis

x^{r},x^{r-1}y,\dotsc,y^{r}
. We define the endomorphism
\Phi\in\End_{\Rep_{k}(G)}\bigl(\ind_{ZK}^{G}\Sym^{r}(k^{2})\bigr)
by

\Phi([1,v])\coloneqq\Bigl[\begin{pmatrix}p&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}^{-1},Y(v)\Bigr]+\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}\Bigl[\begin{pmatrix}1&i\\
0&p\end{pmatrix}^{-1},X(\begin{pmatrix}1&i\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\cdot v)\Bigr].

Then
[
BL94
, Prop. 8]
shows that

\End_{\Rep_{k}(G)}\bigl(\ind_{ZK}^{G}\Sym^{r}(k^{2})\bigr)\cong k[\Phi]
as

k
-algebras.

Definition
.

For
0\leq r\leq p-1
and
\lambda\in k
we define the smooth
G
-representation

V(r,\lambda)
by the short exact sequence

(5.2.1)

0\longrightarrow\ind_{ZK}^{G}\Sym^{r}(k^{2})\xrightarrow{\Phi-\lambda}\ind_{ZK}^{G}\Sym^{r}(k^{2})\longrightarrow V(r,\lambda)\longrightarrow 0.

Note:
\Phi-\lambda
is injective by
[
BL94
, Thm. 19]
. Given a
smooth character
\chi\colon\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\longrightarrow k^{\times}
, we put

V(r,\lambda,\chi)\coloneqq(\chi\circ\det)\otimes V(r,\lambda).

Notation 5.2.2
.

For each
\lambda\in k
we consider the characters

(5.2.3)

\displaystyle\mu_{\lambda}\colon\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow k^{\times},

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006ptx

\displaystyle\longmapsto\lambda^{\val_{p}(x)}\quad\text{and}

(5.2.4)

\displaystyle\omega\colon\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\cong p^{\mathbb{Z}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}\subseteq k^{\times},

\displaystyle(p^{n},x)

\displaystyle\longmapsto x\bmod p.

If
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\colon\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\longrightarrow k^{\times}
are smooth
characters, we write

(5.2.5)

\displaystyle\chi_{1}\boxtimes\chi_{2}\colon T

\displaystyle\longrightarrow k^{\times},

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&d\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\chi_{1}(a)\chi_{2}(d).

The representations

V(r,\lambda,\chi)
are now described as follows:

Theorem 5.2.6

(
[
BL94
, Thm. 30]
,

[
Bre03
, Thm. 1.1]
)
.

Let
(r,\lambda)\in\{0,1,\dotsc,p-1\}\times k
and let
\chi\colon\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\longrightarrow k^{\times}
be a smooth character.

(a)

If
(r,\lambda)\notin\{(0,\pm 1),(p-1,\pm 1)\}
, then
V(r,\lambda,\chi)
is irreducible.

(b)

There are non-split short exact sequences

(5.2.7)

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow(\chi\mu_{\pm 1}\circ\det)\otimes\Sp\longrightarrow V(0,\pm 1,\chi)\longrightarrow\chi\mu_{\pm 1}\circ\det\longrightarrow 0

and

(5.2.8)

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow\chi\mu_{\pm 1}\circ\det\longrightarrow V(p-1,\pm 1,\chi)\longrightarrow(\chi\mu_{\pm 1}\circ\det)\otimes\Sp\longrightarrow 0,

where
\Sp=i_{T}^{G}(\mathbf{1}_{T})/\mathbf{1}_{G}
is the Steinberg representation, and where

\mathbf{1}_{T}
and
\mathbf{1}_{G}
are the trivial representations of
T
and
G
,
respectively.

(c)

If
(r,\lambda)\neq(0,\pm 1)
and
\lambda\neq 0
, there is a
G
-equivariant isomorphism

V(r,\lambda,\chi)\xrightarrow{\cong}i_{T}^{G}(\chi\mu_{\lambda^{-1}}\boxtimes\chi\mu_{\lambda}\omega^{r}).

(d)

V(r,\lambda,\chi)
is supersingular if and only
if
\lambda=0
.

By
[
BL94
, Thm. 33]
, every smooth irreducible representation
of
G
admitting a central character is a quotient of some
V(r,\lambda,\chi)
.

5.3.
Computation of the left adjoint on irreducibles

Recall the endomorphism
\Phi
on the representation
\ind_{ZK}^{G}\Sym^{r}(k^{2})

from the previous section.
The character
\delta_{B}\colon B\longrightarrow k^{\times}
is (by Remark
3.4.22
)
explicitly given by

\delta_{B}=\Inf^{T}_{B}\bigl(\omega\boxtimes\omega^{-1}\bigr).

Lemma 5.3.1
.

Let
0\leq r\leq p-1
and recall the map
\eta\colon\Sym^{r}(k^{2})\longrightarrow\hskip-11.99998pt\rightarrow\hskip 1.99997pt\mathrm{L}^{0}(K_{U},\Sym^{r}(k^{2}))\cong\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\omega^{r}
.

(a)

The endomorphism
\mathcal{S}_{\Sym^{r}(k^{2})}^{0}(\Phi)

on
\ind_{ZK_{T}}^{T}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\omega^{r})
is given by

\mathcal{S}_{\Sym^{r}(k^{2})}^{0}(\Phi)\bigl([1,\eta(y^{r})]\bigr)=\Bigl[\begin{pmatrix}p&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}^{-1},\eta(y^{r})\Bigr].

(b)

The endomorphism

\mathcal{S}_{\Sym^{r}(k^{2})}^{1}(\Phi)
on
\ind_{ZK_{T}}^{T}\delta_{B}\otimes\spann_{k}\{x^{r}\}\cong\ind_{ZK_{T}}^{T}(\omega^{r+1}\boxtimes\omega^{-1})
is given by

\mathcal{S}_{\Sym^{r}(k^{2})}^{1}(\Phi)\bigl([1,x^{r}]\bigr)=\Bigl[\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&p\end{pmatrix}^{-1},x^{r}\Bigr].

Proof.

By Theorem
4.3.2
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{0}_{\Sym^{r}(k^{2})}(\Phi)\bigl([1,\eta(y^{r})]\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\in ZK_{T}\backslash T}\Bigl[t^{-1},\eta\Bigl(\sum_{u\in K_{U}\backslash U}\Phi([1,y^{r}])(ut)\Bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\Bigl[\begin{pmatrix}p&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}^{-1},\eta(Y(y^{r}))\Bigr]+\Bigl[\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&p\end{pmatrix}^{-1},\eta\Bigl(\;\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}X(\begin{pmatrix}1&i\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\cdot y^{r})\Bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\Bigl[\begin{pmatrix}p&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}^{-1},\eta(y^{r})\Bigr],

where we have used that the second summand in the second line is zero: If
r>0
,
this follows from
\Image(X)\subseteq\Ker(\eta)
; if
r=0
, we use

\Char(k)=p
. This proves
(a)
. For
(b)

we compute in a similar fashion:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{1}_{\Sym^{r}(k^{2})}(\Phi)\bigl([1,x^{r}]\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\in ZK_{T}\backslash T}\Bigl[t^{-1},\sum_{u\in U/K_{U}}\Phi([1,x^{r}])(tu)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\Bigl[\begin{pmatrix}p&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}^{-1},\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}\begin{pmatrix}1&i\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\cdot Y(x^{r})\Bigr]+\Bigl[\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&p\end{pmatrix}^{-1},X(x^{r})\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\Bigl[\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&p\end{pmatrix}^{-1},x^{r}\Bigr].\qed

Proposition 5.3.2
.

Let
(r,\lambda)\in\{0,1,\dotsc,p-1\}\times k
and let
\chi\colon\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\longrightarrow k^{\times}
be a smooth character. One has

\mathrm{L}^{-i}\bigl(U,V(r,\lambda,\chi)\bigr)=\begin{cases}\chi\mu_{\lambda^{-1}}\boxtimes\chi\mu_{\lambda}\omega^{r},&\text{if $i=0$ and
$\lambda\neq 0$,}\\
\chi\mu_{\lambda}\omega^{r+1}\boxtimes\chi\mu_{\lambda^{-1}}\omega^{-1},&\text{if $i=1$ and $\lambda\neq 0$,}\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Proof.

By Proposition
3.2.12
we have
\mathrm{L}\bigl(U,V(r,\lambda,\chi)\bigr)\cong(\chi\circ\det)\otimes\mathrm{L}\bigl(U,V(r,\lambda)\bigr)
. We may therefore assume from the start that
\chi=\mathbf{1}
is the trivial character. As
\dim U=1
, we know from
Proposition
4.1.5
that
\mathrm{L}^{-i}\bigl(U,V(r,\lambda)\bigr)=0
provided
i\notin\{0,1\}
.
Consider the short exact sequence

(5.3.3)

0\longrightarrow\ind_{ZK}^{G}\Sym^{r}(k^{2})\xrightarrow{\Phi-\lambda}\ind_{ZK}^{G}\Sym^{r}(k^{2})\longrightarrow V(r,\lambda)\longrightarrow 0.

It is immediate from Lemma
5.3.1
.
(a)
that

\mathcal{S}^{0}_{\Sym^{r}(k^{2})}(\Phi)-\lambda
is injective. Hence, the long exact
sequence associated with
\mathrm{L}(U,-)
and (
5.3.3
) splits into the
following two short exact sequences:

(5.3.4)

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow\ind_{ZK_{T}}^{T}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\omega^{r})\xrightarrow{\begin{pmatrix}p&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}^{-1}-\lambda}\ind_{ZK_{T}}^{T}(\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\omega^{r})\longrightarrow\mathrm{L}^{0}\bigl(U,V(r,\lambda)\bigr)\longrightarrow 0,

and

(5.3.5)

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow\ind_{ZK_{T}}^{T}(\omega^{r+1}\boxtimes\omega^{-1})\xrightarrow{\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&p\end{pmatrix}^{-1}-\lambda}\ind_{ZK_{T}}^{T}(\omega^{r+1}\boxtimes\omega^{-1})\longrightarrow\mathrm{L}^{-1}\bigl(U,V(r,\lambda)\bigr)\longrightarrow 0.

The assertion now follows easily.
∎

Corollary 5.3.6
.

Let
\chi,\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\colon\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\longrightarrow k^{\times}
be smooth characters with

\chi_{1}\neq\chi_{2}
, and let
0\leq r\leq p-1
. The
T
-representations

\mathrm{L}^{-i}(U,V)
, for irreducible smooth
G
-representations
V
and
i\in\{0,1\}
, are as presented in Table
1
.

Type

V

\mathrm{L}^{-1}(U,V)

\mathrm{L}^{0}(U,V)

character

\chi\circ\det

0

\chi\boxtimes\chi

special series

(\chi\circ\det)\otimes\Sp

\chi\omega\boxtimes\chi\omega^{-1}

0

principal series

i_{T}^{G}(\chi_{1}\boxtimes\chi_{2})

\chi_{2}\omega\boxtimes\chi_{1}\omega^{-1}

\chi_{1}\boxtimes\chi_{2}

supersingular

V(r,0,\chi)

0

0

Table 1
.
Computing
\mathrm{L}^{-i}(U,V)
for irreducible
V
.

Proof.

If
V\cong\chi\circ\det
, the assertion follows from
Corollary
3.4.24
. If
V
is supersingular, then
\mathrm{L}(U,V)=\{0\}

by Proposition
5.3.2
. Let now
V\cong i_{T}^{G}(\chi_{1}\boxtimes\chi_{2})
for two (not necessarily distinct) smooth
characters
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\colon\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\longrightarrow k^{\times}
. Write
\chi_{j}=\mu_{\lambda_{j}}\omega^{r_{j}}
for some
\lambda_{j}\in k^{\times}
and
0\leq r_{j}\leq p-1
and take
\lambda\in k^{\times}
such that
\lambda^{2}=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}
.
We have
i_{T}^{G}(\chi_{1}\boxtimes\chi_{2})\cong V(r_{2}-r_{1},\lambda_{2}\lambda^{-1},\mu_{\lambda}\omega^{r_{1}})
by
Theorem
5.2.6
.
(c)
. Applying
Proposition
5.3.2
we compute

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}^{0}(U,V)

\displaystyle\cong\mu_{\lambda}\omega^{r_{1}}\mu_{\lambda\lambda_{2}^{-1}}\boxtimes\mu_{\lambda}\omega^{r_{1}}\mu_{\lambda_{2}\lambda^{-1}}\omega^{r_{2}-r_{1}}

\displaystyle\cong\chi_{1}\boxtimes\chi_{2}

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}^{-1}(U,V)

\displaystyle\cong\mu_{\lambda}\omega^{r_{1}}\mu_{\lambda_{2}\lambda^{-1}}\omega^{r_{2}-r_{1}+1}\boxtimes\mu_{\lambda}\omega^{r_{1}}\mu_{\lambda\lambda_{2}^{-1}}\omega^{-1}

\displaystyle\cong\chi_{2}\omega\boxtimes\chi_{1}\omega^{-1}.

This settles the principal series case. Finally, we deduce the special series
case as follows: By Proposition
3.2.12
we may assume

\chi=\mathbf{1}
. By Theorem
5.2.6
.
(b)
there
is a short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\Sp\longrightarrow V(0,1)\longrightarrow\mathbf{1}\longrightarrow 0.

The long exact sequence associated with
\mathrm{L}(U,-)
yields

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}^{-1}(U,\Sp)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}^{-1}\bigl(U,V(0,1)\bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}^{-1}(U,\mathbf{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}^{0}(U,\Sp)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}^{0}\bigl(V(0,1)\bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}^{0}(U,\mathbf{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

Since
\mathrm{L}^{-1}(U,\mathbf{1})=\{0\}
, we deduce
\mathrm{L}^{-1}(U,\Sp)\cong\mathrm{L}^{-1}\bigl(U,V(0,1)\bigr)\cong\omega\boxtimes\omega^{-1}
.
As
\mathrm{L}^{0}\bigl(V(0,1)\bigr)\cong\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\mathbf{1}\cong\mathrm{L}^{0}(U,\mathbf{1})
, it
follows that
\varphi
is an isomorphism, whence
\mathrm{L}^{0}(U,\Sp)=\{0\}
.
∎
